Matías looked down at the passage.

"Well, he says the old things have passed away."

"Yes."

"And the new things have come."

"Yes."

Matías looked up.

"Elías, are you telling me that a new creation might not live differently?"

"I'm saying we should establish what Paul means before deciding everything the verse proves."

Matías closed his eyes briefly.

Adrián smiled.

"He's doing that thing again."

"What thing?" Elías asked.

"Making a simple question take an hour."

Marcos laughed.

Even Matías smiled a little.

Elías shrugged.

"I've been accused of worse."

Matías reopened his eyes.

"Alright. Then tell me what you think Paul means."

Elías glanced down at the passage.

"I think he's describing something God has accomplished in Christ. Something that changes our standing and relationship to Him."

"And our behavior?"

"That may follow from it. We need to examine the passages that address behavior rather than assume this one answers every question."

Matías leaned back.

"That sounds like a very convenient distinction."

"Why?"

"Because if you separate what God has done from how a person lives, you can explain away practically anything."

Elías didn't answer immediately.

Marcos looked at Matías.

"Explain what concerns you."

Matías folded his hands.

"I've spent years working with people who wanted to change their lives."

He looked down at the table.

"Some were caught in habits that were destroying them. Some had damaged relationships. Some had made a terrible mess of things."

Adrián nodded.

"We've all seen that."

"And when they came to Christ, we told them they didn't have to live that way anymore."

"Of course," Adrián said.

"We encouraged them. We prayed with them. We helped them establish new habits. We taught them Scripture."

Matías looked at Elías.

"And I've seen people change."

"I believe you," Elías replied.

"I've watched men become faithful husbands. I've watched people leave destructive habits behind. I've seen families restored."

His voice softened.

"Those things matter to me."

Adrián nodded.

"They matter to me too."

"So when you suggest that a person might be saved without any visible evidence of change, I struggle with that."

"I understand."

"Because I've seen what happens when people convince themselves that what they do doesn't matter."

Elías listened.

Matías continued.

"I don't want to tell someone who's destroying his family that his behavior is irrelevant."

"Neither do I," Elías replied.

"Then what are you saying?"

Elías rested his hand on the Bible.

"I'm questioning whether someone's behavior is the basis on which we determine that God has given him eternal life."

Matías shook his head.

"Again, I'm not saying behavior earns eternal life."

"I know."

"I'm saying that if eternal life is real, there should be evidence."

Elías looked at him.

"And I'm asking where Scripture makes that evidence the test."

Matías stared at him.

"James."

Elías raised his eyebrows.

"James?"

"Chapter two."

Adrián glanced at Marcos.

"Here we go."

Matías turned the pages.

"Faith without works is dead."

He looked at Elías.

"How do you explain that?"

Elías didn't answer.

Instead, he reached for his coffee.

It had gone cold.

He took a sip anyway.

Adrián watched him.

"Well?"

Elías set the cup down.

"I'd want to read the whole passage."

Matías nodded firmly.

"Good. So would I."

He pushed his Bible toward the center of the table.

"Let's read it."

Marcos glanced at his watch.

"Fellows, I have to leave in about twenty minutes."

Adrián looked at the open Bible.

"Twenty minutes for James chapter two?"

"That's what I've got."

Adrián turned to Elías.

"Can you do it?"

Elías smiled.

"Not responsibly."

Matías laughed.

"Then perhaps we should leave it until next time."

Marcos nodded.

"I'd rather do that than rush through it."

Matías closed his Bible.

"Fair enough."

For a while they talked about other things.

Adrián told them about a concert he hoped to attend.

Marcos mentioned a project at work.

Elías asked Matías about a mutual friend who had been ill.

The café gradually emptied.

Yet something about the earlier conversation remained unsettled.

As they gathered their things, Matías turned toward Elías.

"Can I ask you one more question?"

"Certainly."

"Do you believe that someone can be saved and still live a terribly sinful life?"

Elías looked at him.

"I believe Paul addressed some very troubled believers."

"Corinth?"

"Among others."

Matías nodded slowly.

"And do you think their behavior mattered?"

"Very much."

"Then I suppose that's where we need to continue."

Elías picked up his bag.

"Perhaps it is."

Matías stood.

He wasn't angry.

He wasn't offended.

He simply wasn't satisfied.

He had spent years teaching people that genuine conversion meant leaving their old lives behind.

He had seen the good that came from taking that seriously.

And he wasn't prepared to abandon that conviction because of a few questions about terminology.

At the door, Adrián caught up with him.

"Hey."

Matías turned.

"What?"

"For what it's worth, I understand what you're saying."

Matías smiled faintly.

"Thank you."

"I've seen people change too. Remarkably."

"I know."

"And I don't think anybody here wants to minimize that."

Matías nodded.

"Neither do I."

Adrián opened the door.

The cold evening air rushed inside.

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

Matías stepped outside, then stopped.

"Adrián?"

"Yes?"

"Do you ever wonder whether we've made the Christian life more complicated than it needs to be?"

Adrián smiled.

"Every time Elías opens his Bible."

Matías laughed.

Then his expression became thoughtful again.

"I'm serious."

"I know."

Matías looked down at the Bible in his hand.

"I just want to understand what it means to be saved."

Adrián didn't have an answer.

And for once, he didn't try to invent one.

# Chapter 14

## Faith Without Works

Matías arrived first.

That wasn't unusual.

What was unusual was the number of notes spread across the table.

His Bible lay open beside a notebook. Several passages had been underlined, and a small collection of handwritten references rested beside his coffee.

When Adrián walked in, he stopped.

"Oh, dear."

Matías looked up.

"What?"

"Is there an examination today?"

Matías smiled.

"Good morning."

Adrián pulled out a chair.

"Should I have studied?"

"Only if you want to participate."

"Then I'm in trouble."

Marcos arrived a few minutes later.

He glanced at the table.

"Someone's been busy."

Matías nodded.

"I've been thinking about our last conversation."

"So have I," Marcos replied.

"Good."

Adrián looked at the notes again.

"Apparently some of us have been thinking harder than others."

A few minutes later, Elías appeared at the door, carrying a leather satchel.

"Good morning, gentlemen."

He took his seat, opened the satchel, and removed a thick study Bible.

Then a commentary.

Then a smaller notebook.

Adrián watched the growing pile.

"Oh, this is getting serious."

Elías looked up.

"What?"

"Matías brought the evidence. You've brought the entire legal department."

Marcos laughed.

Elías placed the commentary beside his Bible.

"I thought it might be helpful."

Adrián examined the books.

"Are we going to need a bigger table?"

"Possibly," Elías replied.

Matías smiled.

"Actually, I'm glad you brought those."

Elías looked at the notes spread across the table.

"And I'm glad you've been studying."

Adrián leaned toward Marcos.

"Wonderful. Both sides are armed."

Marcos shook his head, smiling.

"Could we at least order coffee before the proceedings begin?"

Matías closed his notebook.

"Fair enough."

The waitress approached, glanced at the books covering the table, and raised an eyebrow.

"Studying for something?"

Adrián nodded solemnly.

"Our final examination."

She smiled.

"When is it?"

"Apparently today."

Elías laughed.

After she took their orders, Matías opened his Bible again.

"Last time, we were discussing whether genuine salvation should produce evidence in a person's life."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

"And you asked me where Scripture makes that connection."

"I did."

"Well, I've been reading James."

Adrián glanced at Elías's commentary.

"And apparently so has half the theological world."

Elías smiled.

"That's why I brought it."

Matías turned to James chapter two.

"Verse fourteen."

He read the question aloud.

"What use is it, my brethren, if someone says he has faith but he has no works? Can that faith save him?"

He looked around the table.

"Now, before anyone explains what James doesn't mean, can we agree on what he actually says?"

Elías nodded.

"That's a fair request."

"James describes someone who claims to have faith and has no works. Then he asks whether that faith can save him."

"Yes."

Matías looked around the table.

"That sounds pretty straightforward to me."

Marcos studied the verse.

"It certainly sounds like James is questioning the value of faith without works."

"Exactly," Matías said.

Elías reached for his commentary.

"Dr. Whitmore argues that James is addressing the usefulness of faith rather than whether someone possesses eternal life."

Matías considered that.

"Why does he say that?"

"Because of the illustration James uses. A brother or sister needs food and clothing. Someone offers kind words without supplying either. Whitmore argues that James is concerned with faith that accomplishes nothing."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"That's interesting."

"It's an important distinction," Elías replied.

Adrián leaned over the table.

"Can we verify it?"

Elías looked at him.

"Verify what?"

"That James is talking about usefulness rather than whether the person is saved."

"Whitmore examines that question extensively."

"I understand. I'm asking whether we can see it in the passage ourselves."

Elías's expression tightened slightly.

"Adrián, Whitmore spent decades studying these texts. He isn't making careless assertions."

"I didn't say he was."

"His work is widely respected."

"I'm sure it is."

Adrián turned his Bible toward the center of the table.

"I'd still like to see it."

Matías nodded.

"So would I."

For a moment Elías seemed ready to say something more.

Then he sat back.

"Alright."

Marcos began reading the passage.

"James asks what good it is if someone says he has faith without works."

"Right," Adrián said.

"Then he gives the example of someone who needs food and clothing."

Matías nodded.

"And the person doesn't help."

"Exactly," Marcos replied. "So the words accomplish nothing for the person in need."

Adrián looked at the verse again.

"That certainly explains why James asks what good it is."

"Yes," Matías said. "Although we still have to explain why he asks whether that faith can save him."

Marcos nodded.

"That's true."

Elías reached toward his commentary.

"Whitmore addresses that in the next section."

Adrián smiled.

"Good. Let's see whether his explanation holds up too."

Elías paused with his hand on the book.

Then he opened it.

"Very well."

He adjusted his glasses and found the paragraph he wanted.

"Whitmore begins by pointing out that James is writing to believers."

Matías nodded.

"He calls them brethren."

"Several times," Elías replied. "And in chapter two, verse one, he addresses them as people holding faith in our Lord Jesus Christ."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"So he's writing to Christians."

"That's how Whitmore understands his audience."

Marcos turned back to the opening of the chapter.

"Well, we can see that much for ourselves."

"Yes," Elías said.

Matías looked at verse fourteen.

"Although calling his readers brethren doesn't necessarily mean every person he describes is saved."

Elías glanced up.

"No. It doesn't."

Matías smiled.

"Good. I wanted to make sure we weren't assuming too much."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"He's getting dangerous."

"He's learning," Marcos replied.

Matías continued.

"Now, what about the question? Can that faith save him?"

Elías consulted the commentary.

"Whitmore argues that the word translated *save* doesn't always refer to receiving eternal life."

"Is that true?" Adrián asked.

"Certainly. Scripture uses the word for different kinds of deliverance."

"Can we see an example?"

Elías began turning pages.

Marcos spoke before he found one.

"Didn't Peter cry out for Jesus to save him when he was sinking?"

Elías stopped.

"Matthew fourteen. Yes."

Adrián smiled.

"Well, there you go. Marcos found one without a commentary."

Marcos shrugged.

"I remember the story."

Matías looked thoughtful.

"Alright. So the word can mean rescue from physical danger."

"Yes," Elías replied.

"That doesn't establish what it means in James."

Elías nodded.

"No, it doesn't."

He returned to the commentary.

"Whitmore points to James five, where turning someone back from wandering is associated with saving a soul from death."

Matías found the passage.

"James five, verses nineteen and twenty."

He read silently.

"That could refer to physical death," Marcos said.

"It could," Elías replied.

"Or it could be understood differently," Matías added.

"Yes."

Adrián looked up.

"So we haven't settled it."

"Not yet," Elías said.

Matías tapped the page.

"Then I think we need to be careful. We can't simply choose the meaning that agrees with our theology."

Elías's hand rested on the commentary.

"Whitmore isn't doing that. He considers the context carefully."

"I didn't say he wasn't."

There was a slight edge in Elías's voice.

"He's a very responsible interpreter."

Matías looked at him.

"I believe you. I'm asking whether his conclusion is supported by the passage."

Elías glanced down at the book.

"Of course."

Marcos turned his Bible toward the others.

"Then perhaps we should look at the example James gives."

Adrián nodded.

"That seems like a good idea."

Marcos read the illustration of the brother or sister without adequate clothing or food.

"James asks what good it does to wish them well without giving them what they need."

"Right," Matías said.

"And the answer is that it does them no good."

"Agreed."

Marcos continued.

"Then James says faith without works is dead."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"So the person who needs help is still hungry. The words accomplished nothing."

"Exactly," Marcos said.

Matías nodded.

"That's clear enough."

Elías looked pleased.

"That's Whitmore's point. The illustration concerns usefulness."

Matías considered that.

"Yes, the illustration certainly concerns usefulness."

He looked at Elías.

"Although James goes beyond saying the words are useless. He says the faith is dead."

Elías nodded.

"That's true."

"So we still have to explain that."

Adrián smiled.

"One question answered. Three more waiting."

"At least three," Marcos said.

Matías turned to verse eighteen.

"Let's keep reading."

They worked through James's challenge concerning faith and works.

When they reached verse nineteen, Matías stopped.

"You believe that God is one. You do well. The demons also believe—and shudder."

Adrián looked up.

"Now there's something I've never understood."

"What's that?" Elías asked.

"Why does James bring demons into a discussion about faith?"

Matías nodded.

"That's a good question."

Elías opened Whitmore's commentary.

"Whitmore makes an interesting observation here. He says we tend to focus on the fact that the demons believe and overlook the fact that they shudder."

Marcos looked at the verse.

"Why would that matter?"

"The Greek word is *phrissousin*. It describes trembling or shuddering with fear."

Adrián frowned.

"So they don't merely acknowledge that God is one. They react to what they know."

"Exactly," Elías replied.

Matías looked thoughtful.

"Then James is showing that their belief produces a response."

"That's Whitmore's observation."

Marcos read the verse again.

"And their response is fear."

"Yes."

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, that makes sense. They know who God is."

Matías looked at Elías.

"Although why would James use demons as an example? They aren't candidates for redemption."

Elías nodded.

"That's another important consideration. James isn't explaining how demons can receive eternal life. He's using what they believe, and how they respond to it, to make a point."

"What point?" Marcos asked.

Elías glanced at the commentary.

"Whitmore suggests that even demons respond to what they know about God. James is challenging a person who claims to possess faith while doing nothing with it."

Matías returned to the passage.

"So James may be saying that merely claiming to believe something, while refusing to act upon it, is useless."

"That's the argument," Elías replied.

Adrián looked at Matías.

"And the demons actually respond."

"They do," Matías said.

He paused.

"Although their response certainly isn't obedience."

"No," Marcos replied. "It's fear."

Elías nodded.

"Which means we shouldn't confuse a response with spiritual maturity, either."

Matías studied the verse.

"Alright. I can see why Whitmore thinks the shuddering matters."

Adrián looked at Elías.

"Can we establish that from the passage, or are we just accepting Whitmore's explanation?"

Elías's expression tightened slightly.

"Whitmore isn't the only scholar who has observed it."

"I didn't say he was wrong."

Adrián pointed at the verse.

"I'm asking whether James himself is drawing attention to the demons' reaction."

Marcos answered before Elías could speak.

"Well, he explicitly mentions it. He could have stopped after saying they believe."

Matías nodded.

"That's true."

Elías glanced at the text.

"Yes. That's a fair observation."

Adrián smiled.

"Good. Then we've established that the reaction matters."

Matías held up a finger.

"We've established that James deliberately mentions it. We still need to determine exactly what he's using it to prove."

Elías looked at him.

"Agreed."

Adrián laughed.

"Wonderful. Now both of you are doing it."

Matías turned the page.

"Now let's discuss Abraham."

Elías straightened slightly.

"Good."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"He's been waiting for Abraham."

Marcos smiled.

Matías read James's statement that Abraham was justified by works when he offered Isaac.

Then he read verse twenty-four.

"A person is justified by works and not by faith alone."

He looked up.

"That's a strong statement."

"It is," Elías replied.

"And Paul says Abraham was justified by faith apart from works."

"Romans four," Elías said.

Marcos opened to the passage.

"Let's compare them."

For several minutes they read through the accounts.

Then Marcos spoke.

"Paul quotes Genesis fifteen, where Abraham believed God and was counted righteous."

"Correct," Elías said.

"And James points to Genesis twenty-two, where Abraham offered Isaac."

"Yes."

Adrián looked between the passages.

"Those events happened years apart."

"They did," Elías replied.

Matías nodded.

"So Abraham was counted righteous before he offered Isaac."

"Exactly."

"Which means offering Isaac wasn't how he originally received righteousness."

Elías smiled.

"That's an important observation."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"And you found that yourself."

Matías laughed.

"I can read, Adrián."

"I know. I'm just impressed."

Marcos returned to James.

"Then what does James mean when he says Abraham was justified by works?"

Elías opened the commentary.

"Whitmore argues that James is using *justify* in the sense of vindication or demonstration."

Matías considered that.

"Can the word mean that?"

"Yes. The word has a range of uses, depending on context."

Adrián raised a hand.

"Before we go any further, can we find an example?"

Elías looked at him.

"An example of what?"

"Someone being justified in a sense other than receiving righteousness before God."

Matías began turning pages.

"Didn't Jesus say wisdom is justified by her children?"

Elías looked surprised.

"Yes. Matthew eleven, verse nineteen."

Marcos found it.

"That certainly isn't describing wisdom receiving eternal life."

Adrián grinned.

"Unless wisdom has been having a very difficult week."

They laughed.

Elías smiled.

"The point is that wisdom is shown to be right by what results from it."

Matías nodded slowly.

"So the word can refer to vindication."

"Yes."

"And Abraham was already counted righteous before Genesis twenty-two."

"Yes."

Matías looked at James again.

"Then I can see why someone would understand James as talking about Abraham's faith being demonstrated through obedience."

Elías nodded.

"That's Whitmore's argument."

Marcos spoke.

"And James says Abraham's faith was working with his works, and by works his faith was perfected."

Matías read the verse again.

"Completed or brought to maturity."

Elías nodded.

"That's how many commentators understand the expression."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"Does that help?"

"It helps me understand the argument."

"Do you agree with it?"

Matías thought for a moment.

"I agree that Abraham was counted righteous before he offered Isaac. I agree that the offering demonstrated something about his faith."

He looked toward Elías.

"I'm still not convinced James is saying works have no bearing on whether faith is genuine."

Elías considered that.

"Nor does the distinction between Paul's and James's uses of justification, by itself, establish that."

Matías smiled.

"Thank you."

Elías glanced down at his commentary.

"Whitmore's argument is more extensive than that."

"I'm sure it is," Matías replied.

Adrián looked at the clock.

"Could we perhaps examine the rest of it before the café closes permanently?"

Marcos laughed.

Matías turned to the final example.

"Rahab."

They read James's account of her receiving the messengers and sending them away safely.

"Her actions mattered," Matías said.

"Very much," Marcos replied.

"And James calls that justification by works."

Elías nodded.

Matías looked at him.

"Would you agree that James is calling believers to act on what they believe?"

"Certainly."

"And that a faith without works is of no practical benefit to someone in need?"

"Yes."

"And that James presents Abraham and Rahab as examples of faith expressed through action?"

"Yes."

Matías sat back.

"Then perhaps we're closer together than I thought."

Adrián smiled.

"Careful. We might accidentally agree on something."

Marcos looked at the final verse.

"There's still the comparison of the body and the spirit."

Matías nodded.

"Exactly."

He read James's closing statement that as the body without the spirit is dead, so faith without works is dead.

"That's the part I don't want us to skip."

Elías looked at the passage.

"Nor should we."

Matías continued.

"A body without the spirit is dead. It doesn't merely become less useful. It's dead."

"Yes," Elías replied.

"So why wouldn't James mean that faith without works is not genuine saving faith?"

Elías consulted his commentary.

"Whitmore argues that James is using the comparison to emphasize separation. Just as the body is inactive without the spirit, faith is inactive without works."

Matías considered that.

"That explains the inactivity."

"Yes."

"Does it establish that the faith still possesses eternal life?"

Elías paused.

"Not by itself."

Adrián looked up.

"Then we're still left with a question."

"Yes," Elías said.

Matías nodded.

"And I don't think we should pretend otherwise."

For a while nobody spoke.

Then Marcos turned back to Romans four.

"There's something we have established, though."

"What's that?" Adrián asked.

"Paul says Abraham was counted righteous when he believed God. James points to something Abraham did years later."

Matías nodded.

"That's clear."

"So whatever James means by justification through works, we can't simply make Abraham's offering of Isaac the event that first secured his righteousness before God."

"Agreed," Matías said.

Adrián looked at Elías.

"And we've established that James is concerned with faith that does something for other people."

"Yes."

"And that the word *save* needs to be understood in context."

"Yes."

"And that the body comparison still needs careful explanation."

Elías smiled.

"You're keeping track remarkably well."

"Someone has to. You've got too many books."

Matías laughed.

He closed his Bible.

"I have to admit, I came here expecting James to settle our disagreement."

"And?" Marcos asked.

"I understand the passage better than I did. I'm not ready to say it settles everything."

Elías nodded.

"That's reasonable."

Matías looked at him.

"I still believe that genuine conversion should change a person's life."

"I know."

"And I still believe obedience matters."

"So do I."

Adrián looked at both men.

"Then perhaps the question isn't whether obedience matters."

Marcos nodded.

"Perhaps it's what obedience tells us about someone's salvation."

Matías considered that.

"Yes."

Elías began putting his books away.

Adrián watched him slide the commentary into his satchel.

"Well, Dr. Whitmore survived the examination."

Elías looked up.

"I should hope so."

"Although we didn't agree with everything."

"We didn't establish everything," Elías corrected.

Adrián smiled.

"Even better."

Matías gathered his notes.

"There's something else I want to return to."

"What's that?" Marcos asked.

"Corinth."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"My favorite subject."

Matías smiled.

"Paul called them fleshly."

"Yes."

"And yet he addressed them as brothers in Christ."

Elías nodded.

"He did."

Matías looked down at his Bible.

"Then I think we need to understand something."

Adrián waited.

"If these people belonged to Christ, and Paul still called them fleshly, what exactly was he identifying?"

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Their behavior?"

"Certainly their behavior was involved," Matías replied.

He paused.

"Although I wonder whether we've been so busy discussing the evidence of carnality that we haven't fully understood what makes someone carnal."

Adrián looked at him.

"Now that's a question worth asking."

Elías fastened his satchel.

"Yes," he said. "It is."

Adrián stood.

"Good. Another question."

Marcos smiled.

"You're getting used to them."

"Not really. I was hoping we'd eventually answer one."

Matías laughed.

"Perhaps next time."

Adrián picked up his jacket.

"That's what you said last time."

They made their way toward the door.

The bell rang as Adrián held it open.

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

"Same time next week?" Marcos asked.

Matías nodded.

"Works for me."

Adrián glanced at Elías's satchel.

"Bring the books."

Elías smiled.

"I always do."

"And perhaps," Adrián added, "leave room for a few more questions."

Elías adjusted the strap on his shoulder.

"There's always room for questions."

Matías looked at him.

"Good."

They stepped outside into the cool evening air.



# Chapter 15

## Can the Flesh Be Retrained?

Adrián was studying the menu when Marcos arrived.

"Anything new?" Marcos asked.

"Not a thing."

"Then why are you reading it?"

"I'm hoping they've changed their minds about the prices."

Marcos laughed and sat down.

Matías arrived a few minutes later, carrying his usual Bible and notebook. Elías followed shortly afterward, his leather satchel hanging from one shoulder.

"Good afternoon, gentlemen," Elías said.

Adrián glanced at the bag.

"How many books today?"

"Just my Bible."

"Really?"

"And a commentary."

"Of course."

"And a few notes."

"There he is."

Elías smiled as he took his seat.

The waitress brought their usual orders, and the conversation wandered for a while. Marcos had been dealing with a difficult situation at work. Matías had visited a friend in the hospital. Adrián had apparently discovered another concert worth attending.

Nobody was surprised.

Eventually Matías opened his notebook.

"I've been thinking about what we've learned so far."

Adrián set down his coffee.

"That could take a while."

"Not everything. Just Corinth and our discussion about salvation."

Marcos nodded.

"Go ahead."

"Well, Corinth taught us that a church can possess remarkable spiritual gifts and still be called fleshly."

"Yes," Adrián said.

"And that jealousy and division can exist among people Paul calls brothers in Christ."

"Right."

Matías glanced at Elías.

"And James has given us quite a bit to think about concerning faith and works."

Elías nodded.

"He has."

"I'm still studying that."

"So am I."

Adrián looked surprised.

"You mean we're allowed to move on without solving every question?"

"Apparently," Marcos said.

"Wonderful. There may be hope for us yet."

Matías smiled.

"There's something else we know."

"What's that?" Marcos asked.

"Whatever carnality means, it isn't limited to the things we usually associate with scandalous sin."

Adrián nodded.

"Corinth certainly taught us that."

"And spiritual activity doesn't necessarily establish spiritual maturity," Matías continued.

"Unfortunately," Adrián said.

Matías smiled.

"I'm not suggesting there's anything wrong with enthusiasm."

"I know."

"Or spiritual gifts."

"Agreed."

"I'm simply saying we've learned to be more careful about what we assume."

Adrián lifted his cup.

"To being careful."

Marcos raised his.

"That may be the first time you've proposed that."

"Personal growth, gentlemen."

They laughed.

Elías had been listening quietly.

"So what do you think we should examine next?"

Matías considered the question.

"Well, we've spent considerable time identifying problems. I'd like to understand how Christians are supposed to deal with them."

Marcos nodded.

"That makes sense."

Adrián looked toward him.

"You're going to enjoy this part."

"Why?"

"Because somebody's finally asking for a solution."

Marcos smiled.

"Nothing wrong with solutions."

"Certainly not."

Matías continued.

"I've been reading about changing patterns of thinking."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Thinking?"

"Yes. The way our minds develop habits."

Marcos leaned forward.

"Now that's interesting."

Matías nodded.

"Apparently, the more often we think or behave in a certain way, the easier it becomes to repeat that pattern."

"That makes sense," Marcos said.

"And people can learn new patterns. They can develop healthier responses, practice different behaviors, and gradually replace destructive habits."

Adrián considered that.

"So you're talking about retraining the brain."

"Exactly."

Elías looked interested.

"That's an area where there's been considerable research."

"Yes," Matías replied. "And some Christian teachers have been incorporating those ideas into discipleship."

Marcos nodded.

"I've heard of that."

Adrián looked at him.

"Of course you have."

"What?"

"You know every program ever invented."

"Not every program."

"Give him another year," Matías said.

Marcos laughed.

Matías continued.

"Think about someone who struggles with anger. Perhaps he reacts the same way every time he feels threatened."

Adrián nodded.

"Alright."

"He can learn to recognize what triggers that response. He can pause, reconsider what he's thinking, and practice responding differently."

"That sounds useful," Adrián said.

"It can be."

Marcos spoke.

"And over time, the new response can become more natural."

"Exactly."

Elías nodded.

"Repeated practice can influence habits. That's a reasonable observation."

Matías looked pleased.

"So perhaps that's part of how we deal with the flesh."

Adrián considered that.

"Maybe."

Matías looked at him.

"You don't sound convinced."

"No, I'm listening. I can certainly see how someone might learn to control his temper."

"Good."

"I'm just wondering whether that's the same thing as dealing with the flesh."

Matías frowned slightly.

"What would be the difference?"

Adrián shrugged.

"I don't know. That's why I'm asking."

Marcos spoke.

"Well, consider the practical results. If a man has a terrible temper and learns not to lash out at his family, that's a meaningful improvement."

"Absolutely," Adrián said.

"And if retraining his responses helps him do that, why wouldn't we encourage it?"

"I would."

Matías nodded.

"Then perhaps we're making this more complicated than it needs to be."

Adrián smiled.

"You sound like me."

"Don't insult me."

They laughed.

Elías opened his Bible.

"There's a passage that might be relevant."

Adrián pointed at him.

"Romans twelve?"

Elías looked surprised.

"Yes, actually."

Adrián grinned.

"I'm learning."

Elías found the passage.

"Paul speaks about being transformed by the renewing of the mind."

Matías nodded immediately.

"That's exactly what I was thinking."

Marcos looked at the verse.

"Then Scripture does connect transformation with the mind."

"Certainly," Elías replied.

Matías looked toward Adrián.

"Wouldn't that support what I'm saying?"

"It might," Adrián answered. "Although I wonder whether renewing the mind and retraining the brain mean precisely the same thing."

Elías looked up.

"That's worth examining."

Matías considered the question.

"Well, both involve a change in thinking."

"Yes," Marcos said.

"Then perhaps they're describing the same process."

"Perhaps," Elías replied. "We should be careful not to assume that simply because the language sounds similar."

Adrián smiled.

"That's why we keep you around."

Elías returned the smile.

Matías wasn't finished.

"Let me give you another illustration."

"Go ahead," Marcos said.

"I heard a preacher describe the flesh as a merry-go-round."

Adrián laughed.

"A merry-go-round?"

"Yes."

"Well, this should be good."

Matías ignored him.

"Imagine one of those old playground merry-go-rounds. Someone has been pushing it, and now it's spinning wildly."

Adrián nodded.

"I remember those."

"So do I," Marcos said. "I'm surprised any of us survived them."

"Some of us barely did," Adrián replied.

Matías smiled.

"Now imagine trying to stop it."

"That could be unpleasant," Marcos said.

"Exactly. If you try to stop it all at once, you could hurt yourself. So instead, you apply a little pressure."

"Slow it down," Adrián said.

"Yes. Just a little at a time. You keep applying resistance until eventually it stops."

Marcos nodded.

"That's a good illustration of gradual change."

"That's the idea," Matías replied. "You don't expect someone who's been struggling with a habit for twenty years to overcome it overnight. You help him gain control gradually."

Adrián considered that.

"I can see why people would find that encouraging."

"So can I," Marcos said.

Matías continued.

"And it gives people something practical to do. They aren't simply told to stop sinning. They're shown how to begin changing."

Elías nodded.

"That distinction matters."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"Can I ask something about the merry-go-round?"

Matías smiled.

"I knew you would."

"Who's pushing it?"

Matías looked at him.

"What?"

"Well, you said it's spinning wildly because somebody's been pushing it."

"Yes."

"Who?"

Matías thought for a moment.

"Bad habits. Wrong choices. Patterns of thinking."

"Alright. And who's applying the brakes?"

"The person who wants to change."

Adrián nodded.

"So the same person who's been pushing it is now trying to stop it?"

Marcos laughed.

"That's an interesting way of putting it."

Matías smiled.

"Well, yes. People have to take responsibility for their behavior."

"Of course."

Adrián stirred his coffee.

"I suppose I'm wondering what happens if the person gets tired of applying the brakes."

Matías considered the question.

"Then he needs encouragement. Accountability. Support."

"Which is why you appreciate the church you visited."

"Exactly."

Marcos nodded.

"That's where a good support system can make a difference."

"Yes," Matías said. "People shouldn't have to struggle alone."

Adrián looked at his friend.

"I agree with that."

Matías smiled.

"Good."

"And I think helping someone develop better habits is worthwhile."

"Absolutely."

Adrián paused.

"I still wonder whether we've answered the original question."

Matías looked puzzled.

"Which question?"

"Whether slowing down the merry-go-round is the same thing as dealing with the flesh."

Matías set down his cup.

"Well, what else would it be?"

"I don't know."

Adrián glanced at Elías.

"What do you think?"

Elías considered the illustration.

"I think it describes behavioral change rather well."

Matías nodded.

"Thank you."

"Whether it describes Paul's understanding of the flesh is another question."

Matías looked at him.

"Why?"

"Because we haven't established that the flesh is simply a collection of learned behaviors."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"That's true."

Elías continued.

"Paul certainly addresses behavior. He also uses the word flesh in ways that require careful attention to context."

Matías nodded.

"Then we should examine those contexts."

"Yes."

Adrián smiled.

"Good. We have another investigation."

Marcos turned toward Matías.

"Can I ask something?"

"Of course."

"Suppose the merry-go-round illustration works. Suppose someone successfully changes a destructive habit."

"Alright."

"Has the flesh been dealt with?"

Matías considered the question.

"In that area of his life, I would say he's made significant progress."

"Certainly," Marcos replied. "I'm asking whether that progress tells us what happened to the flesh itself."

Matías looked at him.

"I'm not sure I understand the distinction."

"Neither am I completely," Marcos admitted.

He thought for a moment.

"Let's say someone used to become angry whenever he was criticized. Through practice, he learns to remain calm."

"Good."

"Now he no longer shouts at people."

"Excellent."

"Does that mean he's no longer angry?"

Matías hesitated.

"Not necessarily."

"Perhaps he's learned not to express it."

"Possibly."

Adrián nodded.

"And that would still be an improvement."

"A considerable one," Marcos replied.

Elías looked at the others.

"So we've identified something useful. A change in behavior doesn't necessarily tell us everything about what's happening inwardly."

Matías nodded slowly.

"That's fair."

Marcos continued.

"Then perhaps we should distinguish between controlling a behavior and addressing whatever produces it."

Matías considered that.

"Alright. Although I wouldn't want us to minimize the value of controlling harmful behavior."

"Neither would I," Marcos said.

"People can be seriously hurt while we're discussing what happens inwardly."

Adrián nodded.

"That's true."

Matías looked around the table.

"If a man is violent toward his family, I'm not going to tell him to wait until we've settled our theology of the flesh."

"Nor should you," Elías replied.

"He needs to stop harming them. His family needs protection."

"Agreed," Marcos said.

"And if practical measures help prevent further harm, we should use them."

Adrián nodded.

"Absolutely."

Matías sat back.

"Good. Because sometimes these conversations make me nervous."

"Why?" Adrián asked.

"Because we can become so concerned about whether a method is spiritual that we forget people are suffering."

That brought a brief silence.

Then Marcos spoke.

"That's worth remembering."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"And that's one of the things I admire about you."

Matías smiled.

"Thank you."

"You actually care whether people get better."

"Of course I do."

"I know. That's why I'm listening."

Matías looked at him for a moment.

Then he nodded.

Marcos returned to the illustration.

"There's another reason I find the merry-go-round interesting."

Adrián smiled.

"Besides the possibility of being thrown across the playground?"

"Yes."

"Alright."

"It's measurable."

Matías looked at him.

"What do you mean?"

"Well, you can observe whether it's slowing down."

"Exactly," Matías replied.

"And if you know which actions slow it down, you can repeat them."

"Right."

Marcos continued.

"That's what makes these approaches attractive. You can establish goals, monitor progress, and help people develop consistent habits."

Adrián laughed.

"There it is."

"What?"

"The spreadsheet."

Marcos smiled.

"I haven't mentioned a spreadsheet."

"You didn't have to."

Matías laughed.

Marcos wasn't offended.

"There's nothing wrong with measuring progress."

"No," Adrián replied. "There isn't."

"And there's nothing wrong with having a plan."

"Agreed."

Marcos looked pleased.

"Then perhaps we should be careful not to dismiss useful methods simply because they're methods."

Elías nodded.

"That's reasonable."

Matías looked toward Adrián.

"See? Even you agree."

Adrián smiled.

"I've agreed with quite a lot today."

"You're making progress."

"Perhaps someone should measure it."

Marcos reached toward his bag.

"Don't you dare," Adrián said.

They laughed.

The waitress returned to clear the empty cups.

"Another round?"

Adrián looked at the others.

"Apparently we haven't finished fixing the Christian life."

She smiled.

"That sounds ambitious."

"You have no idea."

Matías requested tea. Marcos ordered another coffee. Elías declined, and Adrián asked for a slice of cake.

Matías looked at him.

"Didn't you already have one?"

"Yes."

"And you're having another?"

"I'm working on self-control."

"How?"

"Gradually."

Marcos burst out laughing.

Adrián pointed at Matías.

"I'm applying the brakes a little at a time."

Even Matías couldn't stop laughing.

When the waitress returned with their orders, the conversation shifted briefly to other things.

Then Elías looked at the two open Bibles.

"There's something I think we should keep in mind."

Matías looked up.

"What's that?"

"We've discussed two approaches today. Retraining patterns of thinking and gradually controlling destructive behavior."

Marcos nodded.

"Both have practical value."

"Yes," Elías said. "And both raise questions about how they relate to Paul's teaching."

Adrián took a bite of cake.

"So we haven't proved they're the answer."

"No."

"And we haven't proved they're wrong."

"Not simply by discussing the illustrations," Elías replied.

Matías nodded.

"Then what should we do?"

Marcos considered the question.

"Perhaps we should look at what Scripture actually says believers are to do with the flesh."

Elías nodded.

"That would be a good place to continue."

Adrián swallowed.

"Well, I have one request."

"What's that?" Matías asked.

"Can we remember what we've agreed on today?"

Marcos smiled.

"Which part?"

"That changing harmful behavior is good. That helping people develop better habits is good. That accountability can be helpful."

Matías nodded.

"Yes."

"And that none of those observations, by itself, tells us whether we've understood the flesh."

Elías looked at him.

"Precisely."

Matías considered the statement.

"I can agree with that."

Marcos nodded.

"So can I."

Adrián looked pleased.

"Excellent. We have agreement."

Matías smiled.

"Don't celebrate too soon."

"Why?"

"Because we still haven't answered the question."

Adrián sighed.

"There's always something."

Elías began gathering his books.

Marcos glanced at the time.

"We should probably get going."

Matías closed his notebook.

"I'd like to study this further."

"Me too," Adrián said.

Matías looked surprised.

"Really?"

"Yes. Especially the merry-go-round."

Matías smiled.

"What about it?"

Adrián reached for his jacket.

"I want to know whether Paul tells us to slow it down."

Elías looked at him.

"Or whether he describes something different altogether."

Adrián nodded.

"Exactly."

Marcos stood.

"Then next time, let's examine the passages."

Matías tucked his notebook into his bag.

"Agreed."

Adrián glanced at the last piece of cake on his plate.

Then at Matías.

"Do you think I should leave that?"

Matías smiled.

"That depends."

"On what?"

"How fast your merry-go-round is spinning."

Adrián picked up his fork.

"Very slowly, my friend."

He ate the last piece.

"Very slowly."

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

The bell rang as the four men stepped outside.

# Chapter 16

## According to the Flesh

"How's the merry-go-round?"

Adrián looked up from his coffee.

Matías was standing beside the table, smiling.

"Still spinning," Adrián replied. "Although I've made considerable progress."

"Really?"

"Only one piece of cake today."

"It's ten in the morning."

"Exactly. Remarkable self-control."

Matías laughed and took his seat.

Marcos arrived carrying his laptop bag, and Elías followed a few minutes later with his familiar leather satchel.

"Good morning, gentlemen."

Adrián looked at the bag.

"Before you sit down, I have a question."

Elías stopped.

"Yes?"

"Have you brought Dr. Whitmore?"

"Not personally."

"Good. We only have four chairs."

Marcos laughed.

Elías set the satchel beside his chair.

"I did bring my Bible."

"Excellent. We can always use that."

The waitress came by with their usual coffees. She glanced at Adrián.

"No cake today?"

"Apparently I'm being monitored."

She smiled.

"I'll check on you later."

"See?" Adrián said as she walked away. "Accountability everywhere."

Matías opened his notebook.

"I've been thinking about our last discussion."

"The merry-go-round?" Marcos asked.

"Yes. And retraining the brain."

Adrián nodded.

"I've been thinking about that too."

Matías looked pleased.

"Really?"

"Yes. I still think both ideas make sense when it comes to changing habits."

"Good."

"Although we never established whether changing habits is the same thing as dealing with the flesh."

Matías nodded.

"That's where we left it."

Elías opened his Bible.

"I came across an expression in Paul that might help us investigate that."

Adrián smiled.

"Another Greek word?"

"No. This one's in English."

"Wonderful. We're making progress."

Elías turned to the second letter to the Corinthians.

"Paul repeatedly uses an expression that I think deserves attention."

"Which expression?" Marcos asked.

"*According to the flesh.*"

Matías looked up.

"According to the flesh?"

"Yes."

"What does that mean?"

"That's what I'd like us to examine."

Adrián grinned.

"An answer would have been nice."

Elías smiled.

"Let's start with Second Corinthians, chapter ten."

He found the passage.

"Verse three."

Matías followed along in his Bible.

Elías read aloud.

*"For though we walk in the flesh, we do not war according to the flesh."*

Marcos looked at the verse.

"That's interesting."

"Why?" Adrián asked.

"Paul uses the word flesh twice in the same sentence."

Matías nodded.

"And apparently not in the same way."

Elías looked at him.

"What makes you say that?"

"Well, he says he walks in the flesh, yet doesn't wage war according to the flesh."

Adrián considered that.

"So whatever he's admitting in the first part, he's denying in the second."

"Exactly," Marcos said.

Adrián looked at Elías.

"Then perhaps we'd better find out what he means by walking in the flesh."

Elías nodded.

"In this context, Paul is acknowledging his ordinary human existence. He lives in a human body, with human limitations."

Matías looked at the verse again.

"That makes sense. Paul certainly isn't confessing that he's living in sin."

"No," Elías replied.

Marcos continued reading.

"And yet he says he doesn't wage war according to the flesh."

Adrián looked puzzled.

"So he lives as a human being, yet refuses to fight in a certain way."

"That's one way to put it," Elías replied.

Matías looked at the passage.

"What kind of fighting is he talking about?"

Elías turned back a few verses.

"Let's read the context."

He read Paul's reference to people who regarded him as walking according to the flesh.

Marcos followed carefully.

"So someone was accusing Paul of something."

"Yes," Elías said.

"Of operating according to the flesh."

"That's right."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"Was he fighting with the Corinthians?"

"Not physically," Elías replied. "He was defending his ministry and addressing opposition."

Matías nodded.

"Then the warfare is connected to that conflict."

"Yes."

Marcos read the next verse.

*"For the weapons of our warfare are not of the flesh, but divinely powerful for the destruction of fortresses."*

He paused.

"So Paul contrasts fleshly weapons with divine power."

Adrián nodded.

"That's certainly what he says."

Matías continued into the following verse.

"Destroying speculations and every lofty thing raised up against the knowledge of God."

Elías nodded.

"That's part of the warfare he's describing."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"Then he isn't talking about swinging swords."

"No," Elías replied.

"He's talking about confronting arguments and opposition."

"Among other things described in the passage, yes."

Marcos returned to verse three.

"So the contrast isn't between a physical battle and a spiritual battle in the ordinary sense."

Elías considered his wording.

"Paul does use warfare language, and he describes weapons that aren't fleshly. We shouldn't remove the spiritual dimension. His immediate concern is how he conducts his ministry and confronts opposition."

Matías nodded.

"Then we should be careful about calling every personal struggle a spiritual battle simply because Paul uses that language here."

"Agreed," Elías said.

Adrián looked at the verse again.

"There's still something I find interesting."

"What?" Marcos asked.

"Paul doesn't say he has stopped being human."

Matías smiled.

"Obviously."

"No, think about it. He's still a man. He still lives in a body. He still faces opposition."

"Yes."

"Yet he says there's a way of conducting himself that he refuses to use."

Elías nodded.

"That's a fair observation."

Adrián sat back.

"So *according to the flesh* seems to describe something about how he operates."

Matías considered that.

"At least in this passage."

"Right."

Marcos smiled.

"You're learning to qualify your conclusions."

"Terrible habit. I used to be much more confident."

The waitress returned with the coffee pot.

"Refills?"

"Please," Marcos said.

She filled his cup and turned toward Adrián.

"Still no cake?"

"I'm resisting temptation."

"How's that going?"

"Ask me after lunch."

She laughed and moved away.

Elías turned another page.

"There's a second passage that may help."

Adrián looked at him.

"Still in Corinthians?"

"Yes."

"Paul really did have a lot to say to those people."

Matías smiled.

"Apparently he needed to."

Elías found Second Corinthians chapter five.

"Verse sixteen."

He read aloud.

*"Therefore from now on we recognize no one according to the flesh..."*

Marcos looked up.

"Now that's different."

"How so?" Matías asked.

"Paul isn't talking about fighting. He's talking about how he regards people."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

Adrián looked at the verse.

"What does it mean to recognize someone according to the flesh?"

Matías offered an answer.

"Perhaps judging them by outward appearances."

"That's possible," Elías replied. "Let's look at the context."

They read the surrounding verses.

Marcos stopped at verse twelve.

"Paul mentions people who take pride in appearance rather than in heart."

Matías nodded.

"That seems relevant."

"And earlier," Elías added, "he talks about what is visible and what is unseen."

Adrián looked between the passages.

"So there may be a contrast between outward measures and something deeper."

"Yes," Elías said. "Although we should be careful. The expression in verse sixteen also belongs to Paul's larger argument about Christ's death and resurrection."

Matías read the next verse.

*"Therefore if anyone is in Christ, he is a new creature..."*

He paused.

"We looked at that passage when we were discussing salvation."

"We did," Adrián replied.

Elías nodded.

"And notice how Paul reaches it. He has just spoken about Christ dying for all, and about those who live no longer living for themselves, but for Him."

Marcos looked at verse sixteen again.

"So Paul's changed way of regarding people is connected to what Christ has accomplished."

"Yes."

Adrián considered that.

"Then we shouldn't simply turn this into advice about being less judgmental."

Elías smiled.

"Precisely."

Matías nodded.

"Although it certainly challenges the way we evaluate people."

"It may have important implications for that," Elías replied.

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"People can be impressive for all kinds of reasons."

"Certainly," Matías said.

"Education. Influence. Wealth. Reputation."

Adrián smiled.

"Musical talent."

Matías glanced at him.

"I was wondering how long it would take."

Marcos continued.

"And churches often make judgments based on those things."

Elías nodded.

"Sometimes."

Adrián looked at his Bible.

"Then perhaps a person can make a judgment that seems perfectly reasonable from an ordinary human perspective, yet Paul would question the basis of that judgment."

Matías considered the thought.

"Possibly. Although we need to establish exactly what Paul means here."

Adrián smiled.

"Now you sound like Elías."

Matías laughed.

"I'm beginning to understand why he says that."

Elías looked pleased.

Marcos turned back to Second Corinthians ten.

"Can I make an observation?"

"Go ahead," Elías said.

"In chapter ten, Paul speaks about the way he conducts his warfare."

"Yes."

"In chapter five, he speaks about the way he regards people."

"Right."

"So *according to the flesh* isn't limited to outwardly immoral conduct."

Elías nodded.

"These passages certainly show that Paul uses the expression in contexts other than a list of immoral behaviors."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"That seems important."

"It does."

Matías studied the verses for a moment.

"Although I'm not sure we've discovered what the flesh itself is."

"No," Elías said. "We've discovered something about how Paul uses the expression."

Adrián nodded.

"That's progress."

"Some progress," Marcos replied.

Adrián grinned.

"Don't spoil it."

Elías turned to Romans.

"There's another passage worth comparing."

Adrián sighed.

"Of course there is."

"Romans eight."

Matías found it quickly.

"Which verse?"

"Verse four."

Elías read the reference to those who walk not according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit.

Marcos nodded.

"There's our expression again."

"Yes."

Matías continued into verse five.

*"For those who are according to the flesh set their minds on the things of the flesh, but those who are according to the Spirit, the things of the Spirit."*

He looked up.

"Now Paul connects it with the mind."

Adrián turned toward him.

"Which brings us back to retraining the brain."

"Exactly," Matías replied.

Marcos looked at the passage.

"That makes the earlier discussion more interesting."

Elías nodded.

"It does."

Matías continued.

"If the mind is involved, perhaps changing patterns of thinking is part of the answer."

"Perhaps," Elías said.

Adrián looked at him.

"You're being careful again."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because Paul says people set their minds on the things of the flesh or the things of the Spirit. We still need to understand what he means by those expressions."

Matías nodded.

"Fair enough."

Marcos read the verse again.

"There's a difference between saying the mind is involved and saying the flesh is simply a pattern in the brain."

"Exactly," Elías replied.

Adrián looked toward Matías.

"And that's what we were wondering last time."

"Yes."

"Whether changing a habit is the same thing as dealing with the flesh."

Matías nodded.

"I remember."

Elías continued.

"There's another point we should be careful about. Romans eight is part of a larger argument. We shouldn't isolate verse five and assume it tells us everything about the flesh."

Marcos glanced at the following verses.

"Paul goes on to say quite a bit."

"He does."

Adrián looked at the page.

"Then perhaps we should leave the rest for another discussion."

Elías smiled.

"That's unusually restrained of you."

"I'm applying the brakes."

Matías laughed.

"Very gradually."

Adrián lifted his cup.

"Exactly."

Marcos returned to his notes.

"Can we compare what we've seen?"

Matías nodded.

"Go ahead."

"In Second Corinthians ten, Paul refuses to conduct his warfare according to the flesh."

"Yes," Elías said.

"In Second Corinthians five, he says he no longer regards people according to the flesh."

"Right."

"And in Romans eight, he contrasts walking according to the flesh with walking according to the Spirit."

Matías nodded.

"Those are three different contexts."

"Exactly."

Adrián considered the passages.

"So the expression has something to do with how a person conducts himself, how he regards others, and how he lives."

"Those are the areas where we've encountered it," Elías replied.

Matías looked thoughtful.

"That still doesn't tell us everything about the flesh."

"No."

"Or whether the merry-go-round illustration is adequate."

"No."

Adrián smiled.

"Or whether retraining the brain deals with it."

"Not yet," Marcos said.

Matías closed his notebook.

"Then perhaps we should stop trying to define it before we've examined enough passages."

Elías nodded.

"I think that's wise."

Adrián looked surprised.

"Did Matías just suggest we slow down?"

Marcos smiled.

"Write down the date."

Matías laughed.

"Very funny."

Adrián looked at the open Bible.

"There's something else I'm wondering."

"What?" Elías asked.

"When Paul says he doesn't wage war according to the flesh, does that mean a person could be doing something worthwhile and still be doing it in the wrong way?"

Elías considered the question.

"That's certainly a possibility worth examining. Paul was engaged in a legitimate ministry, and he was concerned about the manner in which that ministry was conducted."

Marcos nodded.

"So the activity itself doesn't answer every question about how it's being carried out."

"Right," Elías said.

Matías looked at the passage again.

"That would have implications for a great many things."

Adrián smiled.

"Such as?"

"Teaching. Leadership. Correction. Helping people."

Marcos nodded.

"Running a church."

"Possibly," Matías replied.

Adrián looked toward Elías.

"Even studying Scripture?"

Elías glanced up.

"Studying Scripture?"

"Yes. Could someone study Scripture in a way that Paul might describe as according to the flesh?"

Elías considered the question.

"I wouldn't want to apply the expression without examining the relevant passages."

"Of course."

"Scripture commands us to study and handle the Word accurately."

"I agree."

Elías continued.

"So we'd need a sound biblical reason before describing someone's study as fleshly."

Adrián nodded.

"Fair enough."

He returned to his coffee.

Marcos looked at the clock above the counter.

"We should probably wrap up."

Matías began gathering his things.

"I think we've made some progress."

Adrián grinned.

"Careful. We might become optimistic."

Elías closed his Bible.

"We've established that Paul's expression deserves more attention than we've given it."

Marcos nodded.

"And that we shouldn't confuse a change in behavior with a complete explanation of the flesh."

"Yes," Matías replied.

Adrián stood and pulled on his jacket.

"Well, gentlemen, I have to say something."

The others looked at him.

"What's that?"

"When we started this investigation, I thought carnality meant people doing things they shouldn't."

Matías smiled.

"That's what most people think."

"Now I'm wondering whether it can also involve the way people do things they should."

Marcos considered that.

"That's an interesting question."

Elías nodded.

"One worth testing against Scripture."

Adrián smiled.

"Then I suppose we'll have to keep reading."

Matías stood.

"That was always the plan."

They made their way toward the door.

The waitress called after them.

"Same time next week?"

"Unless we solve everything before then," Adrián replied.

She laughed.

"So I'll see you next week."

"Probably."

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

Outside, Marcos turned toward the others.

"There's something I like about where this is going."

"What's that?" Matías asked.

"We're beginning to distinguish between what people do and the basis on which they do it."

Adrián nodded.

"Yes."

Elías adjusted his satchel.

"And we still have to establish what Scripture says about that basis."

"Exactly," Marcos replied.

Matías looked thoughtful.

"Then that's where we should continue."

Adrián glanced toward the café window.

"Good."

He paused.

"And next week I'm ordering cake."

Matías smiled.

"What happened to self-control?"

Adrián shrugged.

"I'm still working on the merry-go-round."

They laughed and headed toward their cars.

# Chapter 17

## Christ Plus Something

Matías had arrived early.

By the time Adrián entered the café, two Bibles lay open on the table, along with a notebook and a sheet of handwritten references.

Adrián stopped beside the chair.

"Are we being audited?"

Matías looked up.

"What?"

"The paperwork. Should I have brought my tax returns?"

"I'm studying Galatians."

"Ah. Circumcision."

Matías smiled.

"Among other things."

"Wonderful. Nothing like a cheerful subject to begin the evening."

Adrián sat down and reached for the menu.

"Have you ordered?"

"Just coffee."

"Good. I'd hate to miss the opportunity to eat before the argument starts."

Marcos arrived a few minutes later, followed by Elías.

The four settled into their usual places.

Matías waited until the waitress had brought their coffee before turning his Bible toward the others.

"There's something I don't understand."

Adrián smiled.

"Only one thing?"

"For now."

Elías opened his Bible.

"Go ahead."

Matías found Galatians 5.

"Listen to what Paul says."

He read verse two.

*"Behold I, Paul, say to you that if you receive circumcision, Christ will be of no benefit to you."*

He looked up.

"That's an extraordinary statement."

"It is," Elías agreed.

"God commanded circumcision, didn't He?"

"Yes. To Abraham and his descendants."

"Genesis seventeen."

"Correct."

Matías looked around the table.

"So obedience to God could never be wrong."

Adrián nodded.

"Of course."

Marcos considered the statement.

"Provided we're talking about something God actually requires of us."

Matías turned toward Elías.

"What do you think?"

Elías shifted in his chair.

"Well, yes... although..."

"Although?"

"It's not quite..."

He glanced down at Galatians.

"I mean, the way Paul approaches circumcision here is... well, you have to consider the relationship between the covenants."

Matías waited.

Elías tried again.

"There's the Abrahamic covenant, the Mosaic Law, the position of Gentile believers, and the question of justification. Circumcision had a particular significance, and..."

He stopped.

Adrián looked impressed.

"That was a magnificent sentence."

Elías gave him a look.

"Thank you."

"I understood almost half of it."

Matías leaned forward.

"Then I don't understand why Paul is making such a serious issue of it."

Elías looked down at the passage.

"Let's examine what he actually says."

Marcos raised a finger.

"Before we do, wasn't Timothy circumcised?"

Elías nodded.

"Acts sixteen."

"By Paul?"

"Yes."

Marcos looked at Matías.

"So Paul had Timothy circumcised, yet warns the Galatians that accepting circumcision would make Christ of no benefit to them."

Adrián leaned back.

"Now I'm beginning to understand why Matías brought all those references."

Matías smiled.

"There's Titus too."

Elías turned to Galatians 2.

"Exactly."

He read verse three, where Paul says Titus, though Greek, was not compelled to be circumcised.

Then he continued through the passage, explaining that Paul had refused to yield to those pressing the demand so that the truth of the gospel would remain with them.

Marcos frowned.

"So Timothy was circumcised, Titus wasn't, and the Galatians were warned against it."

"Yes," Elías replied.

"Then the physical act itself can't explain Paul's reaction."

"No."

Matías looked at Elías.

"All right. What does?"

Elías turned back to chapter five.

"Read the next two verses."

Matías read Paul's statement that the man receiving circumcision in this sense was obligated to keep the whole Law.

Then he reached verse four.

*"You have been severed from Christ, you who are seeking to be justified by law; you have fallen from grace."*

He stopped.

"Seeking to be justified by law."

Elías nodded.

"That's the issue Paul identifies."

Matías looked at the verse again.

"So they weren't simply getting circumcised."

"No. They were being persuaded to accept it as a requirement for their standing before God."

Marcos leaned forward.

"That explains Timothy and Titus."

"How?" Adrián asked.

"Timothy wasn't circumcised to make him acceptable to God. Paul did it because of the Jews among whom they would be working."

Elías nodded.

"And in Titus's case, circumcision was being pressed as a requirement. Paul refused."

Adrián considered that.

"So Paul wasn't changing his mind about circumcision. The question was what people were making it necessary for."

"Precisely," Elías said.

Matías looked relieved.

"Good. That makes sense."

Adrián smiled.

"Wonderful. Shall we order dessert?"

"Not yet."

"Of course not."

Matías turned to chapter three.

"There's still something I don't understand."

Elías waited.

"These people had already believed in Christ, hadn't they?"

"Yes."

"They'd received the gospel."

"Yes."

"Then why would they think they needed circumcision?"

Marcos shrugged.

"Maybe they thought it would make them more obedient."

Matías nodded.

"That's what I was thinking."

Elías found Galatians 3:3.

"Paul asks them whether, having begun by the Spirit, they are now being perfected by the flesh."

Matías repeated the word.

"Perfected."

"Yes."

"Completed."

"That's the idea."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"So they weren't necessarily thinking they were leaving Christ behind."

"No," Elías said.

"They thought they were adding something."

Matías leaned back.

"Something that would improve their standing."

"Exactly."

Adrián looked between them.

"Christ plus circumcision."

Elías nodded.

"Christ, with the Law treated as necessary for justification and completion."

Adrián smiled.

"I can see why someone might think that would make a better Christian."

Matías looked at him.

"So can I."

"Really?"

"Of course. If someone tells me there's something God wants me to do, I want to do it."

"Even if you don't understand why?"

"I'd want to understand. That doesn't mean I'd refuse to obey."

Adrián nodded.

"Fair enough."

Matías looked at the passage.

"And if someone tells me that greater obedience will produce greater holiness, I don't find that difficult to believe."

Marcos agreed.

"Most people wouldn't."

Elías was about to speak when Matías continued.

"Look, we've spent weeks discussing Corinth. What was wrong with those people?"

Adrián smiled faintly.

"Jealousy. Strife. Divisions."

"Exactly. And what did we say they needed?"

"To grow up," Adrián replied.

Matías nodded.

"To stop behaving like children. To take their Christian lives seriously."

"Yes."

"So what's wrong with expecting Christians to obey God?"

Elías answered.

"Nothing. Paul expects obedience."

"Then why does this conversation keep sounding as though the pursuit of obedience is somehow dangerous?"

Adrián leaned forward.

"Because the Galatians were adding something to Christ."

Matías looked directly at him.

"And I'm asking what you mean by that."

Adrián paused.

Matías continued.

"If I tell a man to stop lying to his wife, have I added something to Christ?"

"No."

"If I tell him to stop getting drunk and frightening his children?"

"No."

"If I tell him to take responsibility for the damage he's caused?"

"Of course not."

"Then don't tell me that expecting obedience is the problem."

Adrián sat back.

"I didn't say it was."

"It certainly sounds like it."

Marcos looked at Elías.

"Can you help us distinguish the two?"

Elías considered the question.

"The Galatian controversy concerned justification through the Law. We shouldn't equate that with every instruction a Christian receives."

Matías nodded firmly.

"Thank you."

Adrián looked at him.

"Then answer me something."

"What?"

"If Christ was sufficient for the Galatians, why did adding circumcision threaten to make Him of no benefit to them?"

Matías hesitated.

"Because they were seeking justification through the Law. We've established that."

"Right. They thought the addition would improve things."

"Yes."

"And Paul says it would do the opposite."

"Yes."

Adrián spread his hands.

"That's what I find strange."

Matías looked down at his Bible.

"So do I."

For a moment, neither spoke.

The waitress arrived with fresh coffee.

"Everything all right?"

Marcos smiled.

"We're trying to determine whether adding something makes it better."

She glanced at Adrián's cup.

"Not always. He put four sugars in his coffee last week."

"Three," Adrián said.

"Four."

"I see my reputation precedes me."

"Your blood sugar probably does too."

Matías laughed.

She filled their cups and moved to another table.

Marcos watched her go.

"She has a point."

"About what?" Adrián asked.

"More isn't always better."

Adrián smiled.

"Thank you, Professor."

Matías returned to the discussion.

"I heard a pastor say something once that might explain why this troubles me."

Elías looked up.

"What did he say?"

"He said, 'I believe in grace, but I would never teach it, because people would just abuse it.'"

Adrián stared at him.

"He actually said that?"

"Yes."

"What did he teach instead?"

"I don't know."

"That's a fairly important question."

Matías nodded.

"Perhaps. Although I understood his concern."

Adrián looked surprised.

"You did?"

"I've known people who used grace as an excuse for terrible behavior. They'd say they were forgiven, then go right back to hurting their families."

Marcos nodded.

"I've seen that too."

"And someone has to tell them it isn't acceptable," Matías continued. "Someone has to insist that following Christ makes a difference."

Elías nodded.

"Certainly."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"Then teach them what grace actually means."

Matías shook his head.

"You say that as though it's simple."

"I didn't say it was simple."

"People can do a great deal of damage while they're learning."

Adrián had no immediate answer.

Elías turned to Romans.

"Paul was accused of encouraging sin."

He read Romans 3:8, where Paul reports the slanderous accusation that he taught people to do evil so that good might come.

Matías looked up.

"That's almost exactly what people fear about grace."

"Yes," Elías replied.

"And in Romans six," Marcos added, "Paul asks whether we should continue in sin so grace may increase."

Elías nodded.

"And answers, 'May it never be.'"

Matías leaned forward.

"Exactly. Paul didn't want people continuing in sin."

"No," Elías said.

"Then he was concerned about the same thing I am."

"Certainly."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"Yet he didn't stop teaching grace."

Matías looked at him.

"No."

"And he didn't tell the Galatians they needed circumcision to make sure they behaved."

"No."

"Then what did he expect to produce the change?"

Matías opened his Bible.

"That's what I want to know."

Marcos looked at Galatians 3.

"Perhaps we should pay attention to Paul's question."

Elías nodded.

"Go ahead."

Marcos read:

*"Did you receive the Spirit by the works of the Law, or by hearing with faith?"*

He looked up.

"Paul begins by asking how they received the Spirit."

"Yes," Elías said.

"Then he asks whether they're being perfected by the flesh."

"Correct."

Matías frowned.

"So?"

Marcos considered his answer.

"Maybe the question isn't whether obedience matters. Maybe it's whether the means they're depending on can actually produce what they're seeking."

Matías looked at him.

"That's an interesting distinction. It still leaves us with the question of how people change."

"Agreed."

Elías nodded.

"And Galatians has more to say about that."

He turned several pages.

"There's something else we should notice about the people promoting circumcision."

Matías looked up.

"What?"

"Paul discusses their motives near the end of the letter."

Elías read Galatians 6:12–13.

The teachers wanted to make a good showing in the flesh. They were compelling circumcision to avoid persecution for the cross of Christ, and they wanted to boast in the Galatians' flesh.

Marcos interrupted.

"Wait. They were afraid of persecution?"

"That's one motive Paul identifies," Elías said.

"And they wanted something to boast about?"

"Yes."

Matías looked troubled.

"I assumed they were concerned about obedience."

"Paul gives us reason to question their motives," Elías replied.

Adrián leaned forward.

"So the Galatians thought they were becoming more complete, while the teachers were trying to protect themselves?"

"At least some of the teachers were motivated by fear of persecution and the desire to boast," Elías answered.

Marcos looked down at the passage.

"That's disturbing."

Matías nodded.

"Especially when you consider how much influence a teacher can have over people who genuinely want to please God."

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Then Matías turned back to chapter five.

"I still think we're missing something."

Adrián smiled.

"We've been missing something for weeks."

"I'm serious."

"So am I. Mostly."

Matías ignored him.

"We've established that the Galatians were being persuaded to add the Law to Christ. We've established that Paul considered that a serious departure from grace."

"Yes," Elías said.

"And we've established that the teachers had motives of their own."

"Yes."

Matías looked at the others.

"Now what does any of that have to do with the way these Christians were actually living? I mean, if they're living obedient lives, isn't that what matters?"

Elías glanced at his watch.

"Excuse me a moment, gentlemen."

He rose and headed toward the washroom.

Matías looked at Marcos.

"Well?"

Marcos had been reading ahead.

"There's something here."

"What?"

"Chapter five. Verse fifteen."

Matías found the passage.

He read aloud.

*"But if you bite and devour one another, take care that you are not consumed by one another."*

Adrián raised his eyebrows.

"That doesn't sound particularly obedient."

Matías ignored the remark.

Marcos continued.

"Look at verse twenty."

Matías read through the list.

"Enmities, strife, jealousy, outbursts of anger, disputes, dissensions, factions..."

He stopped.

Adrián leaned forward.

"That sounds familiar."

Matías turned to First Corinthians.

He found chapter three and read Paul's words about jealousy and strife.

For a moment he stared at the two passages.

"Corinth."

Marcos nodded.

"That's what I was thinking."

Matías looked from one Bible to the other.

"Wait a minute. Corinth was a church full of spiritual gifts."

"Yes," Adrián said.

"And the Galatians were being encouraged to obey the Law."

"Yes."

"Two completely different approaches."

Marcos nodded.

"And some remarkably similar behavior."

Matías shook his head.

"That doesn't prove obedience produces carnality."

"I didn't say it did," Marcos replied.

"Well, that's what it sounds like."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Matías, we're only noticing what Paul wrote."

"I can see what Paul wrote."

"Then what's the problem?"

Matías looked at him.

"The problem is that we keep talking as though expecting Christians to obey God is somehow a mistake."

"Nobody said that."

"Then what are you saying?"

Adrián hesitated.

"I'm saying that whatever the Galatians were being persuaded to add wasn't producing what they expected."

"And how do you know what they expected?"

"Well, they were trying to become more complete, weren't they?"

Matías looked away.

Marcos spoke carefully.

"I think we're questioning whether the Law could accomplish what they were looking to it for."

Matías gave a short laugh.

"Of course. Now even obedience can't accomplish anything."

"That's not what I said."

"Then what did you say?"

Marcos paused.

Matías closed one of his Bibles.

"You know what? I've had enough."

Adrián looked surprised.

"Of what?"

"Of this."

Matías began gathering his things.

"Every time we talk about holiness, it turns into another explanation of why people shouldn't be expected to do anything."

"That's not fair," Adrián said.

Matías stopped.

"Fair?"

His voice rose.

"I've spent years dealing with the consequences of people who don't take obedience seriously. Families destroyed. Promises broken. People hurt. And somehow the answer is always that we shouldn't expect too much of them."

Marcos shook his head.

"Matías, nobody here believes that."

"Then stop making it sound that way."

The words were louder than he intended.

A couple at the next table glanced over.

Matías noticed.

He looked down.

For a moment, he seemed embarrassed.

Then he picked up his coat.

"I need to go."

Adrián stood halfway.

"Matías—"

"Please. Not tonight."

He walked toward the door.

The bell rang as he stepped outside.

Adrián remained half-standing, watching through the window.

Marcos had turned in his chair.

Neither spoke as Matías crossed the parking lot.

He reached his car, opened the door, and climbed inside.

Then he just sat there.

Adrián slowly lowered himself into his chair.

"I've never seen him like that."

Marcos didn't answer.

They watched their friend through the window, neither quite knowing what to do.

For a moment Adrián thought about going after him.

Then Matías started the engine.

A few seconds later, his car pulled out of the parking lot.

Adrián stared after him.

"Well, that went badly."

Marcos rubbed his forehead.

"Yes. It did."

Neither had noticed Elías returning.

He stopped beside the table.

"Where's Matías?"

Adrián looked up.

"He left."

Elías frowned.

"Left?"

"Yes."

Elías glanced at the empty chair.

Then he noticed the two Bibles lying open on the table.

"Without his Bibles?"

Adrián looked down.

Marcos turned around.

For the first time, they noticed what Matías had left behind.

"Oh," Adrián said.

Elías looked from one man to the other.

"What happened?"

Neither answered.

Elías pulled out his chair.

"I was gone five minutes."

Marcos looked at Adrián.

Adrián looked at Marcos.

Finally, Adrián said, "You missed quite a bit."

Elías sat down slowly.

"Apparently."

He looked at the two open Bibles.

One lay open to First Corinthians.

The other to Galatians.

He studied them for a moment.

"Why are these open?"

Marcos leaned forward.

"That's where things got interesting."

Adrián looked toward the window once more.

"And then they got considerably less interesting."

Elías looked at him.

"What does that mean?"

Adrián sighed.

"Well, we found Corinth in Galatia."

Elías stared at him.

"You found what?"

Marcos turned the first Bible toward him.

"First Corinthians three."

Then the other.

"Galatians five."

Elías looked at the passages.

He read Paul's words about jealousy and strife among the Corinthians.

Then he looked at the list of the works of the flesh in Galatians.

For a moment he said nothing.

"That's interesting."

Adrián gave a short laugh.

"That's what we thought."

Elías looked up.

"And Matías?"

Marcos leaned back.

"He didn't take it very well."

"Why?"

Adrián looked at him.

"Because he thinks we're saying obedience produces carnality."

Elías frowned.

"That's not what Paul says."

"I know."

"And that's not what we believe."

"I know that too."

Marcos spoke.

"Matías knows it as well. At least I think he does."

Elías looked at the empty chair.

"Then what happened?"

Marcos considered his answer.

"He started thinking about the people he's tried to help over the years."

Adrián nodded.

"The families. The people who've been hurt. The Christians who used grace as an excuse to do whatever they wanted."

Elías looked thoughtful.

"And he thinks we're dismissing the importance of obedience."

"Yes," Marcos said.

Elías looked at the two Bibles again.

"That's unfortunate."

Adrián shook his head.
