"It's more than unfortunate. He's our friend."

"I know."

"And he left because of something we were discussing."

Elías nodded slowly.

"I know."

For a while nobody spoke.

Then Marcos looked at the passages again.

"Still, there's something here we shouldn't overlook."

Adrián stared at him.

"Marcos."

"What?"

"Our friend just walked out."

"I know."

"And you're still thinking about the passages?"

Marcos looked toward the window.

"Yes."

Adrián shook his head.

Marcos continued.

"Because whatever happened between us tonight, these passages are still here."

Elías looked at him.

"Go ahead."

Marcos turned to First Corinthians.

"Paul called the Corinthians fleshly because of jealousy and strife."

"Yes," Elías said.

"And Corinth was a church rich in spiritual gifts."

"Correct."

Marcos turned to Galatians.

"Now here we have churches being persuaded to come under the Law."

"Yes."

"And Paul warns them about biting and devouring one another. Then he lists strife, jealousy, disputes, dissensions, and factions among the works of the flesh."

Elías nodded.

"That's correct."

Adrián leaned forward.

"That's what struck us."

"What?" Elías asked.

"The difference between the churches."

Marcos nodded.

"Corinth was full of spiritual activity."

"And Galatia was being persuaded toward greater religious obligation," Adrián continued.

Elías looked at the passages.

"Very different situations."

"Exactly," Marcos said.

Adrián pointed at First Corinthians.

"One church had all those spiritual gifts."

Then he pointed at Galatians.

"The other was being persuaded to add the Law."

He looked at Elías.

"And some of the same ugly behavior was showing up."

Elías studied the verses.

"Jealousy. Strife. Factions."

Marcos nodded.

"Remarkably similar."

Elías considered the comparison.

"We should be careful. Paul explicitly identifies jealousy and strife among the Corinthians. In Galatians, he warns about destructive behavior and lists the works of the flesh. We shouldn't assume every item in the list was present among them."

Marcos nodded.

"Agreed."

"And we shouldn't assume circumcision caused every conflict."

"Agreed."

Adrián smiled faintly.

"Anything else?"

Elías looked at him.

"Yes. We shouldn't conclude that obedience is fleshly."

Adrián raised his hands.

"Please tell Matías that."

Elías smiled briefly.

"I intend to."

Marcos looked at the two Bibles.

"Even with those distinctions, the resemblance is striking."

Elías nodded.

"It is."

Adrián leaned back.

"That's what I can't get over."

"What?"

"Corinth and Galatia."

He looked at the passages.

"If you'd asked me which church needed more discipline, I'd have said Corinth."

Marcos nodded.

"And if you'd asked Matías which church was more likely to become mature, he'd probably have chosen the one emphasizing obedience."

"Exactly."

Elías looked at Galatians.

"Yet Paul challenges the Galatians for seeking completion through the flesh."

Adrián leaned forward.

"That's the part that bothers me."

Elías waited.

"They thought they were adding something that would make them better Christians."

"Yes."

"And Paul says that addition, when made necessary for justification, would make Christ of no benefit to them."

"Yes."

Adrián shook his head.

"That's astonishing."

Marcos looked at Galatians 3:3.

"Having begun by the Spirit, are you now being perfected by the flesh?"

Elías nodded.

"Paul is asking whether the Law can complete what the Spirit began."

Adrián looked at Galatians 5.

"And then he tells them to walk by the Spirit."

"Yes."

"Not to add circumcision."

"No."

"Not to depend on the Law to make them complete."

"No."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Then perhaps we've been asking the wrong question."

Elías looked at him.

"Which question?"

"We've been asking which church had the better approach."

Adrián nodded.

"Spiritual gifts or obedience."

"Yes."

Marcos looked at the passages again.

"What if neither one, by itself, tells us whether a church is mature?"

Elías considered that.

"That would certainly be consistent with what we've found so far."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Then what does?"

Elías looked at Galatians 5:16.

*"Walk by the Spirit, and you will not carry out the desire of the flesh."*

He read the words aloud.

Marcos nodded slowly.

"That's where Paul directs them."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"Then why were they looking somewhere else?"

Elías didn't answer immediately.

Marcos looked down at the open Bible.

"Perhaps they thought they were improving upon what they already had."

Adrián nodded.

"Christ plus something."

"Yes."

Elías turned back to Galatians 5:2.

"And Paul says that addition, when treated as necessary for justification, would make Christ of no benefit to them."

Adrián shook his head.

"That's quite a price to pay for something you thought would make you more spiritual."

The waitress approached with the bill.

"Have we solved anything?"

Adrián looked up.

"We've discovered that two groups of Christians can take very different approaches and still end up fighting with each other."

She smiled.

"That doesn't sound particularly surprising."

Marcos laughed.

"Apparently you've met Christians."

"I've served coffee for twenty years."

She placed the bill on the table.

"Good night, gentlemen."

After she left, Elías looked at Matías's empty chair.

"We need to talk to him."

Marcos nodded.

"Yes."

Adrián looked down at the two Bibles.

"And we need to bring these back."

Elías carefully closed them.

For a moment he held them in his hands.

Then he looked at his friends.

"I don't think Matías was wrong to defend obedience."

"Neither do I," Marcos said.

"Nor do I," Adrián added.

Elías placed the Bibles beside his own.

"Then we need to understand what Paul was actually saying."

Marcos looked at Galatians one last time.

"And why two churches with such different approaches were displaying some of the same problems."

Adrián nodded.

"That's the question I can't get out of my mind."

Elías gathered his coat.

"Perhaps we should stop for tonight."

Marcos looked at the clock.

"Probably."

Adrián glanced at Matías's empty chair.

"I wish he'd stayed."

Elías nodded.

"So do I."

They stood and headed toward the door.

Adrián picked up Matías's Bibles.

He understood something of what his friend was going through. Not long ago, Corinth had shaken his own confidence in the things he believed made a church spiritual.

Now Galatia had done much the same to Matías.

Different convictions. Different approaches.

And somehow, the same troubling evidence of the flesh.

Adrián tucked the Bibles under his arm and followed the others out.

# Chapter 18

## What Obedience Cannot Do

Adrián arrived carrying two Bibles.

He placed them on the table, one on top of the other, and sat down.

Marcos glanced at them.

"Still haven't returned those?"

"No."

"Have you spoken to him?"

"Yesterday."

"And?"

"He said he'd be here."

Elías looked up from his coffee.

"How did he sound?"

"Like Matías."

"That's encouraging."

"Not entirely."

Adrián adjusted the Bibles so they were neatly aligned.

Marcos watched him.

"You've been doing that since you arrived."

"Doing what?"

"Straightening them."

Adrián looked down.

"Have I?"

"Three times."

"Well, they're his. He likes things orderly."

Marcos smiled.

"You're becoming more like him."

"Don't say that. I'll start making schedules."

Elías glanced toward the entrance.

"I hope he's all right."

Adrián followed his gaze.

"So do I."

The bell above the door rang.

Matías stepped inside.

He paused when he saw them, then approached the table.

"Good morning."

Adrián stood.

"You're late."

"By two minutes."

"I've been taking lessons from you."

Matías smiled faintly.

"I see."

Adrián handed him the Bibles.

"You forgot these."

Matías looked at them.

"I wondered where they were."

"Apparently, when you storm out of a café, you're supposed to take your belongings."

Matías lowered his eyes.

"Yes. About that."

He sat down.

The others waited.

"I'm sorry."

Adrián pulled out his chair.

"For leaving?"

"For the way I spoke to you."

Marcos nodded.

"We know you were upset."

"That doesn't excuse it."

"No," Marcos said. "It doesn't."

Matías looked at him.

"I was unfair to you."

"A little."

"And to Adrián."

"A little more," Adrián said.

Matías smiled.

"Probably."

Elías leaned forward.

"Was something we said particularly troubling?"

Matías looked at the Bibles.

"All of it."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"That's quite a list."

Matías laughed softly.

"I know."

He ran his hand across the cover of the larger Bible.

"I've spent most of my Christian life believing that if people took obedience seriously enough, their lives would change."

Elías nodded.

"Obedience is important."

"I know. And I haven't changed my mind about that."

"Nobody expects you to."

Matías looked at Adrián.

"Then we started talking about Corinth."

Adrián nodded.

"And I thought I understood their problem. They were immature. They needed discipline. They needed to stop being so impressed with themselves and start obeying Scripture."

"That was part of the problem," Elías said.

"Yes."

Matías paused.

"And then we came to Galatia."

Marcos waited.

"I thought we'd finally found the kind of church Corinth needed to become."

Adrián looked at him.

"A serious church."

"Exactly."

Matías glanced at the open Bibles.

"And suddenly we were reading about jealousy, strife, factions, people biting and devouring one another."

He shook his head.

"I wasn't prepared for that."

Adrián looked down at his coffee.

"Neither was I when we studied Corinth."

Matías looked at him.

Adrián continued.

"You remember how I kept talking about the gifts?"

"Yes."

"How much I admired what was happening there?"

"Of course."

"And then Paul called them fleshly."

Matías nodded.

"I remember."

Adrián smiled faintly.

"I spent half the evening trying to explain why that couldn't possibly mean what it sounded like."

Matías laughed.

"You did."

"And you were very patient with me."

"Was I?"

"Not particularly."

They laughed.

Then Adrián looked at him.

"I know what it's like to have something you believed in shaken."

Matías lowered his eyes.

For a moment he said nothing.

Then he nodded.

"Thank you."

The waitress approached.

"Good morning, gentlemen."

She looked at Matías.

"Nice to see you."

"Thank you."

"Your usual?"

"Please."

She glanced at the two Bibles.

"Found your books, I see."

Matías looked surprised.

"You noticed?"

"Hard not to. Your friends spent half the morning guarding them."

Adrián smiled.

"They're valuable."

"Then perhaps you should stop leaving them behind."

She walked away.

Matías looked at Adrián.

"Thank you for bringing them."

"You're welcome."

He hesitated.

"There's something else I wanted to say."

Elías looked up.

"Go ahead."

"I've been thinking about what happened."

Marcos nodded.

"So have we."

"And I think I understand why I became so upset."

Adrián waited.

"Because I wasn't simply defending obedience."

Matías looked at the others.

"I was defending something I've trusted for years."

Elías leaned forward.

"What do you mean?"

Matías considered his words.

"When I see someone whose life is falling apart, I want to help him change. I want to give him something practical. Something he can do."

Marcos nodded.

"That's understandable."

"And when he starts doing those things, I see improvement."

"Of course."

"So I tell him to keep going. Keep making better decisions. Keep practicing the right habits. Keep resisting the wrong ones."

Adrián listened.

"And sometimes it works," Matías continued.

Marcos nodded.

"Behavior can change."

"Exactly."

Matías looked at him.

"And that's what I kept thinking about the other night. We were talking as though all those efforts were somehow worthless."

Elías shook his head.

"They aren't worthless."

"I know."

Matías paused.

"Although I've started wondering whether I've confused two different things."

Marcos leaned forward.

"Which things?"

"Changing someone's behavior and changing the person."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"That's interesting."

Matías turned toward him.

"Is it?"

"Yes."

"Because I've been asking myself whether those are always the same thing."

Elías considered the question.

"Scripture certainly calls believers to change their conduct."

"I know."

"And it also speaks of a deeper work of God."

"Exactly."

Matías opened his Bible.

"That's why I wanted to return to Galatians."

Adrián smiled.

"Of course you did."

Matías found chapter three.

"Paul asks whether they received the Spirit by the works of the Law or by hearing with faith."

"Yes," Elías said.

"Then he asks whether they're being perfected by the flesh."

"Correct."

Matías looked up.

"What I want to understand is why he thought the Law couldn't accomplish what they were looking for."

Elías leaned forward.

"That's a much better question."

Adrián smiled.

"Careful. He's starting to sound like Dr. Whitmore."

Elías ignored him.

"Let's consider what Paul says elsewhere about the Law."

Matías nodded.

"All right."

Elías turned to Romans 7.

"Paul says the Law is holy, and the commandment is holy and righteous and good."

Matías looked pleased.

"Good."

Adrián laughed.

"We've found your favorite verse."

"There's nothing wrong with liking that verse."

"No, there isn't."

Elías continued.

"The problem isn't that God's Law is evil."

"Exactly," Matías said.

"Paul is explicit about that."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"Then what is the problem?"

Elías turned to Romans 8.

"Look at verse three."

He read Paul's statement that what the Law could not do, weak as it was through the flesh, God accomplished by sending His own Son.

Matías frowned.

"What the Law could not do."

"Yes."

"Why couldn't it?"

Elías pointed to the passage.

"Because of the flesh."

Matías looked down.

"So the problem wasn't with the Law itself."

"No."

"It was with the people under it."

"That is central to Paul's argument."

Marcos leaned forward.

"Then the Law could tell people what righteousness required without giving them the power to produce it."

Elías considered his wording.

"That's a useful way to express the distinction."

Matías looked thoughtful.

"Then what good was the Law?"

Elías smiled.

"Paul asks related questions in Romans and Galatians."

"Yes, and I would appreciate an answer."

Adrián laughed.

"He's back."

Elías continued.

"The Law revealed God's righteous requirements. It exposed sin. It had a real purpose in God's dealings with Israel."

Matías nodded.

"Of course."

"Yet it was never able to justify sinners or overcome the weakness of the flesh."

Marcos looked at Romans 8 again.

"And Paul says God did what the Law could not do."

"Yes."

Matías studied the verse.

"Through His Son."

"Exactly."

He continued into verse four, where Paul speaks of the righteous requirement of the Law being fulfilled in those who walk according to the Spirit.

Matías looked up.

"That's interesting."

Adrián smiled.

"Here we go."

Matías ignored him.

"Paul doesn't say the righteous requirement of the Law was unimportant."

"No," Elías replied.

"He says it's fulfilled in those who walk according to the Spirit."

"Yes."

Matías leaned back.

"So the Spirit accomplishes something the Law couldn't."

Elías nodded.

"That's Paul's argument."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Then the Galatians were being persuaded to rely on something that couldn't produce the result they wanted."

Elías answered carefully.

"They were being persuaded to seek justification and completion through the Law. Paul directs them back to Christ and the Spirit."

Adrián leaned forward.

"And the Law was good."

"Yes."

"Yet it couldn't do what they were expecting."

"Correct."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"That's a little like what you were saying about changing behavior."

Matías considered that.

"How?"

"Well, suppose you tell someone what he should do."

"All right."

"And he agrees."

"Yes."

"And he even starts doing it."

"Good."

Adrián smiled.

"You're enjoying this illustration already."

Matías laughed.

"Continue."

"Does that necessarily mean the flesh has stopped being a problem?"

Matías hesitated.

"No. I suppose not."

"Then perhaps doing the right thing and overcoming the flesh aren't necessarily identical."

Elías nodded.

"That's an important distinction."

Matías looked at him.

"Although I don't want us to make obedience irrelevant."

"Neither do I."

Marcos leaned forward.

"Perhaps we need to stop treating this as a choice between obedience and grace."

Matías looked at him.

"What do you mean?"

"Every time we discuss grace, you worry that we're removing obedience."

"Yes."

"And every time you emphasize obedience, Adrián worries that we're adding something to Christ."

Adrián nodded.

"That's about right."

Marcos continued.

"What if we're asking the wrong question?"

Elías looked interested.

"Go on."

"What if the question isn't whether Christians should obey?"

Matías waited.

"What if the question is what makes genuine obedience possible?"

For a moment nobody spoke.

Then Matías looked down at Romans 8.

"That's what Paul seems to be addressing."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

Matías read verse four again.

*"So that the requirement of the Law might be fulfilled in us, who do not walk according to the flesh but according to the Spirit."*

He looked up.

"Then holiness still matters."

"Certainly," Elías said.

"And obedience still matters."

"Yes."

Matías paused.

"Yet the source matters too."

Marcos nodded.

"That's what we've been trying to understand."

Adrián looked at him.

"And perhaps that's why Galatia is so troubling."

Matías turned toward him.

"Why?"

"Because they were trying to accomplish something good through something Paul said couldn't accomplish it."

Matías considered the statement.

"Yes."

Adrián continued.

"And they thought adding it to Christ would help."

"Yes."

"Yet Paul says it would make Christ of no benefit to them."

Matías looked at Galatians 5 again.

"That's still astonishing."

Elías nodded.

"It is."

For a while they sat quietly.

Then Matías spoke.

"There's something else."

Marcos smiled.

"Of course there is."

Matías looked at Adrián.

"Do you remember what we discovered about Corinth?"

Adrián nodded.

"That spiritual gifts didn't guarantee maturity."

"Yes."

"And that jealousy and strife were evidence of carnality."

"Exactly."

Matías looked down at Galatians.

"Now we have people being persuaded toward the Law, and Paul is warning them about some of the same things."

Marcos nodded.

"Yes."

Matías looked thoughtful.

"Then neither spiritual gifts nor religious obligation could guarantee the kind of life Paul expected."

Elías considered the statement.

"Not in themselves."

Adrián smiled.

"That's a rather uncomfortable discovery."

Matías looked at him.

"Tell me about it."

They laughed.

The waitress approached with another pot of coffee.

"More?"

Adrián looked at the others.

"Perhaps we should ask whether coffee can produce spiritual maturity."

Marcos smiled.

"Based on our experience, apparently not."

She filled their cups.

"Then perhaps you should try tea."

Adrián shook his head.

"Let's not become legalistic."

She laughed and moved on.

Matías watched her leave.

Then he turned back to his Bible.

"I've been thinking about something else Paul says."

Elías looked up.

"Where?"

"Galatians five."

He found verse sixteen.

*"But I say, walk by the Spirit, and you will not carry out the desire of the flesh."*

He paused.

"That's a remarkable promise."

Elías nodded.

"It is."

Matías looked at the others.

"Paul doesn't say to try harder to overcome the flesh."

"No," Elías replied.

"He says to walk by the Spirit."

"Yes."

Matías looked down at the passage.

"Then I want to know what that means."

Adrián smiled.

"So do I."

Marcos nodded.

"Because if we're going to tell people that the Spirit produces what the Law cannot, we ought to understand what Paul means."

Elías looked at the passage.

"Agreed."

Matías closed his Bible.

"Good."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Good?"

"Yes."

"Are we finished?"

"For today."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"Mark the date."

Marcos pretended to write something in his notebook.

Matías laughed.

Then he looked at Adrián.

"Thank you for bringing my Bibles."

"You're welcome."

"And for understanding."

Adrián smiled.

"I've had some practice."

Matías nodded.

"I suppose you have."

They gathered their things.

This time Matías made sure he had both Bibles.

Adrián noticed.

"Very good."

Matías looked at him.

"What?"

"You're learning obedience."

Matías laughed.

"Don't start."

They headed toward the door together.

The question that had troubled Matías had not disappeared.

He still believed Christians should pursue holiness.

He still believed obedience mattered.

Yet he was beginning to consider something he had rarely questioned before.

Perhaps the life God required was not produced merely by demanding it.

And perhaps the Spirit was not simply helping believers accomplish what they could otherwise do for themselves.

He wasn't certain how to explain it yet.

For once, he was content to leave the question open.

Matías waved goodbye and headed toward his car.

Adrián watched him go.

"Elías?"

"Yes?"

"I don't know why yet, but doesn't Matías seem different?"

Elías glanced toward the parking lot.

He watched Matías open his car door, then cracked a smile.

"I wonder what's going on inside him."

# Chapter 19

## An Unexpected Departure

The three men were already seated when Matías entered the diner, pulling a small suitcase behind him.

Adrián spotted him first.

"Well, look who's here!"

Matías approached the table.

Marcos glanced at the luggage.

"Where are you arriving from?"

"I'm not arriving."

Matías attempted a smile.

"I'm leaving."

Elías looked up.

"Leaving?"

"Argentina. My mother isn't doing well."

Adrián's expression changed.

"How bad?"

Matías hesitated.

"Bad."

He looked down for a moment.

"My sister called this morning. Mom's taken a turn for the worse. The doctors don't think she has much time."

"I'm sorry," Marcos said.

"Thank you."

Matías glanced at his watch.

"I can't stay. I have a flight to catch."

"Tonight?" Elías asked.

"In a few hours. I managed to get a seat."

Adrián looked at the suitcase.

"You're going straight to the airport?"

"Yes."

"How long will you be gone?"

Matías shook his head.

"I don't know."

The three men exchanged glances.

"Could be a few weeks," Matías continued. "Could be longer. Right now, I just want to get there."

His voice caught slightly on the last words.

Adrián stood.

"Of course."

Matías looked around the table.

"I know this is sudden. I just didn't want to leave without saying goodbye."

Marcos rose, followed by Elías.

"Don't worry about us," Marcos said. "Go be with your mother."

Matías nodded.

"Thank you."

Elías placed a hand on his shoulder.

"We'll be praying for her. And for you."

"I appreciate that."

Adrián looked at him.

"Do you need a ride to the airport?"

"No, I've arranged one. The driver's waiting outside."

"All right."

Matías looked at the three of them.

For a moment, he seemed to want to say something more.

Then he shook his head.

"Well, I suppose I'd better go."

Adrián stepped forward and embraced him.

Matías held on a moment longer than either expected.

Then Marcos embraced him, followed by Elías.

"Let us know how she's doing," Elías said.

"I will."

Matías picked up his suitcase.

At the door, he turned.

"And don't stop meeting because I'm gone."

Adrián smiled.

"We won't."

"Good."

Matías managed another small smile.

"I'll see you fellows when I get back."

Then he was gone.

The three men watched through the window as Matías approached the waiting car.

Just before getting in, he turned and looked back.

Adrián raised his hand to his ear, thumb and little finger extended, miming a telephone.

Matías nodded.

Then he climbed into the car.

A moment later, it pulled away.

Adrián remained standing.

"I hope he gets there in time."

Neither Marcos nor Elías answered.

Eventually they returned to their seats.

For a while, nobody said much.

Then Elías looked toward the window.

"My parents are getting older."

Adrián looked at him.

"Are they still in Bolivia?"

"Yes. Cochabamba. Both of them."

"How are they?"

"Well enough, thankfully. Although every time I visit, I notice they've aged a little more."

Marcos nodded.

"It's difficult being so far away."

"It is," Elías said. "Especially when something happens unexpectedly."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"My parents came here from Cali when I was young. I suppose I've always taken it for granted that they're nearby."

"Cali, Colombia?" Marcos asked.

"Yes."

"Do they still live close to you?"

"Not far. My mother complains if I go too long without visiting."

"How long is too long?" Elías asked.

"According to her? Three days."

They laughed.

"Your father too?" Marcos asked.

"He's more reasonable. He gives me four."

Elías smiled.

Marcos had grown quiet.

Adrián looked at him.

"What about your family, Marcos?"

"My father is still in Lima."

"Peru?"

"Yes."

"And your mother?"

"She passed away several years ago."

Adrián's expression softened.

"I'm sorry. I didn't know."

Marcos nodded.

"Thank you."

Elías looked at him.

"That must have been difficult."

"It was."

Marcos turned his coffee cup slowly.

"I was able to spend some time with her before she died. Not as much as I would have liked."

He paused.

"I don't think there's ever enough time."

Neither man interrupted.

Marcos looked toward the window.

"I'm glad Matías is going."

Adrián nodded.

"So am I."

The waitress approached with the coffee pot.

"Another round, gentlemen?"

Marcos held out his cup.

"Please."

She began pouring.

"Where's your fourth musketeer tonight?"

"Argentina," Adrián said.

She looked surprised.

"Really?"

"His mother's very ill. He went to be with her."

"Oh, I'm sorry to hear that."

"Thank you," Adrián said.

She glanced at the empty chair.

"He's a nice man."

"He is," Elías replied.

"Always puts his cup and saucer together when he's finished. Makes my job easier."

Adrián smiled.

"That sounds like Matías."

She looked around the table.

"Speaking of making my job easier, would you gentlemen mind moving your cups over a little? We've got a new system for clearing tables."

Marcos looked interested.

"A new system?"

"Yes. The owner's been making changes."

"Working well?"

She hesitated.

"Depends who you ask."

Adrián laughed.

"That sounds encouraging."

"Well, we're supposed to clear tables faster, reduce waste, and get orders out more efficiently."

"Those sound like good improvements," Marcos said.

"They are. In theory."

"In theory?"

She leaned slightly closer.

"Last week we ran out of clean teaspoons because someone decided we had too many."

Adrián burst out laughing.

Marcos smiled.

"That's not an efficiency improvement."

"Tell the owner."

"Did you?"

"Of course."

"And?"

"He ordered more teaspoons."

Elías laughed.

"So the system worked."

"Eventually."

She gathered a few empty plates.

"Anyway, business has been good. Apparently we're having our best year ever."

"That's excellent," Marcos said.

"It is."

She paused.

"Now if we could just get enough people working here to serve all those customers."

Marcos nodded.

"Growth creates its own challenges."

"You're telling me."

She headed back toward the counter.

Adrián watched her go.

"Well, there you have it. The dangers of organized religion."

Marcos laughed.

"Organized religion?"

"First the teaspoons disappear. Next thing you know, nobody's allowed to smile without filling out a form."

Elías shook his head.

"You're impossible."

Marcos leaned back.

"Actually, she's describing a management problem. The goals are reasonable. The implementation needs work."

Adrián smiled.

"Of course you'd say that."

"Why wouldn't I? Good organization helps people."

"Provided you have enough teaspoons."

"Even then."

Marcos reached into his jacket and took out a folded flyer.

"Speaking of organization, our church is hosting something next month."

Adrián looked suspicious.

"Does it involve teaspoons?"

"Not unless someone forgets to order them."

He unfolded the flyer and placed it on the table.

Elías leaned forward.

"What's this?"

"A community event. We've been working on it for months."

Adrián studied the flyer.

"That's quite a program."

"We're hoping to bring together families from across the area. Food, children's activities, health information, and several organizations offering practical assistance."

Elías nodded.

"That sounds worthwhile."

"It is. We've got volunteers from several churches, local businesses donating supplies, and community organizations helping with different parts of the day."

Marcos turned the flyer toward them.

"Several local leaders are involved too. They've been helping us connect with organizations we wouldn't ordinarily reach."

Adrián looked impressed.

"How many people are you expecting?"

"Several hundred, if everything comes together."

"Several hundred?"

"Possibly more."

Elías looked at the flyer.

"That's a considerable undertaking."

"It is. We have teams coordinating food, transportation, setup, children's activities, and volunteers."

Adrián smiled.

"And spreadsheets."

"Several."

"Of course."

Marcos laughed.

"Listen, when you've got hundreds of people arriving, good intentions aren't enough. Someone has to make sure the food arrives, the volunteers know where to go, and the children have a safe place to play."

Elías nodded.

"He's right."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"Are you enjoying this?"

"Immensely."

"I thought so."

Marcos smiled, then became more serious.

"We're hoping it will make a real difference. There are families in the community who need help, and churches have resources that could be put to good use."

Elías studied the flyer again.

"I like that you're working with other organizations."

"We've been building those relationships for years," Marcos replied. "People are beginning to trust us."

Adrián nodded.

"That's no small thing."

"No, it isn't."

Marcos folded the flyer.

"And none of this would happen without planning. We have a good team, responsible leadership, and people who follow through."

Elías smiled.

"You sound proud of them."

"I am."

"And you should be," Elías said.

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, gentlemen, perhaps we've finally found a church that has its act together."

Marcos smiled.

"I certainly hope so."

# Chapter 20

## A Remarkable Success

By Wednesday morning, Marcos had already answered seventeen messages, resolved two scheduling conflicts, and found a replacement speaker for a conference that was supposed to begin in forty minutes.

He was having an excellent week.

Community Impact Week had become the largest annual event his church organized. What had begun years earlier as a modest effort to offer practical assistance to local families had grown into a weeklong program involving professionals, businesses, community organizations, and civic leaders.

Every year, the church opened its doors to the public.

Financial advisers offered guidance on budgeting, debt, and retirement planning. Marriage and family counsellors presented workshops on relationships and communication. Career specialists helped people prepare résumés, develop interview skills, and explore employment opportunities.

Other sessions addressed health, parenting, education, and civic responsibilities. Local leaders participated in discussions about public services, community development, and the challenges facing families.

Several conferences ran simultaneously throughout the day.

The church had become remarkably good at organizing them.

Marcos had helped make that happen.

He knew the schedule, the budget, the participating organizations, and the names of most of the professionals involved.

He also knew exactly how many people had registered.

And this year, the numbers were extraordinary.

The church foyer was crowded.

Volunteers wearing matching badges directed visitors toward registration tables. Large screens displayed the day's schedule, while signs pointed toward the various conference rooms.

In one room, a financial adviser was explaining how families could get out of debt.

Down the hall, a marriage counsellor was preparing to speak about communication and conflict.

Another session offered practical guidance for people seeking employment.

In the largest auditorium, a panel of community leaders was discussing local services, public responsibilities, and opportunities for civic involvement.

Every room was busy.

And everything was running remarkably well.

Marcos checked his phone.

A message from the registration coordinator.

*We've passed last year's total attendance.*

He smiled.

Another message arrived.

*Additional seating needed in Conference Room B.*

He typed a quick reply.

*Open the adjoining room. I'll send two volunteers.*

Then he looked up as a woman approached him.

"Excuse me. Are you Marcos?"

"Yes."

"I just wanted to thank you."

He smiled.

"You're very welcome. Have you been enjoying the sessions?"

"Very much. My husband lost his job a few months ago, and we've been struggling. The financial workshop this morning was exactly what we needed."

"I'm glad to hear that."

"And someone helped him prepare his résumé yesterday. He has an interview next week."

"That's wonderful."

She smiled.

"We didn't know your church did things like this."

Marcos looked around the busy foyer.

"We've been doing it for several years. It keeps growing."

"Well, I hope you never stop."

"Thank you."

She returned to the crowd.

Marcos watched her go, pleased.

This was why he loved the event.

Real people.

Real needs.

Practical help.

He glanced at the attendance figures on his phone.

They were ahead of schedule, ahead of last year's numbers, and comfortably within their revised operating budget.

He couldn't have asked for much more.

Then his phone rang.

"Marcos? We've got a problem with the afternoon panel."

He smiled.

"Tell me."

And headed toward the auditorium.

The week hadn't begun nearly so smoothly.

Three weeks earlier, Marcos had been sitting in the church office, surrounded by schedules, invoices, registration reports, and a growing collection of coffee cups.

The city still hadn't approved one of the permits required for the outdoor activities.

He had submitted the application weeks earlier.

Now they wanted additional documentation.

Marcos stared at the email.

"Additional documentation?"

He picked up the telephone.

After several minutes on hold, he finally reached someone who could help.

"Yes, I'm calling about our permit application."

He gave the reference number.

"Everything was submitted last month."

He listened.

"Yes, I understand. The issue is that our event begins in three weeks."

Another pause.

"Of course. I'll send the revised site plan this afternoon."

He ended the call.

Then he opened his calendar and added another appointment.

Before he could return to the budget, someone knocked.

"Come in."

The building supervisor entered.

"Marcos, we've got a problem."

Marcos looked up.

"Another one?"

"The air conditioning."

"What about it?"

"It's gone."

"Gone where?"

The supervisor didn't smile.

"The main unit has failed."

Marcos leaned back.

"Can it be repaired?"

"We've called the contractor."

"How soon can he come?"

"This afternoon."

"Good."

The supervisor hesitated.

"He's already told me it may need more than a repair."

Marcos looked at him.

"How much more?"

"We won't know until he examines it."

Marcos glanced toward the conference rooms.

They were expecting hundreds of people. Some sessions would fill the larger rooms for hours at a time.

They couldn't have visitors sitting in uncomfortable rooms throughout the week.

"Tell him to come as soon as possible."

By late afternoon, the contractor had confirmed the worst.

The system needed a major overhaul.

The estimate was substantial.

Marcos studied the figures.

"You're certain?"

"Afraid so."

"And how quickly can you do it?"

"If we get authorization today, we can have it running before the event."

Marcos looked at the ceiling.

"Do it."

"Are you sure?"

"We've got hundreds of people coming. We can't have them sitting in conference rooms without air conditioning."

"I'll get started."

Marcos ended the call and reached for the budget.

The contingency fund would cover part of the expense.

The rest would have to come from somewhere else.

He began moving figures between columns.

Then his phone rang again.

"Marcos? It's the volunteer coordinator."

"Tell me you've got good news."

"I'm afraid not."

"What happened?"

"One of the band members was in a car accident yesterday."

"Is he all right?"

"He's in the hospital. They're expecting him to recover."

"Good."

Marcos rubbed his forehead.

"Have you found a replacement?"

"Not yet."

"Well, we need one. Friday's program is already advertised, and we're expecting a full house."

"I've started making calls."

"Try the musicians from last year's event. They were excellent."

"I'll contact them."

"And let me know as soon as you've confirmed someone."

"Of course."

There was a pause.

"Marcos?"

"Yes?"

"Should I send a card?"

"A card?"

"To the musician. The one who was in the accident."

"Oh. Oh, yeah. Of course."

"All right. I'll take care of it."

"Good. Thanks."

Marcos ended the call and returned to his schedule.

By the time he finished rearranging the evening program, it was nearly eight.

His wife called.

"Are you coming home?"

"Yes. Soon."

"You said that an hour ago."

"I know. I'm sorry."

"Have you eaten?"

He looked at the half-finished sandwich on his desk.

"More or less."

She laughed softly.

"What does that mean?"

"It means I'm almost finished."

"With the sandwich or the work?"

Marcos smiled.

"Both."

"I'll believe it when I see you."

"I won't be much longer."

He ended the call, picked up his pen, and returned to the schedule.

At nine-thirty, he finally turned off the office lights.

By Thursday of Community Impact Week, the air conditioning was working beautifully.

The city permits had been approved.

The replacement musicians had confirmed their appearance.

And registration had exceeded every projection.

Marcos stood near the entrance, reviewing the afternoon program, when he noticed a television camera being positioned beside one of the conference banners.

He approached the reporter.

"Everything going all right?"

"Very well, thank you. We're with CBC Windsor. We'd like to get a few more interviews before the afternoon sessions finish."

"Of course. Anything you need."

"We've already spoken with two of your financial advisers and a family counsellor. We'd like to hear from some of the people who've attended."

Marcos smiled.

"I think you'll find quite a few willing to talk."

The reporter glanced toward the crowded foyer.

"This is quite an undertaking. How many years have you been doing it?"

"It's our annual event. It started much smaller. Every year we've been able to expand."

"Well, the response certainly looks impressive."

"Thank you."

A woman approached with her husband.

The reporter introduced herself.

"Would you mind telling us what brought you here?"

The woman glanced at her husband.

"We've had a difficult year financially. We heard about the workshops and thought we'd come."

"Have they helped?"

"Very much. We met with someone who showed us how to organize our debts, and my husband attended the employment session."

Her husband nodded.

"I've already got an interview lined up."

"That's wonderful," the reporter said. "Would you be comfortable sharing that on camera?"

The couple agreed.

Marcos stepped aside as the camera operator prepared the shot.

A few minutes later, the reporter interviewed a young woman who had attended a family counselling presentation.

Then a retired businessman spoke about volunteering his professional experience.

Everywhere the camera turned, there was another encouraging story.

Marcos watched with satisfaction.

The church had worked hard to make this week possible.

And now the whole community would hear about it.

A volunteer approached.

"Marcos, the mayor's office has confirmed their representative for tomorrow evening."

"Excellent."

"And the newspaper wants photographs."

"Send them to the media coordinator."

"Already done."

Marcos smiled.

"You're making my job easy."

She laughed.

"That's a first."

His phone vibrated.

Another message.

He looked at the screen, answered it, and hurried toward the next conference room.

Friday evening's closing program drew another large crowd.

The replacement band performed beautifully.

The audience enjoyed the music, the speakers received enthusiastic applause, and the final sessions concluded without incident.

By the time the last visitors departed, Marcos could hardly believe the week was over.

He stood in the empty foyer, looking at the banners and registration tables.

A volunteer walked past carrying a box of leftover programs.

"Well, we did it."

Marcos smiled.

"We certainly did."

"Are you going home?"

"Eventually."

She laughed.

"You look exhausted."

"I am."

"Then go home."

"I've got a few things to finish."

"Of course you do."

She continued toward the storage room.

Marcos took out his phone.

There were still messages to answer, invoices to approve, and reports to prepare.

The appreciation banquet was scheduled for the following evening.

He had less than twenty-four hours to assemble the final presentation.

He opened his laptop.

Saturday evening, the church's banquet hall looked entirely different.

The conference tables had been removed, the equipment stored, and the rooms cleaned.

Now round tables covered with white linens filled the hall. Floral arrangements stood at their centres, and soft lighting illuminated the stage.

Community leaders, business owners, participating professionals, volunteers, and representatives from local organizations arrived in their evening clothes.

A large screen displayed the words:

**COMMUNITY IMPACT WEEK**

**ANNUAL APPRECIATION DINNER**

Adrián and Elías arrived together.

Adrián stopped just inside the entrance.

"Well, look at this."

Elías surveyed the room.

"Very impressive."

"Marcos certainly knows how to put on an event."

They found their assigned table near the front.

A few minutes later, Marcos approached them.

"Gentlemen!"

Adrián stood.

"Look at you!"

Marcos smiled.

"What?"

"You look like you own the place."

"Not quite."

"Are you sure? I've seen smaller weddings."

Elías shook Marcos's hand.

"Congratulations. We've been hearing wonderful things about the week."

"Thank you. It's been extraordinary."

Adrián studied his face.

"Have you slept?"

Marcos laughed.

"Some."

"Recently?"

"Enough."

"You look tired."

"I am."

"Are you all right?"

Marcos glanced around the banquet hall.

"Never better."

And he meant it.

He had worked harder than he could remember working in years.

There had been moments when he wondered whether everything would come together.

Now, looking around the room, he could see the results.

Every table was occupied.

The professionals were being recognized.

The sponsors had arrived.

The community leaders were present.

Everything was ready.

Marcos checked his watch.

"I'd better get backstage. We're starting in five minutes."

Adrián smiled.

"Of course you are."

"I'll see you afterward."

He hurried away.

Adrián watched him go.

"That man needs a vacation."

Elías smiled.

"I suspect he'd organize it."

"And issue everyone a schedule."

"Probably."

They laughed.

A few minutes later, the lights dimmed.

The master of ceremonies welcomed the guests and thanked them for attending.

Dinner was served.

Between courses, a short video played on the large screen.

Scenes from the week appeared: crowded conference rooms, professionals answering questions, volunteers assisting families, children participating in activities, and community leaders speaking with visitors.

There were photographs of the financial workshops, the employment sessions, the family conferences, and the evening programs.

Then came the testimonies.

A mother described how the parenting sessions had helped her understand her teenage daughter.

A man spoke about the assistance he'd received while searching for work.

A young couple explained how a financial adviser had helped them develop a plan for managing their debts.

The audience applauded each story.

Adrián leaned toward Elías.

"Those people really were helped."

"They were," Elías replied.

The video ended.

The master of ceremonies returned to the microphone.

"Ladies and gentlemen, it's now my pleasure to share the results of this year's Community Impact Week."

The screen changed.

**3,280 COMMUNITY PARTICIPANTS**

Applause filled the room.

"That's our largest attendance ever, representing a substantial increase over last year."

The next slide appeared.

**86 PARTICIPATING PROFESSIONALS**

"These men and women volunteered their knowledge, experience, and time to serve our community."

More applause.

**42 PARTNER ORGANIZATIONS**

"Businesses, community agencies, and other organizations worked alongside us to make this week possible."

Another slide.

**$186,400 RAISED**

The applause grew louder.

"Through sponsorships, contributions, and fundraising initiatives, we've raised more than enough to cover this year's expenses and strengthen our plans for the coming year."

Adrián looked at Elías.

"That's a lot of money."

Elías nodded.

"It certainly is."

The presentation continued.

**18 MEDIA FEATURES AND INTERVIEWS**

The master of ceremonies smiled.

"Our event received unprecedented attention this year, including coverage from CBC Windsor and other local media."

A brief clip appeared from the CBC interview.

The woman who had attended the financial workshop was speaking about the help she and her husband had received.

The audience applauded again.

Then the screen changed.

**147 NEW SEEKER SIGNUPS**

The master of ceremonies paused.

"And perhaps one of our most encouraging results: one hundred and forty-seven new seekers registered during the week!"

The room erupted in applause.

Adrián smiled.

"That's wonderful."

Elías nodded.

The next slide appeared.

**SEEKER ENGAGEMENT — NEXT YEAR'S VOLUNTEER OPPORTUNITIES**

The master of ceremonies continued.

"These are people who want to become involved in what we're doing. Many have already indicated which teams they'd like to join for next year's event."

Another round of applause followed.

Adrián looked at the screen again.

"Oh. They're volunteering."

Elías nodded.

"Apparently."

The presentation moved on.

There were more numbers.

Volunteer hours.

Conference attendance.

Community partnerships.

Financial projections.

Every category showed growth.

Every goal had been met or exceeded.

When the final slide appeared, the audience rose in a standing ovation.

Marcos was invited to the stage.

He approached the microphone to enthusiastic applause.

For a moment, he simply stood there, smiling.

"Thank you."

The applause continued.

"Thank you very much."

He waited until the room quieted.

"I wish I could tell you how much this week has meant to all of us."

He glanced toward the volunteers seated near the front.

"Some of you have worked for months preparing for this event. Others have given up evenings, weekends, and time with your families to make it possible."

He paused.

"We've had our challenges."

A few people laughed.

"Some of you know about the permits."

More laughter.

"And the air conditioning."

That brought a louder response.

"And our musicians, who graciously stepped in at the last minute."

He gestured toward the band.

The audience applauded.

"Through it all, you kept going. You found solutions. You worked together."

Marcos looked across the room.

"And tonight, we can see what that effort accomplished."

He pointed toward the screen.

"Thousands of people served. Dozens of professionals involved. New partnerships established. Resources raised for future programs."

He smiled.

"We set ambitious goals this year, and thanks to everyone in this room, we exceeded every one of them."

The applause returned.

Marcos waited.

"People in our community are beginning to recognize this church as a place where they can find help, encouragement, and practical solutions."

He glanced toward the tables where the community representatives were seated.

"We're grateful for the confidence you've placed in us."

Several guests nodded.

"And next year, we intend to do even more."

The room applauded enthusiastically.

Marcos stepped back from the microphone.

The master of ceremonies returned to thank him, and the audience rose once more.

At their table, Adrián and Elías stood with everyone else.

Adrián applauded vigorously.

Elías joined him.

Marcos looked toward them from the stage.

Adrián raised both thumbs.

Marcos laughed.

Later, as the guests began leaving, Marcos returned to their table.

"Well, gentlemen?"

Adrián smiled.

"What can I say?"

"That's a first," Elías remarked.

Marcos laughed.

"Did you enjoy yourselves?"

"Very much," Elías said. "It was an impressive evening."

"And the work you've done for the community is remarkable," Adrián added.

"Thank you."

Marcos pulled out a chair and sat down.

For the first time that evening, he seemed willing to stop moving.

He loosened his tie.

Adrián looked at him.

"Now there's a sight."

"What?"

"Marcos sitting down."

"Very funny."

"Should we call someone? Is there a procedure?"

Elías laughed.

Marcos shook his head.

"I suppose I deserve that."

"How long have you been working on this?" Elías asked.

"Months. The last few weeks have been especially demanding."

"I can imagine."

Marcos glanced toward the stage.

"Still, when you see the results, it's worth it."

Adrián nodded.

"You certainly accomplished a great deal."

"We did."

Marcos smiled.

"And the best part is that we're already positioned for next year. We have more volunteers, stronger partnerships, and additional funding."

"You're already thinking about next year?" Adrián asked.

"Of course."

Adrián looked at Elías.

"He's hopeless."

Marcos laughed.

"Someone has to plan ahead."

"Yes, yes. And someone has to make sure there are enough teaspoons."

Marcos stared at him.

Then he burst out laughing.

"I'd forgotten about that."

"I haven't."

Elías smiled.

"Neither has the waitress, probably."

Marcos leaned back.

For a few moments, the three friends sat together, enjoying the quiet that followed the evening's excitement.

Around them, volunteers began clearing tables.

A few guests lingered near the entrance, exchanging congratulations and making plans for future projects.

Marcos watched them with satisfaction.

"We've come a long way," he said.

Elías looked around the hall.

"You certainly have."

Marcos nodded.

"And we're only getting started."

Adrián raised his coffee cup.

"Well, here's to a successful week."

Elías lifted his cup.

"To everyone who worked so hard."

Marcos picked up his own.

"And to next year."

Their cups touched.

Marcos smiled.

He was exhausted.

His feet hurt, his phone was full of unanswered messages, and he could barely remember the last evening he'd spent at home without working on the event.

Yet as he looked around the banquet hall, he felt an immense satisfaction.

The church had set its goals.

It had mobilized its people.

It had served the community.

And it had exceeded every expectation.

For Marcos, it had been a remarkable success.

# Chapter 21

## In Need of Nothing

Marcos arrived at Deb's Place carrying a folder.

Adrián noticed it immediately.

"Oh, no."

Marcos looked at him.

"What?"

"More reports?"

"Good morning to you too."

Elías smiled as Marcos took his seat.

"Have you recovered from last week?"

"Mostly."

Adrián looked at the folder.

"Apparently not."

Marcos laughed.

"These are just a few things I wanted to show you."

"You showed us quite a few things Saturday night."

"These are different."

"Of course they are."

The waitress approached.

"Coffee, gentlemen?"

"Please," Marcos said.

She filled their cups and looked at the folder.

"Are you planning another conference?"

Adrián grinned.

"Don't encourage him."

Marcos smiled.

"Not today."

"Good. I haven't recovered from the last group you sent over here."

Marcos looked surprised.

"What group?"

"Some of your volunteers came in for lunch. Fourteen of them."

"Really?"

"All wanted separate bills."

Adrián burst out laughing.

"Fourteen separate bills?"

"Fourteen," she repeated. "And one of them wanted to know whether we offered a group discount."

Marcos shook his head.

"I apologize."

"Don't worry. They were lovely people."

She glanced at Adrián.

"Some customers are more trouble than others."

Adrián placed a hand over his heart.

"After all these years."

She smiled and headed toward the counter.

Marcos opened his folder.

"Anyway, I thought you might be interested in the final figures."

Adrián looked at Elías.

"He's unstoppable."

Elías laughed.

"Let him show us."

Marcos spread several pages across the table.

"We've finished reconciling the accounts. The financial results are even better than we reported at the banquet."

"Really?" Elías asked.

"Yes. A few additional contributions came in afterward, and several sponsors have already committed to next year."

Adrián studied one of the pages.

"That's impressive."

"And the registrations?" Elías asked.

"Still coming in. We've already had inquiries from professionals who want to participate next year."

Elías examined the report.

"You've developed quite a network."

"We have. And that makes a tremendous difference. When people know they can depend on you, they're willing to become involved."

Adrián nodded.

"That makes sense."

Marcos turned another page.

"We're also considering expanding the program. More sessions, additional community partners, perhaps another venue."

"Another venue?" Adrián asked.

"Eventually."

"Marcos, you just finished this one."

"Which is exactly when you should start planning the next."

Adrián sighed.

"Some people never learn."

Marcos smiled.

"Some people never plan."

Elías laughed.

"You're both quite consistent."

Marcos gathered the papers.

Elías watched him for a moment.

"You know, Marcos, your church has certainly shown me something."

"What's that?"

"That a church can become a real asset to its community. Not simply a place people visit on Sunday, but a place that makes a meaningful contribution to the lives of people around it."

Marcos smiled.

"That's what we've been working toward."

"And you've managed to do it on a considerable scale," Elías continued. "With good organization and responsible financial management."

Adrián nodded.

"I have to admit, I was impressed. Especially seeing how many people were helped."

"Thank you," Marcos said. "We've got a wonderful team."

"And apparently a very determined organizer," Adrián added.

Marcos laughed.

"Somebody has to keep things moving."

Elías looked at the folder.

"Your church must have a considerable number of professionals."

"Quite a few. Accountants, lawyers, counsellors, teachers, business owners, health professionals. People with years of experience."

"And they're willing to give their time?"

"Very willing. That's one of the things I'm most pleased about."

Elías smiled.

"It must be encouraging to see people using their abilities to help others."

"It is."

Marcos closed the folder.

"And we've got excellent leadership. Our finances are strong, our facilities are in good condition—especially now that we've replaced half the air-conditioning system—and our community relationships have never been better."

Adrián laughed.

"Well, there's the air conditioning again."

"Don't remind me."

"At least everyone stayed cool."

"Everyone except me."

They laughed.

Then Elías looked at the folder.

"Marcos, may I ask you something?"

"Of course."

"How do you evaluate the spiritual health of your church?"

Marcos considered the question.

"Well, we look at several things."

"Such as?"

"Participation. Giving. Volunteer involvement. Attendance. Whether people are engaged in the life of the church."

Elías nodded.

"And what else?"

"Leadership development. Community impact. Financial stewardship. We review all those things."

Adrián stirred his coffee.

"Those are the same things you measured at the event."

"Some of them, yes."

"Do you measure anything else?"

Marcos looked at him.

"Like what?"

Adrián hesitated.

"I don't know exactly."

Marcos smiled.

"That's the problem with questions like that. Everyone wants a healthy church, although people don't always agree on what that means."

"Fair enough," Elías said.

Marcos continued.

"At least with these things, we can see whether we're making progress."

Elías rested his hands on the table.

"Progress toward what?"

Marcos looked surprised.

"Toward becoming a stronger church."

"In what sense?"

"More effective. Better equipped to serve people. Able to accomplish more."

Elías nodded.

"And those are worthwhile goals."

"Certainly."

"Then perhaps we should distinguish two questions."

Marcos leaned back.

"Go ahead."

"How effectively is the church serving its community? And what is the church's spiritual condition before Christ?"

Marcos considered that.

"Those aren't unrelated."

"No," Elías said. "They needn't be. A church's service may express genuine love, generosity, and faithfulness."

"Exactly."

"Yet the number of people helped tells us how many people were helped. It doesn't, by itself, establish everything about the spiritual condition of those doing the helping."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"Your financial workshops helped people."

"They did."

"That's a fact."

"Yes."

"And whether the people conducting those workshops were walking in the Spirit is another question."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Yes. I suppose it is."

Elías smiled.

"The first question doesn't become unimportant because we're asking the second."

Marcos nodded.

"I appreciate that."

Adrián leaned back.

"So how do we answer the second question?"

Elías reached for his Bible.

"Perhaps we should hear what Christ says about a church that considered itself to be doing very well."

Marcos smiled.

"I thought that question was going somewhere."

"Laodicea."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Ah. Our next candidate."

Marcos laughed.

"Candidate for what?"

Adrián looked at him.

"At this point, I'm afraid to say."

Elías opened Revelation.

"Chapter three, beginning at verse fourteen."

Marcos opened his Bible as well.

Adrián followed.

Elías began reading.

"'And to the angel of the church of the Laodiceans write: These things says the Amen, the Faithful and True Witness, the Beginning of the creation of God.'"

He paused.

"Notice how Christ introduces Himself."

Marcos looked at the verse.

"'The Faithful and True Witness.'"

"Yes."

"Why is that significant?"

"Because we're about to hear two assessments of the same church. One comes from the church itself. The other comes from Christ."

Adrián smiled.

"And I suppose we know which witness to believe."

Elías nodded.

"Exactly."

He continued.

"'I know your works, that you are neither cold nor hot. I could wish you were cold or hot.'"

Adrián frowned.

"That's an unusual thing to say."

"It is."

"'So then, because you are lukewarm, and neither cold nor hot, I will vomit you out of My mouth.'"

Adrián looked up.

"That's rather strong."

"Very strong," Elías agreed.

Marcos studied the passage.

"What's He referring to? Their commitment?"

"Possibly. The temperature imagery has been interpreted in different ways. We should be careful about claiming more than the passage establishes."

Marcos nodded.

"Although His judgment of their condition is unmistakable."

"Yes."

Elías continued.

"'Because you say, I am rich, have become wealthy, and have need of nothing—'"

Marcos looked up.

"Interesting."

"What is?"

"They're wealthy."

"That's what they say."

"Yes, although being wealthy isn't necessarily wrong."

"No," Elías replied. "The passage doesn't condemn wealth itself."

Adrián leaned closer.

"Read the rest."

Elías continued.

"'And do not know that you are wretched, miserable, poor, blind, and naked.'"

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Marcos looked back at the verse.

"That's quite a difference."

"Between what?" Adrián asked.

"Between what they say about themselves and what Christ says about them."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

Marcos read the verse again.

"'I am rich, have become wealthy, and have need of nothing.'"

He paused.

"And then Christ says they don't know their actual condition."

Adrián leaned back.

"So they thought they were doing well."

"Apparently," Elías said.

"And Christ disagreed."

"Rather dramatically."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"I wonder what made them think they were doing well."

Elías turned toward him.

"That's a good question."

"Was it their wealth? Their activities? Their influence?"

"We aren't told all the reasons behind their assessment."

Marcos nodded.

"Then we should be careful about assuming too much."

"Agreed."

Adrián smiled.

"Listen to you two. We've barely started, and you're already arguing about what we don't know."

Elías laughed.

"That's usually where good interpretation begins."

"With an argument?"

"With recognizing what the text actually says."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"Well, it certainly says they considered themselves wealthy and in need of nothing."

"Yes."

"And it says Christ saw them very differently."

"Exactly."

Marcos sat quietly for a moment.

Then he looked at Elías.

"Here's what I don't understand."

"Go ahead."

"If they were wealthy, perhaps they really did have resources. And if they had resources, they could accomplish things that poorer churches couldn't."

"Certainly."

"So why would Christ describe them as poor?"

Elías turned to verse eighteen.

"Let's see what Christ says they need."

He read.

"'I counsel you to buy from Me gold refined in the fire, that you may be rich; and white garments, that you may be clothed, that the shame of your nakedness may not be revealed; and anoint your eyes with eye salve, that you may see.'"

Adrián looked up.

"Buy from Me."

"Yes," Elías said.

"Christ is telling them they need something from Him."

Marcos nodded.

"Something they apparently didn't realize they lacked."

Elías pointed to the verse.

"And notice how His counsel answers His assessment."

He looked at Adrián.

"What does He say about their wealth?"

"That they're poor."

"And what does He offer?"

"Gold refined in the fire."

"Why?"

Adrián glanced at the verse.

"'That you may be rich.'"

Elías nodded.

"Now their clothing."

Marcos answered.

"He says they're naked, and He offers white garments."

"And their sight?"

"They're blind," Adrián said. "He tells them to obtain eye salve so they can see."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"Then Christ is speaking about a kind of wealth their possessions couldn't provide."

"Yes," Elías said.

"And a condition their possessions couldn't correct."

"Exactly."

Adrián glanced at Marcos's folder.

"That's different from asking whether the church can afford another building."

Marcos smiled faintly.

"Considerably."

Elías continued.

"Think about what the church claimed. It had become wealthy. It needed nothing."

Marcos nodded.

"And Christ says it needs riches, clothing, and sight."

"From Him," Adrián added.

Elías looked at him.

"That's the essential point."

Marcos considered the words.

"Then the spiritual question isn't whether the church has enough resources."

"No."

"Or whether its programs succeed."

"No. Those questions have their place."

Marcos looked at the verse.

"The question is whether it possesses what Christ says it needs."

Elías nodded.

"And whether it recognizes its dependence upon Him."

Marcos sat back.

"That makes the distinction much clearer."

Adrián looked at him.

"Does it?"

"Yes. We can have enough money to fund every program we've planned and still need something money cannot provide."

"Precisely," Elías said.

"And we could have very little money and still possess what Christ values."

"Certainly."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Then neither prosperity nor poverty settles the question."

"No," Elías said. "Christ's assessment does."

The waitress approached.

"Everything all right, gentlemen?"

Adrián looked up.

"Yes, thank you."

"More coffee?"

"Please."

She filled their cups.

Then she noticed the open Bibles.

"Back to studying?"

Elías smiled.

"Yes."

"Well, don't let me interrupt."

She turned toward Marcos.

"And congratulations on your big event. I saw something about it on the news."

Marcos brightened.

"Thank you."

"It looked wonderful."

"We were very pleased with how it turned out."

"You should be. It's nice seeing a church do something for the community."

"That's what we're hoping to accomplish."

She smiled.

"Well, keep it up."

As she walked away, Marcos watched her for a moment.

Then he turned back to his Bible.

Adrián looked at him.

"That must be encouraging."

"It is."

"People really appreciate what your church is doing."

Marcos nodded.

"They do."

Elías took a sip of coffee.

"And rightly so."

Marcos looked at him.

"I know."

Elías gestured toward the folder.

"Those reports tell you something important about the work you've done."

"Yes."

"And the gratitude of the people you've helped is genuine."

"Yes."

"Now compare that with what Christ says about Laodicea."

Marcos looked at Revelation.

"He isn't evaluating their public reputation."

"That's right."

"Or how impressed other people are with them."

"No."

"He's telling them their condition before Him."

Elías nodded.

Marcos looked from the Bible to his folder.

"I've never really separated those things."

Adrián looked at him.

"What things?"

"The good a church accomplishes and the spiritual condition of the church itself."

He paused.

"I suppose I've always assumed that if a church was accomplishing good things, it must be spiritually healthy."

Elías considered that.

"Good works can certainly be evidence of spiritual life. Scripture doesn't ask us to disregard them."

"Of course."

"Yet the works themselves have to be understood in relation to Christ. They cannot replace His judgment of the people doing them."

Marcos nodded slowly.

"Yes."

He looked down at the passage again.

"And Laodicea was wrong about itself."

"According to Christ, profoundly wrong."

Marcos was quiet.

Then he asked, "What else does Christ require of them?"

Elías turned to verse nineteen.

"'As many as I love, I rebuke and chasten. Therefore be zealous and repent.'"

Adrián looked surprised.

"He loves them."

"Yes."

"Even after everything He just said?"

Elías nodded.

"That's what the verse says."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"Then this isn't simply a declaration that He's finished with them."

"No. He's calling them to respond."

"And repentance is part of that response."

"Explicitly."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Repent of what?"

Elías looked at the preceding verses.

"Of the condition Christ has exposed. Their self-sufficiency, their blindness to their need, and whatever lies behind His description of their works."

Marcos nodded.

"So He isn't telling them to become more impressive."

"No."

"He's telling them to recognize their condition and turn to Him."

"Yes."

Adrián looked at the words again.

"'As many as I love.'"

He smiled faintly.

"That's encouraging."

Elías continued.

"'Behold, I stand at the door and knock. If anyone hears My voice and opens the door, I will come in to him and dine with him, and he with Me.'"

Adrián stared at the verse.

"Wait a minute."

Elías looked up.

"What?"

"He's speaking to a church."

"Yes."

"And He's standing at the door?"

Elías glanced down at the passage.

"That's the image."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"That's a strange picture."

Marcos frowned.

"Why?"

"Well, if it's His church, why is He knocking?"

Elías considered the question.

"We should be careful not to make the image say more than it does. The invitation is addressed to anyone who hears His voice and opens."

"Still," Adrián said, "it's striking."

"It is."

Marcos read the verse again.

"'I will come in to him and dine with him, and he with Me.'"

He paused.

"That's rather personal."

"Very," Elías said.

Adrián looked at the verse.

"He doesn't offer them a better reputation."

"No."

"Or a larger congregation."

"No."

"He offers them fellowship with Himself."

Elías nodded.

"That's what the invitation promises."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"Then the letter isn't simply about a church needing to perform better."

"No," Elías said. "Christ calls them to repentance and fellowship with Himself."

Marcos looked at the Bible.

"Spiritual riches from Him. Sight. Repentance. Fellowship."

Adrián smiled.

"Those are rather different from your annual goals."

Marcos laughed.

"Considerably."

Then he looked at his folder.

"I suppose we could meet every target we've set for next year and still have to answer those questions."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

Marcos sat quietly.

Then he looked up.

"Although I have to say, I find this difficult."

"What do you mean?" Adrián asked.

"Look at what we've accomplished. Thousands of people received help. Families who were struggling financially found practical guidance. People looking for work got assistance. Professionals gave their time. We brought together organizations that had never worked with us before."

He looked at Elías.

"Those are good things."

"They certainly are."

"And I don't think we should minimize them."

"I don't either."

Marcos leaned back.

"Then I suppose I'm having trouble understanding what we're supposed to do with a passage like this."

Elías waited.

"How does a church know whether it's doing well?"

Adrián looked at the open folder.

"You've got plenty of ways to measure that."

"Exactly. And there's nothing wrong with measuring things."

"No," Adrián said.

Marcos turned toward Elías.

"Suppose I came here this morning and told you our attendance had doubled, our finances were healthy, and our community programs were helping thousands of people. Wouldn't you consider that encouraging?"

"Of course."

"And suppose I told you the opposite. Attendance was collapsing, we couldn't pay our bills, our programs were failing, and nobody wanted to volunteer. Wouldn't you be concerned?"

"Certainly."

"Then those things must tell us something."

"They do," Elías replied.

Marcos spread his hands.

"Well, there you are."

Adrián smiled.

"That sounded suspiciously like a conclusion."

Marcos laughed.

"Perhaps it was."

Elías looked at the passage again.

"Marcos, suppose your event had been poorly organized."

Marcos frowned.

"All right."

"Suppose the workshops hadn't helped anyone, the finances had been mismanaged, and the volunteers hadn't known what to do."

"That would have been a failure."

"Yes. A failure of the event."

"Certainly."

"Now suppose everything went exactly as it did. Thousands helped. Excellent organization. Responsible finances."

"All right."

"Then the event was successful."

"Of course."

Elías nodded.

"Both judgments are legitimate. We're evaluating the event according to what it was intended to accomplish."

Marcos waited.

"Now consider Laodicea. Christ isn't asking whether its resources were adequate for its activities. He's addressing its spiritual condition."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"Yes."

"And His criteria are different."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Then we could say the event succeeded without claiming that its success proves the church is spiritually healthy."

"Exactly," Elías said.

Marcos looked at his folder.

"That's a distinction I haven't given enough thought to."

He paused.

"Although it still leaves a difficult question."

"What question?" Elías asked.

"How do we know what Christ thinks of our church?"

Elías looked down at Revelation.

"We begin by listening to what He has said."

Marcos nodded.

"And comparing our assumptions with His assessment."

"Yes."

Marcos studied the words.

*And do not know that you are wretched, miserable, poor, blind, and naked.*

He frowned.

"That's what bothers me."

Adrián looked at him.

"What does?"

"They didn't know."

Neither man answered.

Marcos tapped the page.

"If they knew they were poor, they could ask for what they lacked. If they knew they were blind, they could seek sight."

He looked up.

"How do you correct something you don't even recognize?"

Elías considered the question.

"In this case, Christ tells them."

"Yes. He does."

"And He directs them to Himself."

Marcos glanced at his folder.

"I can tell you exactly how much money we raised last week. I can tell you how many professionals participated, how many people attended, how many volunteers signed up for next year."

He rested his hand on the folder.

"I can tell you what worked and what didn't. I can even tell you what we need to improve."

He looked at Elías.

"But none of that tells me whether we possess what Christ says a church needs."

Elías nodded.

"No."

Marcos looked back at the passage.

"How would I know if Christ saw something entirely different?"

Elías opened his mouth, then closed it.

For a moment, he studied the words.

"I don't think I can answer that as easily as I'd like."

Adrián looked from one man to the other.

"That's a little unsettling."

Marcos gave a short laugh.

"You're telling me."

The waitress appeared beside the table.

"Anything else for you gentlemen?"

Marcos looked up.

"No, thank you. Just the bill."

"Together or separate?"

"Together," Marcos said.

Adrián smiled.

"Careful. He'll want an itemized report."

She laughed.

"I can give him one."

"Please don't," Elías said.

She left them smiling.

Marcos gathered his papers and returned them to the folder.

He was careful to put them in order.

Elías closed his Bible.

Adrián reached for his coat.

For a moment, Marcos remained seated, looking at the folder.

Then he opened it again.

"There's one thing I want to show you."

Adrián sat back down.

"What now?"

Marcos found the page he wanted.

"This is our projection for next year. We've already secured several commitments, and if the current trend continues, we could reach nearly five thousand participants."

Adrián whistled.

"That's quite an increase."

"It is."

Elías leaned over to examine the figures.

"Ambitious."

"Achievable," Marcos replied.

He pointed to another column.

"And this is the funding we've already secured."

Elías nodded.

"You've certainly planned carefully."

Marcos smiled.

"That's what I keep telling you."

He closed the folder.

The waitress brought the bill.

Marcos paid it, and the three men stood.

As they headed toward the door, Adrián looked at him.

"So what's next? Another week of conferences?"

"Not immediately."

"Good."

"We have a planning meeting on Monday."

Adrián stopped.

"Monday?"

Marcos smiled.

"There's a lot to prepare."

"Marcos, you haven't even recovered from this year."

"I'll recover."

Elías laughed.

Outside, they paused beside their cars.

"Well, gentlemen," Marcos said, "I suppose we'll have more to discuss next time."

"I think so," Elías replied.

Adrián looked at him.

"Are we finished with Laodicea?"

Elías shook his head.

"Not yet."

Marcos smiled.

"I didn't think so."

They said goodbye.

Marcos climbed into his car and placed the folder on the passenger seat.

His phone vibrated.

A message from the church office.

*Excellent news. Two more sponsors confirmed for next year.*

He smiled and typed a reply.

*Wonderful. Add them to Monday's agenda.*

He set the phone down.

Then he noticed his Bible lying beneath the folder.

He pulled it out and opened it to Revelation.

His eyes found the words again.

*Because you say, I am rich, have become wealthy, and have need of nothing.*

He sat for a moment.

Then he looked at the folder beside him.

He knew what was in it.

He had checked every figure himself.

And he was certain the figures were correct.

The figures told him that the event had succeeded.

They did not tell him whether the church was rich toward Christ, whether it could see its own condition, or whether it was living in fellowship with Him.

He looked back at the Bible.

*And do not know...*

His phone vibrated again.

Marcos glanced at the screen.

Another message from the planning committee.

He picked it up.

Then he put it down.

For once, he couldn't think of what to do next.

# Chapter 22

## The Fourth Chair

The bell above the door jingled as Adrián entered Deb's Place.

He shook the cold from his jacket and made his way toward their usual table.

Elías was already there.

So was Marcos.

Adrián stopped beside them.

"Good afternoon, gentlemen."

Elías looked up.

"Adrián."

Marcos smiled.

"You're late."

"Three minutes."

"Five."

"You're becoming Matías."

Elías laughed.

"Somebody has to keep you accountable."

Adrián pulled out his chair, glanced at the table, and frowned.

"Wait a minute."

Marcos looked at him.

"What?"

"Where's your folder?"

"At the office."

Adrián stared at him.

"Your folder?"

"Yes."

"The one with all the reports?"

"That's the one."

"And you left it at the office?"

Marcos took a sip of coffee.

"Apparently."

Adrián turned toward Elías.

"Should we call someone?"

Elías smiled.

"Perhaps he's recovering."

"Or perhaps the folder finally surrendered."

Marcos laughed.

"Are you two going to do this every time we meet?"

"Only until you bring it back."

A cup clattered against a saucer at the counter.

"Sorry, Deb!" someone called.

"You're all right!" came the answer. "The cup survived!"

Their waitress approached with a fresh mug.

"Coffee, Adrián?"

"Please."

She filled it and glanced toward the empty chair.

"Still missing your fourth musketeer?"

Elías nodded.

"He's in Argentina."

"I remember. How's his mother?"

"Very ill, last we heard."

Her expression softened.

"That's difficult."

"Yes."

"Well, I hope he's doing all right."

She gave a small nod and moved toward the next table.

Adrián wrapped his hands around the mug.

"Any word from Matías?"

Elías shook his head.

"Nothing new."

Adrián looked at the empty chair.

"Doesn't seem right without him."

"No," Elías said.

For a moment, the three men sat quietly.

Then Adrián turned toward Marcos.

"How are you doing?"

"Fine."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Fine?"

"Yes."

"That's a very short answer."

Marcos smiled faintly.

"Would you prefer a report?"

"Now there's the Marcos I know."

Elías laughed.

Marcos looked down at his coffee.

Adrián watched him.

"Still thinking about Laodicea?"

Marcos nodded.

"Yes."

"Have you made any progress?"

Marcos didn't answer immediately.

Then he reached for his Bible.

"I've been reading it every day."

Elías looked interested.

"Revelation three?"

"Yes."

Marcos opened the Bible.

The pages made a soft rustling sound.

He found the passage quickly.

"I've been trying to understand something."

"What?" Elías asked.

"How a church could be so completely mistaken about itself."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Well, they thought they were rich."

"Yes."

"And Christ said they were poor."

Marcos nodded.

"Poor. Blind. Naked."

He paused.

"Wretched and miserable."

Elías said nothing.

Marcos looked up.

"Those aren't mild words."

"No," Elías replied.

"They're humiliating."

Adrián looked at him.

"Humiliating?"

"Imagine being told you're naked when you've been showing everyone how well dressed you are."
