“He's questioned a position the church requires its teachers to affirm. He knows the church's expectations. And he's continued questioning the interpretation after being instructed.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“So from the elders' perspective, he really has crossed a boundary.”

“Yes.”

“And they have a responsibility to maintain that boundary.”

“That's how they understand their responsibility.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Then perhaps we should be careful about blaming them.”

Elías looked at him.

“I don't blame them.”

The others waited.

“I know Harold and Peter. They're good guys. I remember enjoying their teaching.”

He paused.

“They're doing exactly what they've been taught faithful elders are supposed to do.”

Matías considered that.

“Then perhaps the problem isn't that they're failing to follow their instructions.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“Perhaps we need to examine the instructions.”

Elías looked down at his Bible.

He had spent years teaching those instructions.

And he wasn't certain where to begin.

Marcos pulled a paper napkin toward him.

“Let's think about how a church gets into a situation like this.”

He drew a rectangle.

Adrián leaned over.

“That's a terrible rectangle.”

“It's not an art competition.”

“Fortunately.”

Marcos smiled and continued.

“Suppose a congregation studies Scripture and reaches certain conclusions.”

Elías nodded.

“That's normal.”

“They write those conclusions into a doctrinal statement.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“To clarify what they believe, provide consistency in teaching, and protect the congregation from error.”

“Good reasons?”

“Certainly.”

Marcos tapped the rectangle.

“So this represents the church's established teaching.”

Adrián examined it.

“A very square church.”

Matías laughed.

Marcos ignored him.

“Now someone comes along who has been studying Scripture and notices something that doesn't seem to fit one of those conclusions.”

He drew a small circle beside the rectangle.

“Daniel,” Matías said.

“Exactly.”

Adrián pointed at the circle.

“He's very round.”

“Thank you, Adrián.”

“My pleasure.”

Marcos continued.

“Daniel asks whether the interpretation actually comes from the passage.”

Elías nodded.

“And the elders explain the established position.”

“Which is reasonable,” Matías said.

“Yes,” Elías replied.

“Yet Daniel remains unconvinced.”

“Right.”

Marcos set down his pen.

“Now what?”

Elías looked at the napkin.

“The elders have to decide whether he can continue teaching.”

“Because?”

“Because teachers are expected to affirm the church's doctrine.”

“And if Daniel continues questioning it?”

Elías was quiet.

“They have to enforce the boundary.”

Matías leaned forward.

“Otherwise the statement no longer functions the way it was intended.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián looked at the drawing.

“So the elders aren't suddenly doing something different from what they've always done.”

“No,” Elías said. “They're being consistent.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's what troubles me.”

Elías looked up.

“Why?”

“Because the statement was created to protect the congregation.”

He pointed at the rectangle.

“Now the congregation has to protect the statement.”

Matías considered that.

“And if someone won't agree with it?”

Marcos pointed toward the circle.

“He may have to go.”

Nobody spoke.

Adrián looked at the napkin for a long moment.

“Wow.”

He pushed it away slightly.

“I don't like that drawing anymore.”

Neither did Elías.

Anna brought their breakfasts.

For a while they ate.

Elías watched his friends talking and laughing about something that had happened during the week.

He was grateful for the interruption.

The conversation was difficult because he understood the elders' reasoning so well.

He had helped establish some of the church's teaching materials.

He had defended its positions.

He had explained to younger believers why doctrinal boundaries mattered.

He still believed they mattered.

Marcos returned to the subject when Anna had gone.

“There's another part of this.”

Elías looked up.

“Go ahead.”

“You said the elders were relieved after Daniel left.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“They believed they'd made the seriousness of the matter clear.”

“And what did they hope would happen?”

“That Daniel would stop questioning the doctrine.”

“Anything else?”

Elías hesitated.

“They hoped the other teachers would understand that the matter wasn't open for debate.”

Matías put down his fork.

“So Daniel becomes an example.”

Elías nodded.

“One elder actually used those words.”

Adrián looked up sharply.

“He did?”

“Yes. He said sometimes you have to make an example of someone to protect the rest of the congregation.”

Adrián sat back.

He remembered Daniel at the retreat, sitting with his Bible open, excited about a passage in Romans.

“An example,” he repeated quietly.

Marcos looked at Elías.

“What would the elders consider a successful outcome?”

“If Daniel accepts the doctrine, the matter is resolved.”

“And if he doesn't?”

“They may remove him.”

Matías frowned.

“And the other teachers?”

“They'll know the consequences of raising similar questions.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“So either way, the questions stop.”

Elías looked at him.

“Yes.”

Adrián shook his head.

“Then they win.”

Nobody answered.

He looked around the table.

“They get what they wanted. Nobody challenges the doctrine anymore.”

Matías considered that.

“Or nobody challenges it where the leaders can hear.”

Elías lowered his eyes.

He remembered Peter's satisfaction after the meeting.

*I suspect the others will think twice.*

He had understood the remark at the time.

He had even understood why Peter found it reassuring.

Now he was beginning to hear it differently.

Marcos spoke quietly.

“Imagine someone else in that congregation studying First John.”

Adrián nodded.

“He notices the same question.”

“What does he do?”

Matías answered.

“Perhaps he keeps it to himself.”

“And the elders never hear about it,” Marcos said.

Elías looked up.

“They may believe everyone agrees.”

“Exactly.”

Adrián stared at his coffee.

“A whole church can appear united because nobody wants to ask.”

Elías was quiet.

He remembered meetings from years earlier.

Questions raised.

Arguments presented.

Conclusions reaffirmed.

He remembered how relieved he had been when the discussions ended.

He had assumed the people involved were satisfied.

He couldn't remember asking.

“I used to think that way,” he said.

The others looked at him.

“I remember feeling threatened when someone questioned a doctrine I'd spent years teaching.”

Matías waited.

“I thought I was concerned about the congregation. And I was.”

Elías paused.

“Yet I also remember wanting the questions to stop.”

Marcos nodded.

“Because you believed the teaching was important.”

“Yes.”

“And you didn't want people confused.”

“No.”

Elías looked at him.

“That was part of it.”

He glanced down at the napkin.

“I wonder how often I mistook silence for agreement.”

Nobody tried to answer.

Matías reached for his Bible.

“Can I turn the question around?”

Adrián smiled.

“Please do.”

“What if the elders didn't believe their interpretation needed to be protected from further examination?”

Elías looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“Suppose Daniel came to them and said, ‘I'm struggling with First John 1:9. I'm not sure our explanation comes from the passage.’”

Marcos nodded.

“And suppose they answered, ‘Show us what you're seeing.’”

Adrián smiled.

“Now that's a different meeting.”

Matías continued.

“They could explain why they've understood it the way they have. Daniel could show them what's troubling him. They could examine the passage together.”

Elías considered it.

“They might still disagree.”

“Of course.”

“And Daniel might be wrong.”

“Certainly.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Yet his question wouldn't automatically become a disciplinary issue.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“No.”

Adrián looked delighted.

“He could keep studying!”

Matías laughed.

“Yes, Adrián.”

“That's what he wanted to do in the first place!”

Elías smiled.

*I'd like to keep studying it.*

He could still hear Daniel's voice.

Marcos looked at the napkin again.

“Perhaps the issue isn't that the church has reached a conclusion.”

Elías nodded.

“Go on.”

“Perhaps the problem begins when the conclusion becomes something the leaders must protect from further examination.”

Matías considered that.

“Then a question feels like a threat.”

“Yes.”

“And the person asking it becomes a problem.”

Elías looked down.

He knew that feeling.

He had experienced it many times.

He remembered someone questioning a familiar interpretation and the immediate uneasiness that followed.

He had thought the uneasiness meant he was alert to danger.

Now he wondered whether some of it had come from somewhere else.

Adrián opened his Bible to Acts seventeen.

“Here's what I keep coming back to.”

He read about the Bereans receiving the word with readiness and examining the Scriptures daily to see whether the things they heard were so.

He looked up.

“They examined Paul's teaching.”

Matías smiled.

“The apostle Paul.”

“Yes!”

Marcos leaned forward.

“And Luke calls them noble for doing it.”

Elías nodded.

He had taught the passage many times.

Adrián looked at him.

“Daniel is doing that.”

“He's trying to,” Elías replied.

“He hasn't said he's reached a final conclusion.”

“No.”

“He hasn't demanded that the church accept his interpretation.”

“No.”

“He's asking whether the passage actually teaches what they've been saying it teaches.”

Elías nodded.

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Of course, being willing to ask questions doesn't mean every conclusion is correct.”

“Exactly,” Elías said.

“And someone can misuse questions to avoid accepting what Scripture plainly teaches.”

“Yes.”

Marcos added, “And leaders must still protect the congregation from genuinely destructive teaching.”

Elías nodded.

“Absolutely.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Then we need to know what the person is actually saying.”

“And whether he's willing to listen,” Matías added.

“And whether the concern is about Scripture or agreement with an established position,” Marcos said.

Elías looked at his friends.

Nobody was suggesting that truth didn't matter.

Nobody was asking him to abandon careful interpretation.

They were taking Scripture seriously.

And nobody seemed afraid of discovering that an interpretation might need correction.

He found that refreshing.

Marcos looked again at the napkin.

“There's something else about this drawing.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Besides the obvious artistic deficiencies?”

Marcos smiled.

“Yes.”

He pointed at the rectangle.

“Where are the elders?”

“Inside,” Matías said.

“And the congregation?”

“Inside.”

“And Daniel?”

Adrián pointed to the little circle.

“Out here.”

Marcos nodded.

“Now here's what interests me. The elders aren't free to step outside the rectangle either.”

Elías looked up.

“What do you mean?”

“They're required to defend the same boundaries they're enforcing.”

Matías considered that.

“So if they discover something in Scripture that challenges an established position, they have a problem too.”

Elías looked at the drawing.

“Yes,” he said slowly. “They would.”

Adrián studied the rectangle.

“You know what's interesting?”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“We've always talked about churches getting trapped inside their four walls.”

Matías nodded.

Adrián pointed at the napkin.

“The worst thing the church can do is not enclose itself within four walls, but enclose its thinking within the boundaries of its own interpretation.”

Elías stared at the drawing.

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then he began to smile.

“You know something?”

Marcos looked up.

“What?”

“I never thought of that.”

Adrián grinned.

“Neither did I until just now!”

Elías laughed.

He looked again at the rectangle.

For years he had helped people understand the boundaries of their church's doctrine.

He had never considered that those same boundaries might prevent them from discovering something Scripture was saying.

And the remarkable thing was that he didn't feel compelled to defend them.

He wanted to understand.

“That's an extraordinary thought,” he said.

Matías smiled.

“Isn't it?”

Elías nodded.

“I think we need to look at that more carefully.”

“Excellent!” Adrián said. “Another question!”

And they laughed.

Matías turned to Ephesians four.

“There's something else.”

He read about Christ giving apostles, prophets, evangelists, pastors, and teachers for the equipping of the saints, for the work of ministry, and for the building up of the body of Christ.

Marcos leaned forward.

“Read the purpose again.”

Matías did.

Elías followed along.

He knew the passage well.

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“So Christ gives these people to the church.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And they're there to equip the saints.”

Marcos nodded.

“Not to make the saints dependent upon their ability to answer every question.”

Elías looked up.

Matías continued reading about growing into maturity, no longer being children tossed about by every wind of doctrine.

“That's important,” Elías said.

The others looked at him.

“The passage doesn't dismiss the danger of false teaching. It specifically addresses it.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

“Yet the answer involves the body growing into maturity.”

Matías smiled.

“Exactly.”

Elías looked at the text again.

He had often used Ephesians four to explain why churches needed sound teaching.

He still believed that.

Now he noticed how much emphasis the passage placed upon the growth of the whole body.

Adrián leaned forward.

“Then shouldn't the leaders be helping people learn to recognize truth for themselves?”

Marcos nodded.

“That's part of equipping them.”

“And to examine Scripture,” Matías added.

Elías looked down at the passage.

“Yes.”

Adrián smiled.

“Then Daniel's questions could be an opportunity.”

“For what?” Marcos asked.

“For everyone to learn!”

Matías laughed.

Adrián looked from one to another.

“Think about it. Daniel finds something difficult. He brings it to the elders. They study it with him. Maybe Daniel learns something. Maybe the elders learn something. Maybe the whole congregation discovers something wonderful.”

Elías smiled.

“And perhaps they discover Daniel was mistaken.”

“Then help him see it!” Adrián said.

“Or perhaps the church's explanation needs correcting,” Marcos added.

“Then correct it,” Matías replied.

Adrián spread his hands.

“Either way, Scripture hasn't changed!”

Elías looked at him.

The words stayed with him.

Scripture hadn't changed.

The question was whether they had understood it.

He had spent years defending the reliability of God's Word.

He still believed it completely.

Yet he was beginning to recognize how closely his confidence in Scripture had become tied to his confidence in his explanations of Scripture.

The two had become difficult to separate.

Marcos looked down at Ephesians four.

“Then the leaders have a wonderful responsibility.”

Elías nodded.

“To equip the saints.”

“Yes,” Marcos said. “To help them grow in their knowledge of Christ. To help them become discerning. To help them handle Scripture faithfully.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“And the leaders themselves are still learning.”

“Of course,” Elías said.

Adrián grinned.

“Imagine an elder saying, ‘That's a good question. I don't know.’”

Matías laughed.

“Scandalous.”

“Or, ‘I've taught this for twenty years, and you've given me something to reconsider.’”

Marcos smiled.

“That would take humility.”

Elías looked at them.

He could imagine saying those words once.

Years ago.

Before he had become the man people expected to have answers.

He remembered the pleasure of opening Scripture without knowing what he would discover.

He remembered being wrong about a passage and feeling grateful when someone helped him understand it.

He remembered Daniel at the retreat, asking question after question, delighted whenever someone opened another passage.

And he remembered Harold and Peter.

Men who wanted to protect the church.

Men who had become convinced that protecting the church required protecting the conclusions they'd already reached.

Elías felt no satisfaction in recognizing their difficulty.

He understood it too well.

Marcos looked up from Ephesians.

“There's something almost tragic about this.”

“What?” Matías asked.

“The church needs exactly what Daniel is looking for.”

Elías looked at him.

Marcos continued.

“The freedom to examine Scripture, to ask questions, to discover when we've misunderstood something, and to grow.”

Adrián nodded enthusiastically.

“Yes!”

“And the leaders are supposed to help the congregation do that.”

Matías looked down at the passage.

“They're supposed to equip them.”

Elías sat quietly.

The church itself needed the freedom Daniel was seeking.

He thought of Harold.

Peter.

The other elders.

He thought of the people who had sat under his own teaching for years.

And he thought of how much easier it might have been for all of them if they hadn't felt required to defend every conclusion they'd inherited.

Adrián leaned back.

“Wow.”

Matías smiled.

“What?”

“Think how different that church meeting could have been.”

Elías nodded.

He could imagine it.

Daniel opening his Bible.

Harold asking him to explain what he'd noticed.

Peter presenting the reasons for their established interpretation.

Everyone examining the passage.

Nobody's place in the fellowship being threatened.

Nobody needing to win.

He looked at the open Bible before him.

It was a lovely possibility.

And it made the memory of the actual meeting more painful.

Anna came to clear their plates.

“Are you gentlemen finished?”

Adrián looked at the remaining piece of toast on his plate.

“Almost.”

Matías laughed.

“You've been almost finished for ten minutes.”

“I'm considering it carefully.”

Anna smiled.

“Wouldn't want you to rush into a conclusion.”

Marcos burst out laughing.

Elías joined him.

Adrián looked around the table.

“Everybody's a comedian today.”

Anna took the empty plates.

“More coffee?”

“Yes, please,” Elías said.

She looked pleasantly surprised.

“Coming right up.”

When she'd gone, Elías turned toward the others.

“I want to say something.”

They looked at him.

“I don't want to turn against those men.”

Matías nodded.

“We know.”

“I remember enjoying their teaching. I've worked alongside them for years. I understand why they're concerned.”

Marcos listened.

“They're doing what they've been taught to do.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Then perhaps they need to discover something too.”

Elías looked surprised.

“Perhaps.”

Matías smiled.

“They're your brothers.”

“Yes.”

“And Daniel is your brother.”

“Yes.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Then we want what's good for all of them.”

Elías looked at him.

The answer seemed so simple.

He had spent most of the week wondering which side he was expected to take.

The elders had already begun questioning his loyalty.

He knew how quickly disagreement could become division.

Yet his friends weren't asking him to choose between the leaders and Daniel.

They were concerned for everyone.

Adrián smiled.

“And perhaps the elders could use a little encouragement too.”

Elías looked surprised.

“Encouragement?”

“Sure. Imagine carrying the responsibility of having to be right about everything.”

Matías looked at Elías.

“That would be exhausting.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Yes.”

He knew something about that.

Marcos nodded.

“They don't have to be infallible to be faithful shepherds.”

Elías looked down at his Bible.

“No,” he said quietly.

“They don't.”

For a moment, he thought of the separate meeting Harold had proposed.

He still didn't know what he would say.

He didn't know whether the elders would listen.

He didn't even know what he believed about every detail of the disagreement over First John.

Yet he was no longer certain that he needed to settle all those questions before he could care about Daniel.

Or about the elders.

He looked at his friends.

They were still talking, still enjoying one another, still finding new things in Scripture.

None of them seemed interested in establishing himself as the authority at the table.

And Elías found that he didn't need to be either.

They stayed longer than they'd intended.

At one point Matías opened First John again, and the discussion returned to fellowship.

They read the opening verses about fellowship with the Father and with His Son, Jesus Christ.

Then they read John's declaration that God is light and in Him is no darkness at all.

Adrián looked at the passage.

“It's amazing how John begins.”

Marcos nodded.

“He's announcing something about God.”

“And about fellowship,” Matías added.

Elías followed along.

They considered the sequence of John's statements, the claims about walking in darkness, the cleansing blood of Jesus Christ, and the acknowledgment of sin.

They noticed that the passage raised more questions than their brief discussion could settle.

Nobody seemed disappointed.

Marcos made a note to examine the relationship between verses seven and nine more carefully.

Matías wanted to look at how John used confession elsewhere.

Adrián wanted to read the whole letter again.

Elías smiled.

“That's quite a list.”

Adrián grinned.

“Excellent! We have homework.”

Matías laughed.

“I thought we were finished with that.”

“Not when there's so much to discover.”

Elías looked down at First John.

He remembered Daniel's words.

*I'd like to keep studying it.*

He wondered how the young man was doing.

For a moment, he imagined Daniel sitting with them, surrounded by open Bibles and half-empty coffee cups.

He could almost hear Adrián asking him about Romans six again.

The thought made him smile.

He didn't mention it.

Not yet.

Marcos closed his Bible.

“Well, gentlemen, we've managed to add several questions to the ones we started with.”

Matías smiled.

“Productive morning.”

“Very,” Adrián agreed.

Elías laughed.

And he meant it.

They gathered their coats.

Outside, the winter sun had appeared between the clouds, bright against the snow.

Adrián picked up his new scraper.

Matías pointed at it.

“Don't forget your investment.”

Adrián held it up.

“Never.”

Marcos smiled.

“Perhaps next week you'll buy gloves.”

“One step at a time.”

They made their way toward the door.

Elías lingered for a moment, looking back at the table.

Four empty coffee cups.

A folded napkin with a rectangle and a circle drawn on it.

A few crumbs.

Nothing remarkable.

Yet he'd spent the morning with three men who had welcomed questions, examined Scripture, disagreed freely, and laughed together.

Nobody had needed to protect himself from discovering something new.

And nobody had been left outside the fellowship because he hadn't finished understanding a passage.

Elías picked up the napkin.

He looked at the rectangle.

Then at the little circle beside it.

He folded it once and slipped it into his Bible.

“Coming?” Matías called.

“Yes.”

Elías joined them.

Outside, Adrián was demonstrating his new scraper to Marcos.

Matías was laughing.

Elías joined them, smiling.

He still had questions.

Many of them.

Yet for the first time in years, he found himself looking forward to discovering the answers.

And he wasn't in a hurry.

There was so much more of Christ to discover.

# Chapter 32

## A Different Kind of Confidence

Elías had been sitting at the kitchen table for nearly twenty minutes when his wife came downstairs.

His Bible was open. Beside it lay a folded paper napkin.

She stopped at the doorway.

“Did you bring something home from the restaurant?”

He looked up.

“Good morning.”

“Good morning.”

She nodded toward the table.

“Is that a napkin?”

“Yes.”

“Should I be concerned?”

He smiled.

“Probably.”

She came closer.

“What's on it?”

“A drawing.”

“From Adrián?”

“Marcos, actually.”

“Oh. Then it's probably organized.”

Elías laughed.

She looked at him for a moment.

He unfolded the napkin and turned it toward her.

A rectangle. A small circle beside it.

She studied the drawing.

“I'm afraid I don't understand.”

“Neither did I at first.”

He pulled out the chair beside him.

“Come and see.”

She sat down.

“Marcos drew this while we were talking about Daniel and the elders.”

He pointed to the rectangle.

“This represents the church's established understanding of Scripture. The doctrines we've agreed upon and the interpretations we've accepted.”

“And the little circle?”

“Daniel.”

She looked at the drawing.

“Why is he outside?”

“Because he's questioning one of those interpretations.”

“Does that mean he's outside the church?”

Elías hesitated.

“That's what troubles me. He's questioning our understanding of a passage, and somehow that's become a question about whether he belongs.”

She studied the rectangle again.

“So the rectangle isn't Scripture itself.”

“No. It's our understanding of Scripture.”

“And everyone inside is expected to agree with that understanding?”

“On the doctrines we've established, yes.”

She nodded slowly.

Then she asked, “And which one are you?”

Elías glanced at the drawing.

“The circle is Daniel.”

“I know. Which one are you?”

He opened his mouth.

Then closed it.

He had been thinking of the rectangle as a picture of the church's doctrinal boundaries.

He hadn't considered where he stood in it.

“Inside,” he said at last.

She nodded.

“Have you always been?”

“As far as I can remember.”

She ran her finger along one of its lines.

“And you helped draw these?”

Elías looked at her.

“Yes. Some of them.”

She studied the drawing again.

“Then I imagine this has been quite a week.”

Elías smiled.

“You could say that.”

His eyes began to fill with tears.

She noticed immediately.

“Elías?”

He smiled again, a little embarrassed.

“I'm all right.”

She reached across the table and touched his hand.

“What's wrong?”

“Nothing.”

He laughed softly, wiping his eyes.

“That's just it. I've been seeing so many things lately. Things I've read in Scripture for years, things I've taught, things I thought I understood.”

He paused.

“And now I'm beginning to appreciate them in ways I never have before.”

She watched him.

“It's been wonderful,” he said.

The tears came again, and this time he didn't try to hide them.

“I can't quite explain it.”

She smiled gently.

“That's unusual for you.”

He laughed.

“Yes, it is.”

They sat together for a moment.

She looked at the napkin again.

“Are you going to have to change everything you believe?”

Elías considered the question.

“I don't know how much of my understanding will change.”

He glanced at his open Bible.

“I'm discovering that I don't have to know that before I open the Scriptures.”

She smiled.

“You sound happy about that.”

“I am.”

He looked surprised by his own answer.

“I really am.”

She squeezed his hand.

He sat quietly for a moment.

Then he looked at her.

“I've been thinking about something you said.”

“About what?”

“About feeling lonely.”

Her smile faded slightly.

He continued.

“I've thought about it quite a bit.”

“I know.”

“You do?”

“You've been unusually quiet.”

He smiled.

“I suppose I have.”

She waited.

“I've wanted to explain myself,” he admitted. “Several times.”

“I noticed that too.”

He laughed softly.

Then he looked at her.

“I think I'm beginning to understand that explaining myself isn't the same as hearing you.”

She looked down at their hands.

“No,” she said quietly. “It isn't.”

Elías nodded.

He could have continued. There were things he wanted to say about the years they'd spent together, the responsibilities he'd carried, the decisions he'd made with the best intentions.

All of them were true.

He left them alone.

After a while she said, “Do you remember when we used to go walking after supper?”

He smiled.

“Of course.”

“We didn't have anywhere particular to go.”

“No.”

“Sometimes we'd walk for an hour.”

“And end up back where we started.”

She laughed.

“You always said we should have planned a route.”

“I did?”

“Every time.”

He laughed.

She watched him.

“I liked those walks.”

“So did I.”

“I didn't need us to accomplish anything.”

Elías looked at her.

He had never considered that their walks might have meant something different to her than they had to him.

“What did you enjoy about them?” he asked.

She looked surprised.

“Being with you.”

He smiled.

“I enjoyed being with you too.”

“I know you did.”

She paused.

“It's just that we could talk about anything. Or nothing. You didn't seem to have so much on your mind.”

Elías listened.

“And when I told you something, you were interested. Even if it wasn't important.”

He looked at her.

“It was important to you.”

“Yes.”

She smiled a little.

“And that used to be enough.”

Elías was quiet.

He remembered those walks.

He remembered laughing with her over things neither of them could recall the following morning.

He remembered how quickly an evening could pass.

He had missed those things.

He hadn't realized how much she had missed them too.

“I'd like to go walking with you again,” he said.

She looked at him.

“Would you?”

“Yes.”

“Without a destination?”

He considered that.

“That's a rather demanding condition.”

She laughed.

“I know.”

He smiled.

“Perhaps we could manage it.”

She reached for his hand again.

For a while they sat together.

The coffee had gone cold.

Neither of them seemed to mind.

At church the following Sunday, Elías arrived earlier than usual.

The parking lot had been cleared, though a ridge of dirty snow remained along the curb. He parked near the side entrance and sat for a moment, watching people make their way toward the building.

He knew most of them.

He had taught their children, visited their homes, prayed with them, and answered questions after services.

Some had been attending longer than he had.

He saw Daniel's car near the far end of the lot.

For a moment he considered walking over.

Then Daniel emerged with his wife and children, and Elías decided not to interrupt them.

Inside, the familiar sounds greeted him.

Coats being hung.

Children calling to one another.

Someone testing a microphone.

A burst of laughter from the fellowship hall.

Elías found himself smiling.

He had been part of this congregation for so many years that the building seemed almost an extension of his life.

He didn't want to lose it.

“Good morning, Elías.”

He turned.

Peter stood beside the literature table.

“Good morning.”

“How are you?”

“I'm well, thank you. And you?”

“Busy. We've had a few things to sort out.”

Elías nodded.

“I imagine.”

Peter glanced toward the sanctuary.

“Harold wants to arrange that conversation with you. Perhaps Tuesday evening?”

“That should be fine.”

“Good.”

Peter hesitated.

“I hope you understand that none of this is personal.”

Elías looked at him.

“I know.”

“We have a responsibility.”

“Yes.”

Peter seemed relieved.

“We can't allow every established doctrine to become a matter of individual preference.”

“No,” Elías said. “That wouldn't serve anyone.”

Peter nodded.

“Exactly.”

He began to turn away.

“Peter?”

“Yes?”

“May I ask you something?”

“Of course.”

“When you first started teaching, did you ever discover that something you'd been taught wasn't quite what the passage said?”

Peter smiled.

“Certainly. We all have to grow.”

“What happened?”

Peter considered.

“Well, I remember an issue concerning the timing of certain prophetic events. I had assumed one interpretation was obvious until I studied the relevant passages more carefully.”

“And you changed your understanding?”

“Yes.”

Elías smiled.

“Was that difficult?”

“A little. I had to admit I hadn't studied it as thoroughly as I thought.”

They both laughed.

Peter looked at him curiously.

“Why do you ask?”

“I was remembering how wonderful it can be to discover something in Scripture that we hadn't seen before.”

Peter nodded.

“It certainly can.”

For a moment, they stood together in easy agreement.

Then Peter's expression changed slightly.

“I assume this relates to Daniel.”

“In part.”

“Elías, the problem isn't that we oppose learning. The issue is that Daniel is questioning a settled doctrine while serving as a teacher.”

“I understand.”

“And if every teacher feels free to reconsider whatever he wishes, we lose consistency.”

Elías nodded.

He knew the reasoning.

He had used it himself.

Peter continued.

“People need to know what they're going to hear when they come to church.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

Then he asked, “Do you think there's a difference between knowing what someone is going to teach and knowing what someone is allowed to study?”

Peter looked at him.

“I'm not sure I follow.”

“A teacher has agreed to represent the church's position. I understand why that matters. Yet suppose he encounters something in Scripture that makes him question that position. What should he do with the question?”

“Bring it to the elders.”

“And if the elders remain convinced of the established interpretation?”

“Then he needs to decide whether he can continue teaching under our statement.”

Elías nodded.

“That may be a necessary decision about his teaching responsibilities.”

Peter waited.

“I'm wondering about something else.”

“What?”

“Does he still have the freedom to keep examining the passage?”

Peter frowned.

“Of course he can study his Bible.”

Elías smiled.

“I'm glad.”

“Provided he isn't undermining the church.”

Elías recognized the familiar qualification.

He had made it himself many times.

He thought about asking Peter what *undermining the church* meant.

Was Daniel undermining the church by asking his questions? Would he be doing so if he continued studying privately? What if another member approached him with the same question?

There was much he wanted to understand.

Peter glanced at his watch.

“We should probably continue this Tuesday.”

“Yes,” Elías replied. “I'd like that.”

Peter gave him a friendly pat on the shoulder.

“Good. I appreciate your willingness to talk.”

Elías watched him walk toward the sanctuary.

He wasn't angry.

Peter had listened.

He had answered sincerely.

And for a few moments they had enjoyed remembering the pleasure of discovering something new in Scripture.

Then the familiar boundaries had reappeared.

Elías stood beside the literature table, thinking.

He was beginning to recognize those boundaries more quickly.

He still didn't know what to do about them.

Daniel was adjusting a microphone stand when Elías entered the sanctuary.

He looked up.

“Good morning.”

“Good morning, Daniel.”

Daniel smiled.

“Everything all right?”

“Yes. How are you?”

“Doing well.”

He tightened the microphone clip.

“My youngest has decided that six in the morning is the perfect time to begin the day.”

Elías laughed.

“An ambitious child.”

“Very. Unfortunately, he expects the rest of us to participate.”

They smiled.

Elías looked at the cables on the floor.

“Still helping with the sound?”

“For now.”

Daniel said it lightly.

Elías understood.

He watched Daniel check a connection.

“Have you had any more time to study First John?”

Daniel looked up.

“A little.”

“Anything interesting?”

Daniel hesitated.

Then he smiled.

“Quite a bit, actually.”

Elías felt an unexpected pleasure.

“Really?”

“Yes. I've been looking at the opening of the letter and the way John moves from fellowship to light and then to the statements about sin.”

He paused.

“I don't want to get you into trouble.”

Elías looked at him.

“By telling me what you've been reading?”

Daniel gave a small, embarrassed laugh.

“I suppose that sounds strange.”

“It does a little.”

Daniel studied his face.

Elías smiled.

“I'd enjoy hearing what you've found.”

Daniel looked genuinely surprised.

“Would you?”

“Yes.”

Daniel glanced toward the front of the sanctuary.

“I have to finish setting up.”

“Of course.”

“Perhaps another time?”

“I'd like that.”

Daniel smiled.

“So would I.”

He returned to the microphone stand.

Elías walked toward his seat.

He found himself smiling.

Daniel had something to share from Scripture.

Elías wanted to hear it.

He didn't know whether he'd agree.

The thought didn't trouble him.

The morning teaching was from John fifteen.

Elías opened his Bible as the speaker read.

“I am the true vine, and My Father is the vinedresser.”

He knew the passage.

He had taught it.

He had explained its imagery, its structure, its relationship to fruitfulness and discipleship.

The speaker continued reading.

“Abide in Me, and I in you.”

Elías followed the words.

“As the branch cannot bear fruit of itself, unless it abides in the vine, neither can you, unless you abide in Me.”

He read the next verse silently.

“I am the vine, you are the branches.”

He had always loved the imagery.

Today something caught his attention.

*The branch cannot bear fruit of itself.*

He read the sentence again.

It was not a criticism of the branch's effort.

It was a statement about its source.

He thought of the churches they'd studied.

Corinth.

Galatia.

Laodicea.

Ephesus.

Different activities.

Different appearances.

Different ways of measuring spiritual health.

He looked at the words again.

*Without Me you can do nothing.*

He had quoted that verse many times.

Often to encourage believers to pray more, study more, remain disciplined, and depend upon the Lord.

Those were familiar applications.

Today he found himself thinking about the branch.

It didn't produce life.

It received life.

He looked toward the front.

The speaker was explaining the importance of abiding in Christ.

Elías listened.

He wasn't trying to determine whether he agreed with every sentence.

He was thinking about the vine.

A living vine.

Branches drawing life from it.

Fruit appearing because life was present.

He smiled.

He had known the passage for decades.

And there was still so much in it.

The speaker moved to another point.

Elías remained with the image.

For once, he didn't mind being a little behind.

After the service, several people stopped to speak with him.

A young father asked about a passage in Romans.

An older woman mentioned a Bible study she'd been enjoying.

Someone asked whether Elías would be teaching the following month.

He answered what he could.

Then the young father returned.

“I've been thinking about what you said last year concerning Romans seven.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes?”

“I was reading Romans six and eight together, and I'm not sure I understand how they fit with that explanation.”

Elías felt the familiar movement within himself.

He knew the question.

He knew the standard answer.

He could have delivered it almost without thinking.

Instead, he asked, “What did you notice?”

The young man looked surprised.

“Well, Paul seems to say something in chapter six that makes me wonder about the way I've understood chapter seven.”

“Show me.”

The young man opened his Bible.

They stood near the back of the sanctuary while he read several verses.

Elías listened.

The question wasn't new.

He had heard variations of it for years.

Yet the young man had noticed a genuine tension worth examining.

Elías could see that.

“I've always explained the passage a particular way,” he said. “I'd like to look at what you're seeing more carefully.”

The young man smiled.

“Really?”

“Yes.”

“I thought perhaps I was missing something obvious.”

“You may be. I may be missing something too.”

The young man laughed.

Elías joined him.

They agreed to talk again.

As the young man walked away, Elías stood still.

He had just admitted uncertainty about a passage he'd taught for years.

Nothing terrible had happened.

In fact, he'd enjoyed the conversation.

He shook his head slightly.

How strange.

He had spent so much of his life feeling responsible to resolve questions.

Today he'd helped someone keep asking one.

And he was looking forward to studying it with him.

That afternoon, Elías found his wife in the living room reading.

He stood in the doorway.

“Are you busy?”

She looked up.

“Not particularly.”

He glanced toward the window.

The sun had come out, and the streets were beginning to dry.

“Would you like to go for a walk?”

She lowered her book.

“Now?”

“Yes.”

She smiled.

“Where?”

He considered the question.

“I have absolutely no idea.”

She laughed.

“Then give me five minutes.”

He watched her go upstairs.

He was still smiling when she returned.

They walked through the neighborhood, passing familiar houses and bare trees. Snow had melted along the edges of the sidewalks, leaving narrow streams of water running toward the drains.

For a while they talked about ordinary things.

A neighbor's new car.

A family gathering.

A recipe she'd been meaning to try.

Elías listened.

At one point she told him about a conversation she'd had with a friend.

He began to offer an explanation.

Then stopped.

She noticed.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“Elías.”

He laughed.

“I was about to tell you what I thought you should do.”

“I know.”

“Would you rather tell me more about what happened?”

She smiled.

“Yes.”

So she did.

He asked questions.

Some of her answers surprised him.

They walked another block.

Then another.

Eventually she looked around.

“Do you know where we are?”

“Of course.”

“Good.”

“We're lost.”

She burst out laughing.

He joined her.

They weren't lost.

They were only a few streets from home.

Yet neither was in a hurry to return.

At the next corner she slipped her arm through his.

He looked at her.

She smiled.

And they kept walking.

Tuesday evening arrived with freezing rain.

Elías drove carefully through the dark streets, his windshield wipers pushing aside a thin film of ice.

He parked at the church and sat for a moment before going inside.

The meeting was scheduled for seven.

He had prepared.

Of course he had.

There were notes in his Bible, passages he'd marked, and several questions he'd written down.

Old habits were not easily abandoned.

He smiled at the thought.

He wasn't expecting to abandon them.

Careful study had never been the enemy.

He wanted to understand Scripture.

He wanted to help others understand it.

What he was beginning to question was the burden he'd carried alongside that desire.

The need to arrive with the answer.

The need to preserve his standing.

The need to protect an explanation simply because he'd taught it.

He looked at the church building.

Lights glowed behind the windows.

People were inside preparing for another evening of activities.

He thought of Daniel.

He thought of Peter's recollection of changing his mind about a prophetic passage.

He thought of the young father who had asked about Romans.

And he thought of his friends at Deb's Place.

He could still hear Adrián's delighted voice.

*Another question!*

Elías laughed softly.

Then he opened his Bible.

The folded napkin lay between the pages.

He took it out.

The rectangle.

The circle.

He studied them for a moment.

He remembered his wife's question.

*Which one are you?*

He smiled.

He had been inside that rectangle for a long time.

He still was, in many ways.

There were questions he hadn't learned to ask.

Assumptions he hadn't recognized.

Explanations he still reached for instinctively.

And there were things he was only beginning to see.

He thought of the morning's reading in John fifteen.

The vine.

The branches.

The life that came from Christ.

He looked at the rectangle again.

It seemed very small.

He folded the napkin and returned it to his Bible.

Then he went inside.

Harold and Peter were already waiting in the meeting room.

A third elder, Samuel, sat near the window.

They greeted him warmly.

“Elías,” Harold said. “Thank you for coming.”

“Of course.”

They sat.

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Harold opened his Bible.

“I thought it would be helpful to begin with the passage Daniel has been questioning.”

Elías smiled.

“I'd like that.”

Harold looked at him.

There was something different in his expression.

Not defiance.

Not uncertainty exactly.

Something Harold couldn't immediately identify.

He began reading.

Elías followed along.

The words were familiar.

The questions were familiar.

The room was familiar.

Yet Elías noticed something as Harold reached the ninth verse.

He wasn't preparing his response.

He was listening.

And he wanted to understand.

The meeting had only just begun.

# Chapter 34

## I Will Remove Your Lampstand

Adrián was studying the dessert menu when Elías arrived at Deb's Place.

“You're early,” Elías said.

Adrián looked up.

“So are you.”

“I'm five minutes late.”

“Then I'm very early.”

Elías laughed and took off his coat.

Matías and Marcos were already seated. Two Bibles lay open on the table, and Marcos had arranged four mugs beside the sugar dispenser.

Elías glanced at them.

“Expecting company?”

“Anna was bringing coffee,” Marcos said.

“Not just any coffee,” Adrián added. “Our coffee.”

“Is there a difference?” Matías asked.

“Of course. We've been coming here long enough that it should be considered a standing appointment.”

Anna approached with the pot.

“It is,” she said.

Adrián looked delighted.

“You see?”

“I just wish you'd all arrive at the same time.”

“We're working on it,” Marcos said.

Anna filled their mugs.

“And have you found that scraper yet?”

Adrián stared at her.

“Why does everyone remember my difficulties?”

“Did you find it?”

He sighed.

“Under the seat.”

Matías began laughing.

Marcos shook his head.

Elías laughed so hard he had to put down his coffee.

Anna smiled.

“Imagine that.”

Adrián raised a finger.

“In my defense, it was very far under the seat.”

“Of course it was,” she said, returning to the counter.

When the laughter subsided, Matías looked at Elías.

“How are you doing?”

“Very well, thank you.”

“You look rested.”

“I've been sleeping better.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's good.”

Elías took a sip of coffee.

“And my wife and I have been walking in the evenings.”

Adrián looked up.

“In this weather?”

“Yes.”

“Voluntarily?”

Elías laughed.

“Voluntarily.”

Matías smiled.

“How does she like that?”

“I think she's enjoying it.”

“And you?”

Elías looked down at his cup, smiling.

“More than I expected.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Gentlemen, we're witnessing a remarkable development.”

Marcos looked at him.

“What development?”

“Elías has discovered that his wife is interesting.”

Elías burst out laughing.

“She's always been interesting!”

“Then you've been very slow to notice.”

“Adrián!”

The table dissolved into laughter.

Elías shook his head.

He didn't mind the teasing.

There was something pleasant about being able to laugh at himself.

After a while, Matías turned his Bible around.

“Shall we finish Ephesus?”

Elías smiled.

“I'd like that.”

Marcos opened Revelation 2.

“I've been reading the message again.”

“Anything new?” Adrián asked.

“Something that bothers me.”

“Excellent.”

Marcos looked at him.

“Why is that excellent?”

“Because it usually means we're about to discover something.”

Matías smiled.

Marcos shook his head.

“All right. Listen.”

He read the opening verses.

Christ knew the church's works, labor, and perseverance. They could not tolerate evil people. They had tested those who claimed to be apostles and found them false. They had endured for His name without growing weary.

Marcos stopped.

“That's quite a record.”

“It is,” Matías said.

“I would have considered that a very faithful church.”

Adrián nodded.

“So would I.”

Elías looked at the passage.

“I can understand why.”

Marcos turned toward him.

“That's what troubles me. Christ acknowledges all these things.”

“Yes.”

“Then He says they've left their first love.”

Matías read the fourth verse aloud.

“But I have this against you, that you left your first love.”

He paused.

Marcos looked at him.

“How do we put those two things together?”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Perhaps we've been putting them together the wrong way.”

The others looked at him.

“What do you mean?” Marcos asked.

“We've been reading the first three verses and deciding that Ephesus must have been spiritual.”

Matías nodded.

“That's how we approached it.”

Adrián pointed to the next verse.

“Does Christ say that?”

Elías looked down at the passage.

“No.”

Marcos frowned.

“He says they've fallen.”

Matías read the beginning of verse five.

“Remember therefore from where you have fallen, and repent and do the first works.”

He stopped.

“Fallen.”

Elías nodded.

“That's Christ's assessment.”

Marcos looked again at the earlier verses.

“Then why does He acknowledge all those things?”

“Because He knows what they've done,” Adrián said.

Marcos waited.

“He knows their labor. He knows their perseverance. He knows they've tested false apostles.”

“Yes.”

“But we've been assuming that because those activities were commendable, they must have been produced by the Spirit.”

Elías looked at Adrián.

A smile began to form.

“That's interesting.”

Adrián grinned.

“I was hoping you'd say that.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Christ tells us what they were doing. Then He tells us they'd fallen.”

Marcos leaned back.

“So we've been using the commendations to defend a church Christ says has fallen.”

Elías sat quietly for a moment.

“I think that's exactly what we've been doing.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Then why are we still trying to defend it?”

No one answered.

Marcos picked up his coffee.

“I suppose I've always thought that perseverance was a spiritual quality.”

“It can be,” Matías said.

“And diligence?”

“That too.”

“Discernment?”

Elías nodded.

“Certainly.”

Marcos looked at them.

“Then how can those things be present in a church that has fallen?”

Elías considered the question.

“Perhaps we need to distinguish the activity from its source.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Go on.”

“A believer can persevere through dependence upon Christ.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And a person can persevere through determination, discipline, and human effort.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“The outward activity might look much the same.”

“Yes.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“So the flesh can persevere.”

“Certainly.”

“It can be diligent.”

“Yes.”

“It can study Scripture and recognize false teaching.”

Elías nodded.

“It can.”

Adrián sat back.

“Then we've been giving the flesh far too little credit.”

Matías laughed.

“That's an unusual way to put it.”

“I know.”

Adrián looked at Revelation again.

“We seem to think the flesh can produce jealousy, rivalry, and all sorts of obvious sins, yet when someone becomes disciplined, diligent, and doctrinally knowledgeable, we assume the Spirit must be producing it.”

Elías smiled.

“And that assumption isn't justified.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then the activities Christ acknowledges don't necessarily tell us the source from which they were performed.”

“No,” Elías said.

Matías leaned forward.

“And we need to remember something else.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“A believer is not *in the flesh*.”

Elías nodded.

“Romans eight.”

Matías opened his Bible.

He found the ninth verse.

“But you are not in the flesh but in the Spirit, if it is so that the Spirit of God dwells in you.”

He looked up.

“That's the believer's position.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Yet Paul can still warn believers about attempting to accomplish something by the flesh.”

“Galatians three,” Adrián said.

“Exactly.”

Marcos considered.

“So a church composed of believers who are in Christ can conduct its affairs by the flesh.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes.”

“And that doesn't mean their position in Christ has changed.”

“No.”

“It means they're drawing upon the wrong source.”

Elías nodded.

“That's the distinction.”

Adrián looked down at Revelation.

“Then a church could have a very impressive religious record and still be operating by the flesh.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

Marcos was quiet.

Then he looked at the fourth verse.

“And Ephesus had left its first love.”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Gentlemen, I think we're beginning to see why Ephesus is so difficult to recognize.”

Matías turned to Galatians 3.

“Let's read Paul's question again.”

He found the third verse.

“Are you so foolish? Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?”

He looked up.

“There's our distinction.”

Marcos nodded.

“Beginning in the Spirit doesn't mean the flesh becomes capable of completing what the Spirit began.”

“No,” Elías said.

“The flesh remains the flesh.”

Adrián smiled.

“Even when it learns to behave religiously.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Then religious activity can continue through human ability even when dependence upon Christ has been neglected.”

Elías nodded.

“That's possible.”

Marcos looked at Revelation.

“Could that have happened in Ephesus?”

Elías considered.

“The passage doesn't tell us exactly how the church came to leave its first love.”

“No,” Matías agreed.

“Nor does it identify the source of every individual action.”

Adrián nodded.

“Yet Christ does tell us they've fallen.”

“Yes.”

“And that their record hasn't prevented it.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Then perhaps the question isn't whether we can reconstruct every stage of Ephesus's decline.”

“What is it?” Matías asked.

“Why we've been treating its religious record as proof that it was spiritual.”

Elías smiled.

“That's a much better question.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Because the flesh can produce the same outward activities.”

“Yes.”

“And if those activities are genuinely produced through faith?”

“Then Christ is their source,” Elías said. “A believer has no spiritual strength independently of Him.”

Marcos nodded.

“So we can't credit the believer's flesh for what Christ produces.”

“No.”

“And we can't credit Christ as the source of something merely because it looks religious.”

Elías smiled.

“Exactly.”

Matías looked at Revelation 2.

“Then Christ's declaration that Ephesus had fallen is the decisive statement about its condition.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián sat back.

“I think I've been reading the passage backward.”

Marcos looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“I've been starting with the commendations and using them to explain away the warning.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“Instead of allowing Christ's warning to tell us how serious the church's condition had become.”

“Yes.”

Elías looked down at the fifth verse.

“And Christ doesn't leave us wondering whether He considers it serious.”

Anna arrived with another pot of coffee.

“Refills?”

Adrián raised his mug.

“Please.”

She filled it.

“Have you solved everything yet?”

“Not even close.”

“Good,” she said. “I'd hate to lose my Thursday customers.”

They laughed.

When she left, Marcos turned a page in his notebook.

“I've been thinking about the Pharisees.”

Adrián looked up.

“That's not usually how people begin a pleasant evening.”

Matías laughed.

Marcos smiled.

“I'm serious.”

“We know,” Adrián said. “That's what worries us.”

Elías laughed.

Marcos continued.

“They were often extraordinarily disciplined. They studied Scripture. They were concerned about religious purity.”

Elías nodded.

“And Paul could speak of zeal for God without treating zeal itself as proof of righteousness.”

Marcos turned to Romans 10.

He read the second verse.

“For I testify about them that they have a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge.”

He looked up.

“Paul acknowledges their zeal.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Yet their zeal doesn't establish that they're right.”

“No.”

Matías considered.

“And the flesh can be very zealous.”

“Very,” Elías said.

Adrián leaned forward.

“Even zealous about something true?”

Elías nodded.

“Yes. A person can defend a true statement by the flesh.”

Marcos looked at him.

“That's uncomfortable.”

Elías smiled.

“It is.”

He thought for a moment.

“I've spent years defending interpretations of Scripture. Some of those interpretations may be entirely correct.”

Matías nodded.

“Of course.”

“Yet the correctness of an interpretation doesn't establish that my manner of defending it came from dependence upon Christ.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“You could be right about the doctrine and still be operating by the flesh.”

“Yes.”

Marcos nodded.

“And being right might make that harder to recognize.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“I know it can.”

For a moment they were quiet.

Then Matías looked at Revelation.

“Perhaps that's why the Ephesian commendations have been so persuasive to us.”

Marcos nodded.

“We recognize things we consider important.”

“Things that may indeed be important,” Elías said.

“Yes.”

Matías continued.

“Yet we've been treating the presence of those things as proof of the church's spiritual condition.”

Adrián looked at the fifth verse.

“And Christ says it has fallen.”

Elías nodded.

“He does.”

Marcos turned back to Revelation 2.

“Then what does Christ tell them to do?”

Matías read the beginning of verse five.

“Remember therefore from where you have fallen, and repent and do the first works.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Three commands.”

“Remember. Repent. Do the first works,” Marcos said.

Elías nodded.

“We've examined those before.”

“Yes,” Matías replied.

Marcos looked at the preceding verses.

“Does Christ tell them to stop laboring?”

Elías considered.

“He doesn't say that.”

“Does He tell them to keep doing everything as they are?”

“No.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“He tells them to repent.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Then the commendations aren't permission to continue unchanged.”

“No,” Elías said.

Marcos nodded slowly.

“That's important.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Suppose a church receives a warning like this and responds by becoming more diligent.”

Marcos smiled.

“More programs.”

“More meetings,” Matías added.

“More doctrinal examinations,” Elías said.

Adrián laughed.

“Exactly.”

Then he looked at the verse.

“Could all that happen without repentance?”

Elías nodded.

“Certainly.”

Marcos considered.

“Then the flesh might respond to a warning about the flesh by increasing religious activity.”

Matías laughed softly.

“That's quite a thought.”

Elías smiled.

“And a very serious possibility.”

Adrián looked at Revelation again.

“Christ doesn't tell them to produce a more impressive record.”

“No.”

“He tells them to remember where they've fallen from.”

“Yes.”

“And repent.”

“Yes.”

Marcos looked at Elías.

“What about the first works?”

Elías considered.

“They belong with the first love Christ says they've left. Whatever those works involved, Christ is calling them back to something they had departed from.”

Matías nodded.

“Not merely to improve the activities they already know how to perform.”

“No.”

Adrián looked down at his Bible.

“Then this isn't a call to become more successful at being Ephesus.”

Elías smiled.

“No.”

“It's a call to repent of what Ephesus has become.”

The table grew quiet.

Marcos looked at the fifth verse.

“That is a very different message.”

Elías sat back.

He had been listening to the others with growing interest.

There had been a time when he would have wanted to organize the entire discussion into a lesson.

He would have identified the main points, clarified the terminology, and prepared a conclusion.

Tonight he was enjoying the discoveries as they came.

Matías looked at him.

“You're smiling.”

“Am I?”

“Yes.”

Elías laughed.

“I've been enjoying this.”

Adrián grinned.

“Another remarkable development.”

“What now?”

“Elías is enjoying a conversation he hasn't organized.”

Marcos laughed.

Elías shook his head.

“I'm becoming a source of entertainment.”

“You always were,” Matías said. “You just didn't know it.”

They laughed.

Elías looked around the table.

“There's something I want to tell you.”

The others waited.

“I used to feel responsible for making sure everyone understood a passage the way I understood it.”

Adrián nodded.

“We remember.”

Elías laughed.

“Yes, I imagine you do.”

He grew thoughtful.

“Now I find myself wanting to know what you've discovered.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's been a pleasant change.”

Elías looked at him.

“I think so too.”

He paused.

“I haven't lost my interest in doctrine. If anything, I enjoy studying Scripture more than I used to.”

Matías nodded.

“Because you don't have to defend every conclusion?”

“Perhaps that's part of it.”

Elías looked down at his Bible.

“I'm discovering that there's much more of Christ to appreciate than I ever realized.”

Adrián smiled.

“And more questions.”

“Many more.”

Matías laughed.

“Then you're in good company.”

Elías looked at his friends.

He thought of his wife waiting at home.

The walks they'd begun taking again.

The conversations in which he'd learned to listen rather than explain.

He smiled.

There was so much he had missed while trying to be certain of everything.

Then Matías turned back to Revelation.

“We haven't finished the warning.”

He read verse five slowly.

“Remember therefore from where you have fallen, and repent and do the first works; or else I am coming to you swiftly, and will move your lampstand out of its place, unless you repent.”

No one spoke.

Adrián lowered his cup.

“Read the last part again.”

Matías did.

“Or else I am coming to you swiftly, and will move your lampstand out of its place, unless you repent.”

Marcos looked at the page.

“That's severe.”

Elías nodded.

“Very.”

Adrián looked up.

“What exactly is the lampstand?”

Elías turned back to Revelation 1.

“Christ explains it in verse twenty.”

He found the passage.

“The seven lampstands are seven churches.”

Marcos looked at him.

“So Christ is threatening to move the church's lampstand out of its place.”

“Yes.”

“What would that mean?” Adrián asked.

Elías considered.

“The passage doesn't describe exactly how the judgment would be carried out.”

Matías nodded.

“We shouldn't invent details.”

“No,” Elías said.

He looked at the fifth verse.

“Yet Christ is speaking about the congregation itself. The threat is unmistakably serious.”

Marcos leaned back.

“After all those commendations.”

“Yes.”

Adrián shook his head slowly.

“That's what astonishes me.”

Matías looked at him.

“What?”

“Christ knows everything they've done.”

He pointed to the earlier verses.

“He knows their labor. Their perseverance. Their discernment. He knows they've tested false apostles and endured for His name.”

Elías nodded.

“Nothing has escaped His notice.”

“And knowing all of it,” Adrián continued, “He still says they've fallen.”

Marcos looked at verse five.

“And tells them to repent.”

“Yes.”

“Or He'll move their lampstand.”

Elías nodded.

Silence settled over the table.

For a while, nobody reached for his coffee.

Elías looked at the words.

He had read them many times.

He had taught them.

He had explained the symbolism of the lampstands and the importance of repentance.

Yet he couldn't remember ever sitting with the warning quite like this.

Christ had acknowledged the church's labor.

Its perseverance.

Its discernment.

Then He had declared that it had left its first love.

He had not told them to add a little affection to an otherwise satisfactory spiritual condition.

He had called them fallen.

And He had threatened their lampstand.

Elías looked at his friends.

“There's something remarkable here.”

Matías waited.

“Ephesus had been examining others.”

Marcos nodded.

“Testing those who claimed to be apostles.”

“Yes.”

Elías looked at the page.

“And now Christ was examining Ephesus.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“The church that could recognize what was wrong with others had something seriously wrong with itself.”

Elías nodded.

“And Christ wasn't willing to overlook it.”

Matías looked at the fifth verse.

“Not even for all the things He acknowledged.”

“No.”

Marcos sat back.

“I don't think I've ever appreciated the severity of this warning.”

“Neither have I,” Elías said.

Adrián was quiet.

Then he asked, “Do you think the church could have believed it was doing well?”

Marcos considered.

“With that record? I certainly might have.”

Matías nodded.

“Especially if I measured its condition by those activities.”

Elías looked at the passage.

“Yet Christ's assessment was different.”

Adrián shook his head.

“Imagine discovering that everything you were proudest of couldn't reverse His verdict.”

No one answered.

Outside, headlights passed across the restaurant windows.

Anna was wiping down the counter.

At another table, a couple shared a piece of pie.

The four men remained with their open Bibles.

After a while, Marcos spoke.

“I think we've been making the same mistake with Ephesus from the beginning.”

Matías looked at him.

“What mistake?”

“We keep trying to decide whether it was a good church or a bad church.”

Adrián nodded.

“And the commendations keep pulling us toward calling it good.”

“Yes.”

Marcos looked at verse five.

“Yet Christ has already told us its condition.”

Elías nodded.

“He has.”

Marcos continued.

“So why do we keep trying to soften that verdict?”

No one answered immediately.

Then Matías said, “Perhaps because we recognize things in Ephesus that we've always considered marks of faithfulness.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“I certainly do.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“Then maybe the warning is as much for people like us as it was for Ephesus.”

Elías considered.

“In the sense that we need to hear what Christ says rather than rely upon our own measures of spiritual health?”

“Yes.”

Elías nodded.

“I think that's a fair application.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Then let's be clear about what we've discovered.”

Adrián grinned.

“Marcos is organizing the conclusions.”

“Someone has to.”

They laughed.

Marcos continued.

“Christ acknowledges the church's record.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“He says it has left its first love.”

“Yes.”

“He calls it fallen and commands repentance.”

“Yes.”

“And unless it repents, He threatens to move its lampstand.”

Elías nodded.

“That's the message.”

Marcos looked at them.

“Then we can't use its record to declare it spiritually healthy.”

“No,” Elías said.

Adrián smiled faintly.

“Even though that's exactly what we wanted to do.”

“Yes.”

Matías closed his Bible halfway, then opened it again.

“There's something else.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“We've been talking about religious flesh.”

Adrián nodded.

“Quite a bit.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“The flesh can labor. It can persevere. It can become knowledgeable. It can defend a religious position.”

Elías nodded.

“And it can find confidence in all those things.”

“Yes.”

Matías paused.

“But it cannot produce the life of Christ.”

The words remained between them.

Elías looked at him.

“No,” he said quietly. “It cannot.”

Marcos considered.

“Then a church can maintain a remarkable religious record by the flesh.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And still have fallen from what Christ intended.”

Elías nodded.

“That is the danger.”

Adrián looked back at Revelation.

“And Ephesus shows us why that danger cannot be treated lightly.”

Matías touched the fifth verse.

“Because Christ doesn't treat it lightly.”

They sat in silence again.

This time nobody seemed eager to break it.

Anna approached with the check.

“Another productive evening?”

Adrián looked up.

“Very.”

“Did you solve anything?”

“Several things.”

“Wonderful.”

“And discovered several more questions.”

Anna smiled.

“That sounds about right.”

Marcos reached for the check.

Adrián stopped him.

“I'll get it.”

Matías raised an eyebrow.

“Are you feeling all right?”

“Perfectly.”

Elías laughed.

Adrián took the bill and studied it.

Then he looked at Anna.

“Is there a discount for regular customers who have made significant discoveries?”

“No.”

“Not even a small one?”

“No.”

He sighed.

“Very well. Truth is expensive.”

Anna laughed.

“Coffee isn't.”

As she walked away, Marcos began putting his Bible into its case.

Matías remained seated.

“There's one thing I keep thinking about.”

Elías looked at him.

“What?”

“The four churches.”

Adrián smiled.

“We've spent quite a bit of time with them.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked at the open Bible before him.

“We began with Corinth because Paul called them carnal.”

Marcos nodded.

“And we wondered whether other churches might offer a better picture of spiritual health.”

“Galatia seemed to,” Adrián said.

“Until we examined Paul's question about the flesh,” Matías replied.

“Laodicea seemed successful,” Marcos said.

He smiled faintly.

“I was particularly impressed with that one.”

“And Ephesus seemed faithful,” Elías added.

Adrián looked at him.

“You were particularly impressed with that one.”

Elías laughed.

“Yes, I was.”

Matías looked around the table.

“Gentlemen, I think we have something more to examine.”

Marcos nodded.

“I've been thinking the same thing.”

Adrián looked at them.

“Another church?”

“Not exactly,” Matías said.

“Good. I was beginning to wonder how many we'd find.”

Elías smiled.

“What are you thinking, Matías?”

Matías rested his hand on his Bible.

“We've examined each church separately.”

“Yes.”

“I'd like to put them beside one another.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“And see what they have in common?”

Matías nodded.

“Exactly.”

Adrián considered.

“Corinth, Galatia, Laodicea, and Ephesus.”

“Yes.”

He smiled.

“Gentlemen, that sounds like a very interesting evening.”

Elías looked at his friends.

He thought of how differently he had regarded each church when their conversations began.

He had been quick to recognize Corinth's divisions.

He had admired Galatia's seriousness.

He had understood the attraction of Laodicea's accomplishments.

And he had felt most comfortable with Ephesus.

Now he wasn't so certain that any of those measures could tell him what he most needed to know.

He looked at Revelation 2 one last time.

Christ had acknowledged what the church had done.

And He had declared what it had become.

Elías closed his Bible.

“I think we should do that.”

Matías smiled.

“Next Thursday?”

“Next Thursday,” Marcos said.

Adrián stood and reached for his coat.

“Excellent.”

He looked at Elías.

“And in the meantime, enjoy your walks.”

Elías laughed.

“I intend to.”

“With no destination?”

“None whatsoever.”

Adrián shook his head.

“Remarkable. The man is becoming unpredictable.”

They laughed as they made their way toward the door.

Outside, the evening was cold and clear.

Elías paused beside his car and looked up at the sky.

He thought again of the church at Ephesus.

A remarkable religious record.

A condition Christ called fallen.

And a warning that none of its accomplishments could overturn.

He was glad they had taken the time to read the whole message.

He was glad they hadn't mistaken religious activity for spiritual life.

And he was glad they hadn't softened Christ's warning.

Next Thursday, they would bring all four churches together.

For now, one question remained with him.

If the flesh could maintain such an impressive religious record, how many of the things believers commonly regarded as evidence of spiritual health might tell them very little about their actual dependence upon Christ?

Elías smiled faintly.

He was looking forward to finding out.

# **Chapter 35**

## **The Church We Would Have Chosen**

Adrián was already seated when Marcos arrived at Deb’s Place.

He had a coffee in front of him, a second cup beside it, and a rather satisfied expression on his face.

Marcos stopped beside the table.

“Two coffees?”

Adrián looked up.

“One is mine.”

“And the other?”

“Also mine.”

Marcos shook his head.

“Of course.”

“I ordered the second one before the first could disappoint me.”

“Has it?”

“Not yet. I'm trying to stay ahead of the problem.”

Marcos laughed and took his seat.

Anna appeared from behind the counter.

“Morning, Marcos.”

“Good morning, Anna.”

“The usual?”

“Please.”

She glanced at Adrián's two cups.

“I see you've met our coffee inspector.”

Marcos smiled.

“Has he approved today's batch?”

“Apparently. He hasn't threatened to leave yet.”

“I'm considering it,” Adrián said.

Anna rolled her eyes.

“You've been considering it for years.”

“And yet you keep improving.”

She laughed and headed toward the counter.

Marcos settled into his chair.

“How was your week?”

“Good. Yours?”

“Busy.”

“That's not news.”

Marcos smiled.

“I suppose not.”

Adrián studied him for a moment.

“You look rested.”

“Thank you.”

“Did something happen?”

“What do you mean?”

“You usually look like you've spent the night organizing something.”

Marcos laughed.

“I've actually been making an effort to leave work at work.”

Adrián stared at him.

“Are you feeling all right?”

“Perfectly.”

“Should I call someone?”

“Drink your coffee.”

“I'm trying. You keep distracting me with these alarming developments.”

The bell above the door rang.

Matías entered, carrying his Bible beneath one arm.

Adrián raised a hand.

“Ah! The responsible member of our group.”

Matías smiled as he approached.

“Good morning.”

“Good morning, my friend.”

Marcos stood briefly to greet him.

“How are you?”

“Very well, thank you.”

“And your father?”

“Doing better. My sister says he's been getting out more.”

“That's encouraging.”

“It is.”

Matías sat down and placed his Bible on the table.

Adrián glanced at it.

“Only one?”

Matías looked puzzled.

“One what?”

“Bible.”

“Yes.”

“No notebook? No additional references? No reading schedule?”

Matías smiled.

“Not today.”

Adrián turned toward Marcos.

“Something is happening to these men.”

Marcos laughed.

“Apparently.”

Matías looked at the two coffee cups.

“Something is happening to you too, I see.”

“What?”

“Your self-control.”

Adrián glanced down.

“Oh, that.”

“Two coffees?”

“I'm taking precautions.”

“Against what?”

“Running out.”

Matías laughed.

A few minutes later Elías arrived.

He looked considerably more relaxed than he had during many of their earlier meetings.

Adrián noticed immediately.

“Gentlemen, here he is.”

Elías smiled.

“Good morning.”

“Tell us.”

“Tell you what?”

“Where did you walk this week?”

Elías laughed.

“You're still interested in that?”

“Immensely.”

“We walked along the river.”

“With your wife?”

“Yes, Adrián. With my wife.”

“And?”

“And what?”

“Did you discuss anything?”

“Of course.”

“Anything theological?”

“Not particularly.”

Adrián looked at the others.

“Remarkable.”

Elías shook his head.

“We talked about our family. Some friends. Things we'd like to do.”

“And you didn't turn any of it into a Bible lesson?”

“No.”

“Not even a small one?”

“Not even a small one.”

Matías smiled.

“How did your wife enjoy that?”

Elías laughed.

“Very much.”

“Imagine that,” Adrián said.

Elías pulled out his chair.

“Are we going to discuss my marriage all morning?”

“Only until you start correcting us.”

They laughed.

Anna brought another coffee and set it before Elías.

“Here you are.”

“Thank you, Anna.”

She looked around the table.

“All four of you today.”

“Unfortunately,” Adrián said, “they keep finding their way back.”

“Somebody has to keep an eye on you.”

“That's what they tell me.”

Anna smiled and returned to the counter.

For a while the conversation wandered.

They talked about family, the weather, a mutual friend who had recently moved, and a restaurant Adrián insisted they needed to visit.

Matías had heard about it.

“Isn't that the place with the enormous portions?”

“Exactly.”

“I've seen pictures.”

“Pictures don't do it justice.”

Marcos smiled.

“You're recommending a restaurant based entirely on how much food they serve?”

“Of course not.”

“What else?”

“The desserts.”

Elías laughed.

“An important distinction.”

“Thank you.”

Eventually Matías reached for his Bible.

“I've been thinking about what we said last Thursday.”

Adrián looked at him.

“About restaurants?”

“About the churches.”

“Oh. Those.”

Marcos nodded.

“So have I.”

Elías settled back.

“We said we'd compare them.”

“Yes,” Matías replied.

Adrián glanced around the table.

“Four churches.”

“Four,” Marcos said.

“Corinth, Galatia, Laodicea, and Ephesus.”

Elías nodded.

“And four rather different situations.”

Adrián took a sip of coffee.

“Very different.”

Matías opened his Bible, then stopped.

“Actually, before we start looking at passages, can I ask something?”

“Of course,” Marcos said.

Matías looked toward Adrián.

“When we first started talking about Corinth, you really liked that church you visited.”

Adrián smiled.

“I did.”

“You were enthusiastic about it.”

“Very.”

“And when we examined Corinth, you weren't particularly happy.”

Adrián laughed.

“That's putting it mildly.”

“Why?”

Adrián considered the question.

“Well, I suppose I liked what I saw.”

“The excitement?” Matías asked.

“Yes.”

“The spiritual gifts?”

“Certainly.”

“The freedom people seemed to have?”

“Absolutely.”

He leaned back.

“I liked the atmosphere. People were happy to be there. They sang. They prayed. They shared things. It felt alive.”

Marcos nodded.

“You've always appreciated that kind of gathering.”

“I have.”

Adrián smiled.

“Look, I've been to churches where everybody looks like they're attending a funeral. I don't particularly enjoy that.”

Matías laughed.

“We know.”

“And I'm not saying quiet churches are necessarily wrong.”

“That's generous of you,” Elías said.

Adrián pointed at him.

“I'm growing.”

Elías smiled.

“Clearly.”

Adrián continued.

“When I see people excited about the Lord, I enjoy it. I like being around people who seem genuinely happy.”

“Nothing wrong with that,” Marcos said.

“Exactly.”

Adrián paused.

“Although I suppose I was assuming something.”

“What?” Matías asked.

“That because people seemed excited, they must be spiritual.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“And Corinth challenged that assumption.”

“It certainly did.”

Adrián shook his head.

“I still remember reading that they weren't lacking in any gift.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And then Paul calls them fleshly.”

“Because of jealousy and strife,” Matías added.

“Right.”

Adrián looked at his cup.

“That was quite a surprise.”

Marcos smiled.

“You defended them for a while.”

“I did.”

“Rather enthusiastically.”

“I'm an enthusiastic person.”

“Even when you're wrong?”

“Especially then.”

They laughed.

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Can I tell you something?”

Adrián nodded.

“Of course.”

“When we were studying Corinth, I wasn't nearly as troubled as you were.”

“I remember.”

“Actually, I felt rather comfortable with Paul's criticism.”

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

“Comfortable?”

“Yes.”

“That's an unusual response.”

Matías smiled.

“I know.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“I thought Corinth was a good example of what happens when Christians don't take their lives seriously enough.”

Adrián nodded.

“And you thought they needed discipline.”

“Exactly.”

“Standards.”

“Yes.”

“Accountability.”

“Certainly.”

Adrián grinned.

“And a reading plan.”

Matías laughed.

“Possibly.”

Marcos smiled.

“Preferably before breakfast.”

“Now you're both being ridiculous.”

Adrián lifted his hands.

“We learned from the best.”

Matías shook his head, smiling.

Then he became thoughtful again.

“The point is, when I heard about a church emphasizing obedience and holiness, I was impressed.”

“The church you visited,” Marcos said.

“Yes.”

Matías nodded.

“I thought those people understood something Corinth had missed.”

“And you admired them,” Elías said.

“Very much.”

He paused.

“More than I realized.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

Matías considered his answer.

“I suppose I saw in that church the kind of Christian life I'd been trying to live.”

The others listened.

“Serious. Disciplined. Committed. Careful about sin. Intentional about obedience.”

He smiled faintly.

“It was familiar.”

Adrián nodded.

“It sounded like you.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.
