“And when we began studying Galatia, I didn't want to hear that their attempt to become more complete through the flesh was a problem.”

Marcos nodded.

“You were defending something important to you.”

“Very important.”

Matías paused.

“Although I wasn't simply defending the church.”

He looked at Adrián.

“I was defending the way I'd been trying to live.”

Adrián's expression softened.

“I remember.”

Matías smiled.

“I think you understood that before I did.”

“Only because Corinth had already given me a little practice.”

They laughed quietly.

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Marcos reached for his coffee.

“Well, I suppose my turn is coming.”

Adrián grinned.

“Oh, absolutely.”

Marcos smiled.

“Go ahead.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Community Impact Week.”

Marcos laughed.

“I knew it.”

“Three thousand two hundred and eighty participants.”

“You remember the number?”

“Of course.”

“I'm impressed.”

“Eighty-six professionals.”

Marcos shook his head.

“Please don't.”

“Forty-two partner organizations.”

Matías was laughing now.

“And,” Adrián continued, raising one finger, “one very tired organizer.”

“That part is accurate,” Marcos admitted.

Elías smiled.

“You certainly accomplished a great deal.”

“We did.”

“And people were genuinely helped,” Matías added.

Marcos nodded.

“Yes. That's still something I'm grateful for.”

Adrián looked at him.

“And you loved every minute of organizing it.”

“Not every minute.”

“The air conditioning?”

“Especially not the air conditioning.”

They laughed.

Marcos leaned back.

“I did enjoy seeing everything come together.”

“We noticed,” Adrián said.

Marcos smiled.

“I know.”

He looked at the three men.

“You know, when I first heard about the church at Laodicea, I didn't immediately think about the rebuke.”

“What did you think about?” Elías asked.

“The resources.”

Adrián nodded.

“Of course.”

“The ability to accomplish things. The financial stability. The confidence.”

Marcos paused.

“I understand why a church would appreciate those things.”

“Certainly,” Elías said.

Marcos continued.

“When you have resources, you can help people. You can plan ahead. You can respond to needs. You can support worthwhile work.”

Matías nodded.

“All true.”

“And when you see those things happening, it's encouraging.”

“Of course,” Adrián said.

Marcos looked down at his coffee.

“That's why Christ's assessment of Laodicea bothered me so much.”

Elías waited.

“They said they were rich and needed nothing.”

“Yes.”

“And He said they were poor and blind.”

Marcos shook his head.

“I couldn't get away from that.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Because they were wrong about themselves?”

“Yes.”

Marcos paused.

“And because I recognized how easily I could have made the same judgment.”

He looked at his friends.

“If you'd asked me a few months ago whether our church was healthy, I could have given you a very impressive report.”

Adrián smiled.

“You did give us a very impressive report.”

“Several,” Matías added.

Marcos laughed.

“Yes. Several.”

Then he grew thoughtful.

“And every figure could have been correct.”

Elías nodded.

“That's important.”

“Yet those figures couldn't tell me whether we were depending on Christ.”

“No,” Elías said.

Marcos looked at the Bible.

“I think that's what I found so unsettling.”

He paused.

“I knew how to evaluate the success of an event. I wasn't nearly as certain how to evaluate the spiritual condition of a church.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“And Laodicea made you question the difference.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“I suppose I've always been impressed by churches that get things done.”

Adrián grinned.

“We had noticed.”

“Yes, thank you.”

They laughed.

Then Marcos looked toward Elías.

“And I think we know which church impressed you.”

Elías raised an eyebrow.

“Do we?”

Adrián sat up.

“Oh, we certainly do.”

Matías smiled.

“Ephesus.”

Elías looked at the three of them.

“Apparently my preferences haven't been particularly well concealed.”

“Not at all,” Adrián said.

Elías laughed.

“Well, I did admire Ephesus.”

“Did?” Adrián asked.

“I still admire what Christ commended.”

Matías nodded.

“As we should.”

Elías looked at his Bible.

“Think about their history. Paul taught there. Timothy was instructed to confront false doctrine there. The elders were warned about false teachers.”

Marcos smiled.

“And Christ commended their discernment.”

“Yes.”

Elías nodded.

“They had persevered. They had labored for His name. They had tested those claiming to be apostles and found them false.”

Adrián looked at him.

“You're beginning to sound excited.”

Elías smiled.

“I suppose I am.”

“Careful. That's my department.”

They laughed.

Elías continued.

“Those commendations are genuine. Christ Himself gives them.”

“Of course,” Matías said.

“And I don't want us to diminish them simply because we know what comes afterward.”

Marcos nodded.

“Neither do I.”

Elías rested his hand on the Bible.

“Yet what comes afterward is impossible to ignore.”

Adrián looked at him.

“They had fallen.”

“Yes.”

“And Christ told them to repent.”

“Yes.”

“And warned that He would remove their lampstand.”

Elías nodded.

“Unless they repented.”

He sat quietly for a moment.

“I've been thinking about that warning all week.”

Matías looked at him.

“What particularly troubles you?”

Elías considered the question.

“I suppose it's how easily I would have assumed that a church with those commendations was spiritually healthy.”

Marcos nodded.

“You would have trusted its doctrine.”

“Yes.”

“Its discernment.”

“Yes.”

“And its perseverance.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“All of it.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“You'd have joined that church in a heartbeat.”

Elías laughed.

“Quite possibly.”

“Then you'd have volunteered to teach.”

“Perhaps.”

“And within six months you'd have corrected everyone.”

Matías burst out laughing.

Elías shook his head.

“Adrián.”

“What?”

“You're enjoying this.”

“Immensely.”

Elías laughed too.

“Unfortunately, you may have a point.”

Adrián looked delighted.

“Gentlemen, did you hear that?”

Marcos smiled.

“We heard.”

“Someone write it down.”

Elías waited for the laughter to settle.

Then he looked down at Revelation.

“I've spent much of my Christian life valuing doctrinal accuracy.”

Matías nodded.

“And rightly so.”

“Yes. Truth matters.”

Elías paused.

“Yet I've also enjoyed being the person who knew the answer.”

Adrián smiled.

“We know.”

Elías looked up.

“Thank you.”

“You're welcome.”

They laughed again.

Elías continued.

“I've been thinking about how quickly I could recognize an error in someone else's teaching.”

Marcos nodded.

“You've always been good at that.”

“Perhaps.”

Elías looked at him.

“Although being able to identify an error doesn't tell me everything about the source of my own confidence.”

Matías leaned forward.

“That's interesting.”

Elías smiled.

“Isn't it?”

Adrián glanced between them.

“Now both of you are becoming dangerous.”

Matías laughed.

Elías continued.

“When I read Christ's message to Ephesus, I see a church whose religious record I would have admired.”

He paused.

“And Christ says it had fallen.”

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Then Adrián looked around the table.

“Well.”

He set down his coffee.

“I think we've discovered something rather embarrassing.”

Marcos smiled.

“What's that?”

Adrián pointed toward Matías.

“He liked the disciplined church.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.”

He turned toward Marcos.

“You liked the successful church.”

“Certainly.”

Then toward Elías.

“And you liked the doctrinally correct church.”

Elías smiled.

“Very much.”

Adrián leaned back.

“And I liked the exciting church.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's accurate.”

Adrián looked at the three of them.

“So we spent all this time trying to figure out what makes a church healthy…”

He paused.

“…and we each had a favorite before we finished reading.”

Matías laughed.

“Apparently.”

Elías smiled.

“That's an interesting observation.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Don't turn it into a theological term.”

“I wasn't going to.”

“You were thinking about it.”

Elías laughed.

“Perhaps.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“There's something else.”

Adrián turned toward him.

“What?”

“We didn't simply admire different churches.”

“No?”

“We were using different things to judge them.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“That's true.”

Marcos continued.

“Adrián noticed excitement and spiritual gifts.”

“Yes.”

“Matías noticed discipline and obedience.”

“Yes.”

“I noticed resources, organization, and results.”

“And Elías noticed doctrine,” Adrián added.

Elías nodded.

“Correct.”

Marcos looked around the table.

“Each of us was impressed by something different.”

Adrián smiled.

“And each of us thought his preferred church had something the others needed.”

Matías laughed softly.

“That's certainly how I felt about Corinth.”

Adrián looked at him.

“You were going to fix my church.”

“Someone had to.”

They laughed.

“And I thought Corinth had something your church needed,” Adrián replied.

Matías raised an eyebrow.

“What?”

“A little joy.”

Matías laughed.

“Of course.”

Marcos smiled.

“And I thought both of you needed better organization.”

Adrián turned toward him.

“There's a surprise.”

Elías laughed.

Marcos looked at Elías.

“And you probably thought all three of us needed better teaching.”

Elías smiled.

“I may have entertained that possibility.”

Adrián threw up his hands.

“Gentlemen, we've solved it!”

Matías laughed.

“Have we?”

“Of course. All four churches needed to become more like the one each of us preferred.”

Marcos burst out laughing.

Elías shook his head.

“That's rather revealing.”

Adrián smiled.

“Isn't it?”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“More coffee?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She began pouring.

“What are you gentlemen laughing about?”

“Our remarkable ability to recognize what's wrong with everyone else.”

Anna smiled.

“Well, you've had plenty of practice.”

Marcos laughed.

“Apparently.”

She looked at Adrián.

“Anything wrong with your coffee?”

“Nothing whatsoever.”

“That's a first.”

“I'm growing, Anna.”

“Keep it up.”

She moved to the next table.

The four men were still smiling when Matías looked down at his Bible.

“There's something I keep coming back to.”

Elías turned toward him.

“What is it?”

“Galatians three.”

Adrián smiled.

“Your favorite passage.”

Matías laughed.

“Not exactly my favorite when we first studied it.”

He opened the Bible.

“Paul asks whether, having begun by the Spirit, they were now being perfected by the flesh.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías looked around the table.

“The Galatians were looking to something that couldn't complete what the Spirit had begun.”

“Right,” Marcos said.

Matías continued.

“And we've been discussing how easily we look at things that impress us and assume they establish spiritual health.”

Adrián nodded.

“Gifts.”

“Discipline.”

“Results,” Marcos added.

“Knowledge,” Elías said.

For a moment, they sat quietly.

Then Adrián looked at the four of them.

“Those are all rather different things.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“And none of them is necessarily wrong.”

“Certainly not,” Elías replied.

Adrián leaned back.

“Then I suppose the question is what we're expecting them to tell us.”

Matías nodded.

“That's worth considering.”

Marcos looked at his Bible.

“Especially if we're using them to answer a question they can't answer.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Such as whether we're depending on Christ.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

Adrián looked toward the window.

A couple was walking past, laughing about something neither of them could hear.

He watched them for a moment.

Then he turned back.

“You know what I find interesting?”

Matías smiled.

“There's more?”

“Always.”

Adrián looked at the open Bibles.

“We've spent weeks examining what the Corinthians were doing, what the Galatians were trying to do, what the Laodiceans had accomplished, and what the Ephesians knew and defended.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián paused.

“And I keep thinking about what Jesus said about the vine.”

Matías looked up.

“John fifteen?”

“Yes.”

Elías opened his Bible.

“Verse five?”

Adrián smiled.

“That's the one.”

Elías found the passage and read.

“‘I am the vine. You are the branches. He who remains in me, and I in him, the same bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.’”

Marcos looked at the verse.

“Apart from Me.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías leaned forward.

“He doesn't say the branches need to become more impressive.”

Adrián smiled.

“No.”

“Or that they need to prove how much they can accomplish,” Marcos added.

Elías looked at the passage.

“The branch bears fruit because of its relationship to the vine.”

Matías nodded.

“Because its life comes from the vine.”

“Yes.”

Adrián sat quietly for a moment.

Then he smiled.

“Imagine a branch boasting about how many grapes it produced.”

Marcos laughed.

“Adrián.”

“No, seriously.”

He lifted one hand as though addressing an audience.

“Ladies and gentlemen, I'd like to thank everyone who made this harvest possible.”

Matías began laughing.

Adrián continued.

“First, my personal commitment to fruitfulness.”

Marcos covered his face.

“And of course, my outstanding record of perseverance.”

Elías was laughing now.

“Don't forget your doctrinal accuracy,” he said.

Adrián pointed at him.

“Absolutely. I have always maintained the correct position on grapes.”

They laughed so loudly that Anna looked over from the counter.

“Everything all right?”

“Perfectly!” Adrián called.

She shook her head, smiling.

When the laughter subsided, Marcos looked at the open Bible.

“That's actually quite a picture.”

Adrián smiled.

“A ridiculous one.”

“Yes.”

Marcos paused.

“Although it does make the point.”

Elías nodded.

“The branch has no independent life.”

Matías looked at the verse again.

“And yet it really does bear fruit.”

“Certainly,” Elías said.

Adrián leaned back.

“So the fruit is real.”

“Yes.”

“And the branch isn't the source.”

“No.”

He smiled.

“I think I like that.”

For a while, nobody spoke.

The diner continued around them.

Anna carried plates to a nearby table. Someone called for a refill. A child laughed from one of the booths.

Marcos looked at his friends.

“There's something encouraging about that passage.”

Matías nodded.

“What?”

“Christ doesn't tell the branches to manufacture life.”

Elías smiled.

“No.”

“He identifies Himself as the vine.”

“Yes.”

Marcos looked down.

“I've spent so much time thinking about what a church can accomplish.”

Adrián smiled.

“We know.”

Marcos laughed softly.

“Yes, you do.”

Then he looked at the passage again.

“It's good to be reminded that Christ is the source of spiritual life.”

Matías nodded.

“Very good.”

Elías sat quietly, his Bible open before him.

His phone vibrated.

He glanced at the screen.

Adrián noticed.

“Trouble?”

Elías smiled.

“No.”

“Another theological question?”

“Not this time.”

“Then what?”

“A message from my wife.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Ah.”

Elías read it and laughed softly.

Matías smiled.

“What did she say?”

“She wants to know whether I'd like to go for a walk this evening.”

Adrián nodded solemnly.

“A very serious invitation.”

Elías smiled.

“Apparently.”

“Does she have a destination?”

“No.”

Adrián looked at Marcos.

“Gentlemen, we may have a problem.”

Marcos laughed.

“What problem?”

“Our friend is about to participate in an activity with no measurable objective.”

Elías shook his head.

Matías smiled.

“And no reading assignment.”

“Or doctrinal examination,” Adrián added.

Elías laughed.

“I think I'll survive.”

He typed a reply and put the phone away.

Adrián watched him.

“What did you tell her?”

“That I'd love to.”

Adrián smiled.

“Good answer.”

Elías looked down at his Bible for a moment.

Then he closed it.

“I'm looking forward to it.”

The others smiled.

Anna approached with the bill.

“Together or separate?”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Together.”

Marcos raised an eyebrow.

“Again?”

“I'm feeling generous.”

Matías smiled.

“Are you sure?”

“Absolutely.”

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

He examined it.

Then looked up.

“Anna.”

“Yes?”

“Is this the correct amount?”

She folded her arms.

“Why?”

“I just want to make sure we're not being charged for all the theological discoveries.”

She laughed.

“Those are free.”

“Wonderful.”

“The coffee isn't.”

Adrián sighed.

“There's always a catch.”

They laughed as they gathered their things.

Outside, the afternoon was cool, with a light wind moving along the street.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars, reluctant to end the conversation.

Matías looked at the others.

“I enjoyed today.”

“So did I,” Marcos said.

Adrián smiled.

“Even though we didn't finish comparing the churches?”

Elías looked at him.

“Did you expect us to?”

“Not really.”

Matías laughed.

“I think we discovered something important.”

Marcos nodded.

“We each had our own reasons for admiring the churches we admired.”

“And those reasons weren't necessarily the same as Christ's assessment,” Elías added.

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“No.”

He glanced at the others.

“That's something to think about.”

Matías smiled.

“Next Thursday?”

“Of course,” Marcos said.

Elías nodded.

“I'll be here.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Provided you haven't become too busy walking.”

Elías laughed.

“I think I can manage both.”

They said their goodbyes.

As Adrián walked toward his car, he thought about the afternoon.

Four friends.

Four churches.

Four very different ideas of what made a church worth admiring.

He had always assumed that the things which excited him were the things that mattered most.

Matías had valued discipline.

Marcos had valued accomplishment.

Elías had valued knowledge and discernment.

And each of them had found something in Scripture that challenged the measure he trusted.

Adrián opened his car door, then paused.

He remembered the laughter about the branch boasting of its grapes.

He smiled.

It really was a ridiculous picture.

Then he thought about how naturally the four of them had defended the things they admired.

Perhaps the picture wasn't quite as ridiculous as he'd thought.

He climbed into the car.

Next Thursday, they would have more to discuss.

For now, he was glad they'd begun to see the difference between the things that impressed them and the life that came from Christ.

And he was particularly glad that none of them had suggested a fifth church.

Four were proving quite enough.
